The wonderful thing about Foley is it is performed to picture.” His approach to both cueing and editing Foley is the performance, although he admits he prefers less Foley in a film than some others might enjoy. “The most important thing is how much emotional content is in it … we tell stories for the heart and soul.” While working at Paramount, he fondly recalls his experiences with director Tony Scott, who he describes as “the most precise director I have ever worked with about what he wanted \[from sound\].”

![Figure 5.7 Supervising sound editor F. Hudson Miller photographed by Dan Weeks. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of F. Hudson Miller](Images/fig5_7.jpg)

*Figure 5.7* *Supervising sound editor F. Hudson Miller photographed by Dan Weeks.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of F. Hudson Miller*

![Figure 5.8 Marko Costanzo performing a rain cue. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of George Lara](Images/fig5_8.jpg)

*Figure 5.8* *Marko Costanzo performing a rain cue.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of George Lara*

Director Kevin Lima, who has a background directing animated films such as *A Goofy Movie* (1995), *Tarzan* (1999), and *Enchanted* (2007), comments, “you’re building a perception of a world … and aural landscape of our own choosing.” When considering the sound design for his movies, he is focused on “how to orchestrate those sounds depending on emotional context.” Like Miller, Lima believes the emotion should motivate sound in film. “Too much sound becomes distracting … sound and the character work hand in hand.” An example he gives is “how would this character grab their prop?”

It is unusual for a director to play a role in the Foley performance in Los Angeles, but not as unusual in New York. The Coen Brothers will collaborate with Foley artist Marko Costanzo frequently. Thinking back to the Foley for the movie *Ishtar* (1987), Costanzo recalls that director Elaine May was very “hands on” regarding Foley. “She knew what she wanted and would persist until she was satisfied.” In these situations, even if there is a bona fide spotting session, the actual cuing for the Foley is not executed by the Foley editor. Rather, the director sees the sonic performance as part of the actor’s character and includes the task in their role.

## [**SPOTTING REDUX**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_3)

Sometimes, a spotting session is for the intention of “repurposing” an already released film. When a film is going to be shown on network or cable television and has to be conformed to the commercial breaks or other time considerations, it sometimes has to be shortened. At other times, however, sound editors have to add footage that was not in the original release to expand the length of the film for television. This requires editing in sound effects, performing and editing Foley and ADR, and editing additional music cues.

The spotting sessions of repurposed films are short and sweet. The network provides simple notes, and we all conform to the new requirements. The trick is getting the new sound to match the old sound so it can then be remixed at the dub stage.

Sound editors get the stems from the original dub of the film and go back into the material to see what changes or additions were requested. Once they find sound effects that fit and match, Foley artists perform the necessary cues, and the editors edit them in. The same would be done for ADR. Another editor would be finding music cues that could be stretched or added to the new version.

There might also be a separate spotting session for the airline or television version of a film. This usually does not affect Foley much. The purpose is to replace objectionable language with new ADR lines that cover the old lines. If you have heard “Oh, that is freaking great” or “Just flake off,” you can be sure you were watching either the airline version or the repurposed television version. Sometimes you will even notice that words are missing. Now you know why.

## [**CUEING FOR SHOEING**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_4)

Once a Foley editor has the spotting notes, the next step is to translate these concepts and limitations into cue sheets to be used on the stage. Until recently, the more experienced and meticulous editors would present handwritten cue sheets to the Foley crew on the stage. The cue sheets were notated in vertical columns with the principal footstep cues beginning in tracks one, two, three, and so on. The middle tracks would be background footstep cues, and the outside tracks would be various props. The final track was a cloth pass.

Now, most cueing is done on a Pro Tools session. The tracks are horizontal, but the approach is the same. The first main character’s footsteps are on track one. The track assignments remain as they did previously. The main difference is that, instead of writing the cues in, the cues are marked into a session the session is saved on a hard drive or a flash drive, and sometimes it is sent to a server. The stage loads in the sessions from the server or the drive. The cues are then recorded right in the regions marked and tagged in Pro Tools. Cue sheets can be printed from the Pro Tools sessions.

It is not necessary to cue Foley to the first specific moment of movement. Foley artists will be looking at the action in the context of the scene. Whereas ADR is cued to the first “mod” of sound to better help the actor be in perfect sync, Foley can be cued more loosely. As long as one cues with the thought of helping the artist anticipate the action, the cueing will be appropriate. There is a danger to cueing too closely to the first footstep. Mixers and artists like a “breath” before the actual cue begins. Sometimes “streamers” are programmed into the recording equipment, which help the artist hit sync, but many Foley artists are used to “eyeballing” it. The important thing is to get close to the moment, not on top of it.

Another important trick to cueing is that it is better for the Foley artists if the cues have a flow to them. If the picture is edited to show a back-and-forth perspective (we see Jane walk down the hall, then Max in the kitchen, then Jane, then Max), it is easier for the Foley artists if they keep Jane’s cue going, rather than stopping it and cueing for the next cut. The walk will feel more natural and the artist will be more relaxed. Remember, Foley is a performance. Cueing for the artist and the mixer, not for the editor, is best practice.

Before the use of digital editing stations, when cues were handwritten, cueing was a slow, painstaking, monotonous, and, at times, boring process. However, it was also the most critical use of time in a Foley editor’s workflow. It would take about 4 hours for an experienced editor to cue 10 minutes of film. An animated film would take longer, as would an action sequence. Digital cueing is faster because the editor selects the recording track and types in the cue. The care that used to be taken to select the exact track is not as critical now because the Foley mixer can move tracks with ease on the stage and accommodate additional tracks to build a Foley effect. Also, schedules are shorter for Foley editors, and the result is that less time is used for cueing.

When editors cue Foley, they should watch every scene several times. All editors vary in their approach, but there are some conventions they keep in mind. First, they cue the main characters in the scene, character by character, keeping each character on a consistent track if possible. Mimi is on track one, Joe is on track two, Maurice is on track three, and so on. If Mimi has a complicated walk and needs to go from one surface to another during that walk, it might be best if the editor cues the concrete footsteps on track one and jumps to a second track for the grass footsteps. Some Foley artists can make that transition on the stage easily in one track. However, if the artist is less experienced or the mixer does not have a lot of skill punching in on a cue, then the editor can cue the walk on two tracks.

The next step in cueing is to look at background characters. They might be groups of people or perhaps individuals. If some background person walks from right to left or left to right, the editor would most likely cue that person on a separate track and indicate the direction of the walk. If there are characters on the left, center, and right of the screen, the Foley artist will need three tracks just for those incidental background people (BGs). With principal players, it is best to identify names. However, with BGs, use of clothing color, height, or an identifying characteristic can help the Foley artist see quickly who has been cued. Examples would be “tall man, blue shirt, right to left” or “two girls running center.”

To be as specific as possible without using too many words is another best practice. This is particularly true if the mixer and Foley artists are not going to be using cue sheets but are going to be reading the cues off Pro Tools or another digital system. One illustration of how easy it is to give too little information comes from the cueing of the film *Platoon* (1986). The Foley editor, who was experienced and knowledgeable, cued “man carrying child.” Although this sounds just fine, the scene included several men, each carrying a child.

Once the footsteps are cued, the next step is to start the reel or scene from the top and begin identifying props. It is preferable to keep similar props on similar tracks. Paper cues should be on different tracks from metal cues or leather chairs, for example. When Foley artists are on the Foley stage, the mixer adjusts the microphone to record certain props. Many Foley artists perform all the paper cues in one sitting, so it is easier if they do not have to hunt for each similar kind of cue. It is helpful to think in terms of “food groups,” like paper, hand pats, body falls, metal crashes, cop gear, horse footsteps, saddle and bridle, debris, and so on. The key is to have a clear road map for the artists and mixer, so everyone is “on the same page,” literally and figuratively.

In the day of magnetic film and then 24-track recording, Foley artists would use tracks judiciously. Now, with Pro Tools, there are fewer limitations on track use. The good news is that it is possible to split out cues and layer more. The bad news is that this encourages the less disciplined editor to throw cues in anywhere without much thought. Once again, however, it is essential to consider the workflow. The people who come after the cueing need to have a secure sense of direction and communication. The more precise and methodical the cueing, the more relaxed and specific the Foley session can be. Foley artists are always under intense time pressure. If Pro Tools crashes or the microphone starts humming, time will be lost while the problem is addressed. Organized cueing allows room for mistakes, retakes, and outtakes.

When it comes to cueing Foley, the editor is serving the needs of the Foley artists and the mixer. Foley stages are expensive and usually booked in advance. The number of Foley sessions booked for a project is usually decided before the first spotting session. The editor should cue only what needs to be performed on the stage. If the effects editor is going to use library effects for the cue, then it does not need to be cued for the stage. It is best to prioritize. What must be done on the stage is what should be cued. It is always acceptable to cue some additional cues that are not necessary. If “wish list” cues are going to be added, then it is wise to notate them as such.

In the words of Foley mixer David Jobe, when it comes to the biggest obstacle on the Foley stage, “It’s time, time, time!”

## [**CUEING ON THE FLY**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_5)

When cueing for television or episodic shows, the conventions differ for prime time than for cartoons, reality television, or daytime television. There is virtually no time for cueing for cartoons. So, they are cued on the Foley stage “on the fly,” meaning in real time. As the Foley artists are watching what is happening, they are most likely making some notes about what needs to be done, as is the mixer. These shows are done quickly and with formulaic props. Rather than taking time to design a particular sound for each sonic event, the opposite is true. The Foley artist is performing “guerrilla Foley” and getting as much done as quickly as possible. Sync is not as critical nor is quality.

Foley artist Marko Costanzo and mixer George Lara record the Foley for *The Marvelous Mrs. Maisel*, and the show is cued by Steven Visscher, who is the supervising Foley editor. “He saved us a lot of time by being sensible in what they spotted,” Costanzo relates. Cuing is not always that way, as Costanzo says, “it always depends on who is in charge.”

The spotting sessions so carefully described earlier are the scenario for the big budget features, but they have less in common with the prime-time television shows viewers watch regularly, whether on a flat screen, a computer, or a phone. Even if there is cueing for the Foley, it might be little more than a laundry list of “to dos” for the Foley artists. There is typically no coordinated spotting session. It is a formulaic process where the cast resides in the same venues weekly so the sound changes very little.

Games require short spurts of cues that vary only slightly. It takes less time to cue the Foley for a game, and typically there is less detail to cue. The focus on Foley for games comes more during the performance of Foley. The minute changes with each possible choice a player might make can involve dozens of takes of footstep and prop cues that look almost identical. Thus, the cueing is simpler, but the shooting and editing are very specific.

Other countries do not necessarily follow the same conventions for cueing that has been outlined here. For example, Julien Naudin does not like to follow cued Foley sheets. He does everything intuitively and decides the cueing at the moment of performance. Naudin is a third-generation Foley artist and has a well-developed method for how he likes to work. He has a close relationship with the director Lars von Trier, and the two communicate on an instinctual level about how the Foley should work within a film. Naudin travels to Denmark to work with von Trier, where the director has a rare role with the Foley in that he is in the vicinity and available for any questions or clarifications.

There is a valuable lesson here for anyone who performs Foley for any project anywhere. Budget, time, experience, quality, personnel, and exhibition all have some determination in how much preparation will be accomplished before the Foley artist has to perform the sounds. The ability to be flexible, intuitive, and open-minded is a great asset.

## [**HEARING THE SWEET SPOT: EDITING FOLEY**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_6)

Sound editors are introduced to the profession with the most modern type of technology available. Before the digital age, editors would have cut on optical sound or magnetic film. The optical soundtrack had a visual line, similar to the waveform of today, with which to decipher where sound would begin and end. The editor had a visual cue as to what to edit. Magnetic film had no visual reference. The editor had to rely solely on the sound itself. When deciding where to edit in a sound or move the sync of a Foley cue, the editor would mark the place to cut on the magnetic film itself with a white china marker, then cut it with a splicer, hang the piece of sound on the rack of a “trim bin,” and perhaps replace that piece of magnetic film at a spot later in the track. She or he might have to “flop” the track, so the sound would not play against the sound head, if the purpose of replacing the tape was for sync only. Otherwise, the leftover sound would be interjected into the new cue.

In the days of editing a sound effect on magnetic film, the library effect that would be added was placed on the track being edited, and it was edited in to fit the action of the scene. The editor had to be so very experienced and skilled that they could imagine how this effect

![](Images/fig0010.jpg)

 would play along with the subsequent effects that would be edited on future tracks and layered to create the sonic magic. The audience might think the helicopter was one sound, but in reality, it was created by many tracks of complementary sounds, one by one, on the mammoth monster all editors have a love-hate relationship with: the Moviola. The editor did not have the luxury of playing all of the tracks together while editing. Thus, a real working knowledge of how the sounds would all play together, along with the imagined Foley and background tracks, was essential.

Now we have digital workstations. We can see the waveforms of every bit of sound, we can magnify the wave to “fine-cut” a sound effect, or we can pull back and see the entire session of tracks and edits. There are many advantages to the new technology. However, it is helpful to understand how the more fundamental technology of magnetic film taught the editor to be selective, nuanced, and patient in the quest for the right combination of sounds or the appropriate way to approach sync in editing Foley.

### [**The Initial Approach**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_6_1)

Editing Foley is a different skill than editing library sound effects. The task for the Foley editor is to take the already performed tracks and finesse them. Rather than creating sound effects for the overall design of a scene, the editor is fine-cutting the Foley so that it flows elegantly from cue to cue. This might seem, at first blush, a simple task that any neo phyte editor with an elementary understanding of Pro Tools can adequately accomplish, but that is not the case.

Just as the picture editor must be judicious in not overcutting a scene and risk sanitizing a performance, the Foley editor must not overcut the Foley and risk sanitizing the character of the walk. Foley walking is an acting performance. It is the sonic extension of the actor’s intent. Thus, as much of the feel of that performance must be preserved as possible, just as the actor’s speeches and facial expressions must be preserved. The editing must be sparse and specific, and it should honor the actor’s and the Foley artist’s original intentions.

![](Images/fig0011.jpg)

The specific techniques for editing Foley are based on the idea that the fewer edits one makes, the better. With that in mind, all experienced editors follow some conventions. First, look at the entire cue. Do not start editing right away, no matter how tempting it may be. The inclination is to start “fixing” the sync as soon as something seems out. But once an editor starts cutting, rest of the cue is affected. Because this is a performance, editors should watch the entire performance at least twice. What they will notice is where the sweet spot of the cue is. A wise editor will look at the more challenging part of the cue to finesse the sync and will also focus on where the really important visual emphasis is located. It is not important to line up every impact of sound. What is critical is to view the performed cue in context and to notice what other sounds will be in the scene. A footstep cue is more important if it is in isolation than if there is dialogue on top of it. The level at which the cue will be played in the final mix is not the level the editor works with on a workstation, nor the dynamic range most likely projected through the workstation speakers.

Having watched the cue once, in context with the scene, an editor takes notice of where the sync goes out. Is it at the beginning? Does the entire cue seem a little late all the way through? This is the most common issue and the easiest one to fix. It is possible that all that is needed is to pull the entire cue up two frames. The cue might fall beautifully into place at that point.

Some Foley artists are irregular in their sync. Some footsteps might fall right into good sync, then one or two fall very late, then one is early, and then they fall back in good sync again. With a calm and patient eye, the editor can notice the footsteps that are out of sync. Can they be moved with one edit? The main goal is to avoid cutting every step so the organic life of the cue is preserved. While the goal is always to get the best sync on stage, the performance dictates the direction of the editing.

### [**Life Lessons in Editing**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_6_2)

Many years ago, I was the Foley artist on a baseball film. I received a phone call from one of the editors cutting my Foley. This was back in the day when editors would each cut the sound effects, dialogue, and the Foley on one reel, so several editors would be cutting my Foley. I had not worked with this particular editor before, so he was not familiar with how I worked, nor was I familiar with him. But I knew he had more often edited dialogue and sound effects. He had issues with my sync. He told me he was bringing my performed version of the Foley and his edited version down to compare. Mind you, while this was back in the 1980s, when there was more time for finessing, this was still a pretty odd request.

He came onto the Foley stage, which was costing the studio a pretty penny, and insisted I come to a cutting room and look at the Foley. I complied. We viewed my unedited version, as I had walked it, and his version as he had cut it. It bears noting that he had told the assistant to order another duplicate (dupe) of the unedited Foley, again at great expense, so he could make his point.

He played the Foley as I had walked it. It was a footstep cue. The main character had a simple walk throughout the reel. It was not challenging or complicated. It looked pretty good to me. Then he showed me his edited version. The reel, which was magnetic film, had lots of splices in it. Experienced Foley editors had already taught me how to tell how well Foley was walked and edited by seeing a few, but not too many, splices on the side of the film reel. So, I knew something was very wrong. I wondered if the Foley had been improperly duplicated. I wondered if the equipment on the stage had slipped the sync. I knew the mixer and I had been meticulous. I was stymied. Then I saw what had happened. He had cut virtually every step. He had no production track to follow, so he was matching every step to what he perceived was the footfall.

The steps he had cut made the character look like his knees did not bend when he walked. I tried to defuse the situation. I learned something very important that day. Dialogue editors often edit very differently than Foley editors. Luckily, the supervising sound editor reassured me. He was comfortable with my quality of work and was satisfied with my professionalism. Still, I did not really know what was different about dialogue editing and Foley editing.

About a year later, a beginning Foley editor, who was smart but inexperienced, asked me to look at some Foley he was cutting. It was of a dancer doing some ballet steps on a hardwood floor in toe shoes. His edited version was so confusing that it took hours for us to figure out what went wrong. He had started cutting at the first sign of a sync issue. He did not know dancing, and he did not know what to do as things got more and more out of sync because he started editing from the top of the scene. Once again, this was on magnetic film. So, once he started cutting, the subsequent sync was affected. Nowadays, with nondestructive editing, Foley editor can undo what they did and start over. But then, they could not.

I cannot remember how many hours I sat there, trying to decipher where the dance sounds should fit with the picture. It did help that the Foley artist who performed the cue was a former dancer. I could tell what she was trying to do. However, I believe I was of little help to the poor editor.

These two stories provide valuable lessons about cutting Foley. The best practice is to look at the entire cue prior to editing anything and then plan what to do with the cue. Before editing one frame of sound, a wise editor makes sure where the sync is good and where it is loose. Editing only begins when it is clear how the Foley artist’s performance was intended.

Later, when I began editing, I realized that once I started performing surgery on Foley, I was lost. It is better to relax and take the necessary time. To assume that most of the work will be in good sync, and only cut what really draws attention to itself, can pay dividends in time and art. The overview or gross editing comes first. There is plenty of time to go back and fine-cut. Most Foley artists, even the beginners, do more good sync than bad. An editor can do more good by editing less. This philosophy will work most of the time. Unless an editor is “topping production”—editing the Foley to be in perfect sync with the production track—perfection is not necessary, nor truly possible. Excellence, not perfection, is the goal.

Scott Hecker started cutting Foley when he worked with veteran editor and Foley artist Bob Rutledge. Because he had been trained so well, he did not make the typical rookie errors:

> I would look at a cue before cutting and “hone in.” You have to figure out where you are going to make your edits and where you are going to get back in sync before you start cutting at all.

Hecker defends the practice of having new editors cut Foley: “It is a good place to learn to edit because it’s synchronizing sound to picture and nine times out of ten \[it is\] a literal application, whether it’s someone setting down a teacup or pounding on a door.” Scott also cautions, “But if you reduce the art of Foley editing to purely that, then you’re missing 80 percent of it.” Hecker’s advice is even more critical with digital sound. Becoming enthralled with the sound waves and wanting to match them will cause an artist to lose sight of what Foley is all about. Foley exists to breathe life into the character. Overediting can suck the life out of the character.

Mark Pappas has worked with Hecker frequently. When he began editing, he made plenty of mistakes, and his reels would come back from the stage needing fixing. Pappas had asked for advice from other editors, who gave various tips. Some said to match the cadence of each individual step. Some said to focus on being more rhythmic. He was not sure which way to go. However, upon further reflection, these editors were saying the same thing, but with different expressions. They were emphasizing the “feel” of Foley rather than the sterility of sync.

In the earlier days of editing, when severe budget cutbacks were not the driving force that they are now, there would be time for Pappas to have someone check his work to guide him. After cutting a unit of magnetic film, he could run the unit with his supervisor to make sure it was ready to go. Nowadays, the chance to check work is rare, so the wise Foley editor is painstaking and meticulous and asks as many questions in the beginning as possible. As Pappas says,

> It was surprising how tedious it was. You cut that sequence and you almost get done with it and you play it back and it takes about ten seconds and you’ve been sitting there for hours working on it!

It is easy to get caught up in the weeds when first editing Foley. As with any new skill set, there is a tendency to look at every single step, every piece of a prop cue, every pop, every sizzle, and every breath and forget that the sounds go by so fast the audience will not notice. Also, it is invaluable to remember this: Foley is only one aspect of the sound design in the scene, and the sound is in concert with the dialogue, the music, the acting, the cinematography, and the set design. All things considered, unless it is a dialogue film with nothing but Foley and production sound in the scene, the collaboration of all sound elements is a comfort and reassuring.

### [**Editing for Sync**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_6_3)

It is preferable to preview the scene before editing any Foley. Any film for a festival, an independent screening, or a film school project should allow the time to really examine what to edit. However, most experienced editors do not always watch every sequence before editing it. Mark Pappas, along with other Foley editors, has developed a methodology that, over the years, has worked for him. His years of experience have enabled him to start cutting from the top of a cue. He knows most of the Foley artists and how they approach their cues, so he can anticipate where the sync issues will probably come up. Additionally, schedules do not always allow editors time to preview as they did in the past. Nowadays, the best editors hone their technique to find the fastest and best ways to streamline the process as best they can.

Because most Foley artists do not always have perfect sync, the editor will only be concerned if the sync is noticeably early or late or unless it needs “top production” or to be combined with another track. The conventional wisdom is that a frame late is virtually imperceptible.

There is an alternative theory about sync. The theory is that sound travels more slowly than light, so effects and Foley should be edited slightly early to compensate. If we watch a symphonic or choral performance at a distance, we will notice that the conductor’s arms are ahead of what we hear. She or he is conducting into the next measure by the time the sound reaches the part of the audience that is far away. This is what supports the theory of editing sound one or two frames early.

The flaw in this reasoning is that, in modern film viewing, the listener is not far away from the point of reference in the soundtrack. The mixed track is coming through a complement of speakers intended to engage the ear from different places. The sound is close to the audience. It is not traveling more slowly than the picture. It makes more sense to cut the sound to fit with what can be seen and not try to compensate for the different rates at which sound and light travel.

An important aspect of cutting Foley is to play it with the production track to see quite quickly what does not work. By editing Foley in a sterile environment with no concept of the big picture, an editor to get caught up in minutiae. It is so important to understand that Foley is a craft of subtlety. It will not be what the audience watches. It needs to not draw attention to itself. Badly performed and badly edited Foley always draws attention. Good Foley lays in beautifully, as though it happened in production. It is essential to listen to the Foley alone while editing it. But before leaving the cue and going to the next one, it is essential that the editor listen to the edited version with the production track. That will reveal what needs to be refined in editing.

New tools continue to be developed to “help” editors, but in reality these can prove to be dangerous. One example is the edit tool called Beat Detective. It was originally designed for music to ensure that drum parts were put in sync, but now it can be used for Foley. An editor can push a button and Beat Detective will find the silent spot and put cuts there to perfect the sync in the Foley. However, this can result in sterile-sounding Foley. Tools and plug-ins are designed to make things easy or fun, but they may actually marginalize the real art of editing Foley. A wise editor learns the classic methods of editing and adapts their own approach for improving skill and aptitude.

## [**MODERN TIMES**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec5_7)

The Foley editor will need to make adjustments in the editing process whenever there is new technology to learn. With magnetic film, editors would edit one unit of recorded Foley at a time and keep in mind the other complementary tracks. It was not possible to hear them all together during the editing process. The editor would have to wait until the predub. Today, it is possible to hear many tracks of Foley at once and have a clear perspective on how they will all sound together. With every new version of Pro Tools, or any other digital system, there are new plug-ins to add and new shortcuts to learn. This necessarily means that Foley editing does not remain stagnant.

Before digital workstations, editors would simply edit the sound. Now it is not uncommon for the supervisor to require the editor to present a premixed version of the Foley. While this is a time-saving practice for the dub, it imposes the perspective of the editor onto the mixer. Most mixers would prefer to have the stage time to finesse the volume of the Foley while having the sound effects, music, and dialogue close at hand to contribute to the overall balance of the dub. It is advisable to start conservatively whether mixing within Pro Tools or another system. More can always be done, but patience helps the editor assess each change made before delving into another one.

Plug-ins are seductive. They are developed for one reason: for consumers to purchase them. They are not often necessary but can save time if used. Caution is advised. To be a skilled editor, learning the classic lessons of editing for storytelling with sound will result in confidence and competence. There is no substitute for experience.

It is also possible for a neophyte editor to have a false sense of competence with the digital workstation. The tool one uses is not what determines the quality of the product. The tool is meant to make the craft easier, not to take the place of artistry and experience. Digital editing encourages speed, so the editor might not develop the skill to shape the cue to perfection. A Foley editor can modify, equalize, expand, or compress time and change the pitch of a sound. It all sounds like great fun. But before one gets carried away with the bells and whistles, learning to edit simply and purely is, again, the recommended practice. Mark Pappas relates his experience of moving from magnetic film to digital editing:

> I feel we were more of craftsmen before. The last thing you would want to do is get a reprint, because that meant taking it out and filling the hole with leader so you could keep the same length, then sending it \[the cue\] out. Then once you got it back, you’d put the sound back up. It was a lot more laborious to do something like that. Instead, we would go through and try to make everything work any way we could.

Digital editing, however, has made conforming previous sound edits to new picture changes much easier. Instead of putting up one unit at a time and changing each edit, the editor can update the entire session with a few edit changes in Pro Tools. All of the tracks can be viewed and conformed quickly. “It might take a day to update cue sheets with new footages \[with mag\]. Now, as soon as you’re done cutting and changing, cue sheets are done too because if you want them, they print out,” Pappas explains. “You need to listen to it.”

Beginning Foley editors tend to want to cut according to the waveforms on the computer screen. Let’s go “back to heads,” as we say, and think this through. Foley is sound. Editing sound requires listening to it in context with the picture. If an editor watches the wave and lets that dictate the editing process, the editing will have a less natural and believable feel. What works best is to watch the picture and listen to the sound as it accompanies the picture. If it works, leave it alone. If it does not, then looking at the waveforms allows for precision editing.

Editing prop cues has more in common with library sound effects than footstep cues. Many of the edits will be to tighten sync or clean out unwanted aspects of the prop cue. However, once again, it is a performance with a personality that is particular to the scene. To focus on the perfection of the edit rather than the impressionistic flow of the prop risks overcutting and taking the life out of the performance.

In today’s world of the internet and downloadable sound effects, it is common to order an array of sound effects from a company. This is actually a good way to get some basic effects for any sound effects library. The obvious question becomes, why not just buy the stock Foley effects offered as a set as well? The answer would be that ordering any stock effects is a good idea with which to begin. However, a sound library of effects will still have the performance issues specific to Foley. Once performed Foley effects are saved into a sound library, they become sound effects. They are valuable for any pedestrian cues that a project will need. However, they will not be a good substitute for the specific peculiarities of a performance. If an editor always keeps in mind the importance of the organic nature of the Foley performance, they will make wise choices. Foley and edited sound effects each have their intended uses and most ideal applications. Editors are clear about when to use one or the other or when to very deliberately use both as a hybrid.

# [**chapter | six**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch06)

## [**Scene Change**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch06)

### [The Foley Stage](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch06)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-9](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-9)

![](Images/fig0012.jpg)

There are few encounters that are more intriguing than a visit to a Foley stage. The peculiar experience of being in a dark room, with designated areas on which to walk, strange items skewed around, a pile of mismatched and unattractive footwear on the floor, and the strangely dressed people manipulating various pieces of metal, wood, and the like is not comparable to any other. Few people would ever forget the unique impressions of that encounter.

What few people would spend much time contemplating is how that room was conceived, designed, and executed. If a person did devote time to studying and investigating the room itself and the accompanying mixing booth and prop room, the specifics of the construction and the demands of the technology would add yet another layer of appreciation to the secret world of “Foleydom.” For those who are curious and wish to construct or adapt a room to be a Foley stage, the principles and requirements are fairly simple to utilize once they understand the necessary components. Then they can embellish at their discretion.

This chapter will begin by giving the detailed requirements for the ideal electronic and acoustical situation for a Foley stage. After these ideal sets of guidelines will follow the many variations and alternatives that other Foley artists have utilized for different reasons and with differing results. The purpose is to make clear that, while there is a codified ideal of how Hollywood perceives a standard Foley stage, it is by no means the only standard nor is it the only successful approach. Additionally, there are various conflicting opinions of what Foley stages should sound like and how they should ideally operate. While every opinion expressed is from the perspective of a professional with gravitas and credibility, these professionals do not all agree. Art is not objective, nor is its pursuit.

## [**THE ACOUSTICAL BLUEPRINT**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_1)

An intelligently designed and appropriately constructed Foley stage begins with the electrical requirements. Although most rooms have conventional standards for electrical wiring, a Foley stage has specific needs. When all is said and done, no matter how beautiful the room looks or how impressive the recording equipment is, if the room is not wired with the specific goal of recording sound effects with a wide variety of dynamic ranges and little, if any, interference from outside noise and electrical hum, then it is possible to spend countless hours tracking down the noises and trying to get rid of them. The first rule of the Foley stage is that it must work electronically.

Jim Fulmis, owner of Fultek Professional Audio and a veteran electrical engineer, has designed and wired many recording and rerecording stages and knows firsthand the nightmare of trying to fix a stage that is broken. When asked when he prefers to be involved with the construction of a Foley stage, he says, “It is always best to be involved in the early stages of design rather than one \[a Foley stage\] that has already been built.” This is the sentiment of most engineers. The earlier the electrical experts can give assurance that the concept for a room is workable, the better. Once the construction of the stage has begun, it is already too late for optimal electrical applications.

To begin with, the engineer must consider the shape of the room. The best shape for the Foley stage is a rectangle rather than a square. The walls, ceiling, and floor should not be perfect parallels. There are formulas in construction that allow for walls that are slightly off in measurements so the sound waves can be broken up. The goal is to reduce the chance of sound wave reflection (bounce), so that the room is less reverberant. Most acousticians agree with this perspective. Some, however, prefer rooms with more bounce and life. On a Foley stage, it is more typical to want less reverb.

In a room built to capture and record sound, the electrical needs are peculiar as well. When building an office building or a home, one does not have to be concerned with any buzz or hum that is running through a microphone cable. However, on a Foley stage, a hum or buzz can shut down operations for hours or even days. Thus, it is essential for the wiring used for microphones to be placed away from, and not parallel to, the wiring used for electricity. If the microphone wiring and the electrical wiring are too close together or, as is sometimes the case, bundled together, then when the mixer tries to record there will be a hum. Microphone wiring will draw current from the electrical wiring, and it can be impossible to get rid of the resultant hum.

I have spent many days on Foley stages when we could not find the source of the hum. Sometimes we had to stop work until an engineer could find and eliminate the hum. Sometimes this fix would last for a while; other times the hum came back. There are stages that Foley artists and mixers dread working on because there is hum that never goes away. Had an expert who understood the use of the room laid the wiring, this problem could have been avoided.

Another possible pitfall to be avoided via sound engineering is perpetual buzz in the microphone line. When wires are properly grounded, there will never be a buzz. It is best to have two types of grounds. One is the audio ground and one is the electrical. Some engineers will have one ground that does both. The bigger the ground wire size, the better. It is also essential to have an adequate pipe size for the wires to go through.

It is ideal to have proper grounding and wiring for a room that relies totally on the efficient and reliable use of electricity and microphones. However, every Foley artist and mixer have horror stories about hum and buzz. Most of the issues that come up on a stage could be avoided if the time and money were used at the front end to set up the stage properly. If the room is being adapted after being used for other purposes, an expert should examine the wiring before building anything. Simple mistakes can cause huge problems, as Fulmis illustrates:

> I can cite an instance where they brought in a welding cable and ran a copper ground in and added another ground. I was called in after the stage was down for two days and I said, “We’re going to disconnect all the grounds.” Then we found out there was an air conditioning conduit touching a cabinet that the equipment was in and that was creating a ground loop. So, we put blocks of wood on it to keep that from ever happening again and put one ground in. The stage was quieter than it had ever been.

The goal is to have the room work properly from the time the first switch is flicked to record. It is never true that the bugs necessarily have to be worked out of any stage. Fulmis reiterates,

> I’ve seen so many places where they just keep adding grounds to this or grounds to that, and that will work today. But then tomorrow you plug something else in and now you’ve got a new ground loop and a new buzz or hum.

If the room is wired well, it will not be necessary to spend a fortune on equipment. As Fulmis says, “You can use pretty cheap gear, provided it’s adequate to the job, and get away with it. You can get really good sound out of inexpensive gear if it’s all put together correctly. That’s the most important thing.”

## [**THE INTERIOR DESIGN**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_2)

An ideal Foley stage combines function and form. Not only does it need to have the essential elements of a workspace that makes sense to the artists, but it should also have an atmosphere that allows artists to be comfortable and efficient. It is easier to be creative and productive if the stage is designed in a way that maximizes the artist’s time and energy.

Most experts agree that Foley stages need to have a minimum of reflection. While it is possible to use baffles to soften sounds that are too sharp or reflective, it is easier to work in a room that is not as live as a music studio. If the stage is utilized for other purposes, then some compromises with the acoustics will be necessary.

If one is using a room that has already been built, chances are it has four walls specifically designed to be parallel. It is possible to build walls inside walls to shape the room in a preferred way. A Foley stage does not have to be locked into the original design.

In addition to nonparallel walls, ceiling, and floor, the surfaces should have traps built in or added on that enhance the flow of sound waves. Instead of hard walls (too much reflection) or soft walls (too absorptive), a stage should include a mixture of both surface types. Traps with different textures and shapes to help absorb sound can be added without making the room so dead that it has no personality at all. A good rule to follow for the placement of traps is to mix it up. Pair soft with hard: soft surface with hard surface, and so on. Two traps of similar material put together do not usually result in the best acoustics. Play with the shapes as well. Each room has its own identity. It is a good idea to learn the acoustic style of the room and be willing to adjust and modify traps as necessary. Two rooms designed, constructed, and engineered the same way will sound different.

It is a great idea to build a floating floor to have the best results with the Foley pits. A floating floor has air between it and the foundation. Foam or some other acoustic material can be inserted into the space to isolate sound that can travel through the floor from another source.

![Figure 6.2 Angled walls and ceiling with alternating hard and soft traps for optimum movement of sound waves. ¶ Source: Photo by the author](Images/fig6_2.jpg)

*Figure 6.2* *Angled walls and ceiling with alternating hard and soft traps for optimum movement of sound waves.* ¶ *Source: Photo by the author*

If building a floating floor is impractical, rugs positioned inside the Foley pits to deaden any sounds can be effective. Baffles and rugs should be placed under the microphone stands as well. The Foley mixer will be the best guide for determining what works.

Jody Thomas was a story editor who is now a Foley artist. She cut her Foley teeth at Warner Brothers on many television shows. She now specializes in animated television shows, which she Foleys on her own Foley stage next to her house. She has confronted all of the issues of building a stage from inception to practical use. She began with a large, prebuilt shed as the shell for her building and used all the wisdom of the best experts to build herself a stage so she could work at home and be available to her daughter. Because Thomas does both animated television shows and feature films, she needed a stage that was large enough to operate in but small enough to be economical. Her approach is useful to any film school or independent filmmaker who wants to have a workable arrangement that sounds good.

First, Thomas went through the process of acquiring the proper building permits. She then selected her team to construct and wire the stage. She carefully crafted where her Foley pits would be, where to build the water pit, where she would store her props, and what level of technology she would use.

Thomas has constructed her Foley pits so they are built into the floor. Some of the pits have some air under them, but her dirt pit is actually dirt on a dirt foundation. Thomas’s home is out in a more rural area of Los Angeles, so she does not have a lot of street traffic. Also, her home is built up in the hills, so her floors are away from the street.

Another innovation is that she has several exchangeable surfaces that can slide into the top of a pit. This allows her to have more surface choices without the need for a big room with several custom pits. One is a shallow splash pit that can have a metal grate over it for a hollow metal surface.

Thomas has utilized two clever ideas to maximize the storage space for her props. Her room has a Rubbermaid garage system rail that looks like crown molding. She can clip all sorts of things onto the rail. Also, she has pegboard on her wall. Not only is pegboard acoustically absorptive but she can also hang props on it.

For her mixing booth glass, Thomas has followed conventional Foley stage wisdom. She has two panes of glass, one angled, so the sound waves do not reflect either in the stage or between the panes.

Thomas uses a TV monitor for her picture. This monitor has an articulating arm, so she can move the monitor to any angle in the room. This allows her to use all her space for performing cues. Monitors give off less noise than a typical television, so while it may be tempting to get a fancy television, think sonically. Get a monitor that can be moved around. That is a simple and effective solution.

![Figure 6.3  Pegboard for hanging items, and cupboards and a kitchen surface for performing cues. ¶ Source: Photo by the author](Images/fig6_3.jpg)

*Figure 6.3*  *Pegboard for hanging items, and cupboards and a kitchen surface for performing cues.* ¶ *Source: Photo by the author*

The door to her stage is a double door. This is an essential element to diminish the effect of noise from the mixing booth and from outside. One door will not be enough to keep noise from leaking. Two doors together, constructed with a bit of air in between them, offer the best chance of keeping sounds isolated. It helps if all doors that lead to the outside or to other rooms are double doors.

## [**IT’S THE PITS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_3)

Foley pits are ideally set into the ground. The size can vary, but keep in mind that at some time more than one person is going to be standing in a pit or on a surface together. Thus, the size needs to easily accommodate that scenario. The location of pits in relation to each other is a matter of personal taste. It is helpful to think about what surfaces one travels to and from most frequently and put them next to each other. Keep in mind that anything with metal on it will cause “ring off” on a cue. That will be a constant problem. It is best to have any pit with metal away from most of the others. Rugs or foam baffles can be used to cover the metal.

Some artists like to have exterior surface pits near each other and interior surfaces near each other. They also like to have a concrete surface near one or two interior surfaces, for example, wood and linoleum or tile. It really is just logic. Most live-action shows will, at some point, have a character go inside from outside and vice versa.

Some artists like to have two dirt pits, if possible. One would contain very gritty, coarse dirt that has a noticeably dry sound. Another, however, might contain a softer kind of dirt. Try decomposed granite or brown dirt. Potting soil is not recommended. It is moist, which leads to all sorts of things that grow and cause an odor, and it has a soft sound that has virtually no character at all. It is a good idea to have at least one gravel pit. Foley artists can use fine pea gravel and add some of the gravel to a dirt pit to create more interest to the dirt. Rather than have large rock gravel sitting in a pit, store a bag of it for occasional use. Buy the dirt and gravel at any large home improvement supplies store. A small section of real, poured asphalt is a great addition. Put it near the concrete.

In addition to a concrete surface, there should be at least one hard wood surface, but two hard woods with contrasting sounds would be better. If possible, it is terrific to have a boardwalk surface. This surface needs to be built over a pit so there is echo and life to the sound. Another practical surface is an industrial pallet. It is also useful to have a section of tile and/or linoleum. Metal surfaces are terrific, if they can be built in. However, it can also be useful to have many different types of metal surface plates that are easily stored, because metal surfaces vary greatly in the movie world. Think about the escalator, the subway grate, the bus, metal bridges, etc. It might not be possible to build all of these sounds with a small room and budget. *Be practical*. Build what is used the most and store the rest. Usually, when creating something futuristic, epic, or animated, a Foley artist needs several possible metal sounds. Keep in mind that metal reflects sound and adds a quality to any prop recorded around it. It is a good idea to have absorptive material around metal or drape cloth over it when not in use.

It is helpful to have a mat to use for grass. Some Foley artists have found that the grass mats used for display around Easter or summertime are perfect. Wholesalers who specialize in display materials for retailers usually have several kinds of grass-like materials. Astroturf is not the same surface and sounds like what it is. Previously, many artists used quarter-inch tape. It was fast, accessible, and inexpensive. With the improvements in technology, sound recording is so sensitive now that sometimes quarter-inch tape sounds rough and scratchy; it is fine for augmenting certain types of foliage, but for grass it may not be acceptable. If one is relying on quarter-inch tape, it might help the sound to put it under a piece of cloth in the dirt pit.

Any Foley stage needs various carpet and rug surfaces. Industrial carpet pieces and various throw rugs are essential. Some stages have carpeting built over pit covers, which is useful for minimizing the reflection of the covers themselves but is not always useful to walk on. Variety and versatility are the goal.

If it is possible to have a real water pit, with a pump to empty it, that is great. The sound is better if it is flush to the ground, which means cutting into the floating floor. If this is not possible, then a water pit that is above the ground is fine. Be careful about using a microphone with water cues. Water slaps against the sides and can sound like a bathtub. Move the mic around to see where the best placement is to avoid reflection and splashing. The pit should be deep enough to simulate the ocean. A good guideline is to allow several feet of space for water, which will prevent it from spilling out or splashing everywhere on the stage.

Many films have dishes in the sink or water drip cues. Therefore, it is useful to have a place to install a sink and faucet. If it is not possible to do that (which is more often than not the case), then the workspace will need to include a large vessel in which to drip and splash small amounts of water.

Foley stages get dirty. Walking in dirt pits puts dirt in the air. It is essential to have an air conditioner system that cleans the air. After dirt pits are walked for a few hours the air is unhealthy to breathe, and chances are an artist cannot work with the air conditioner on because the noise will be recorded, so calculate how to manage dirty air. Also, the dirt lands on surfaces, so stages need to be cleaned regularly. This is a real problem. Often Foley artists are asked to use a stage that someone else has used during another shift. Because there is not enough time to clean between shifts, the dirt can accumulate, and an unhealthy work environment may result. Also, dirt pits become full of bacteria, so the dirt needs to be removed and replaced regularly. It takes time and discipline to maintain appropriate Foley hygiene. However, it is the considerate and professional way to proceed.

## [**TOOL TIME**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_4)

When it comes to purchasing equipment for the stage, although it is seductive to buy the priciest tools with the most accessories, these are really unnecessary for a Foley stage. Even a downloadable edition of Pro Tools will suffice for most purposes. It is important to remember the task at hand. Mixers need to be able to record props and footsteps with a wide variety of dynamic range. It is best to have several microphone choices. A preamplifier is essential for the recording system, as well as a microphone amplifier. A toolkit or application that utilizes time code and some kind of intercom system that allows the artist on the stage to hear playback and allows the mixer to hear the artist in between cues are preferable. If possible, some kind of audio or visual system that alerts the Foley artist to the upcoming cue should be installed. Most Foley stages incorporate a series of beeps (as is used in ADR to cue the actor prior to a line) or a visual streamer that crosses the picture to predict the cue.

Thomas has several microphones and a preamp. “I knew what I needed and what I didn’t need,” she says. Sennheiser, Schoeps, and Neumann have well-respected microphones that are typically used when recording Foley. Ric Viers, author of *The Sound Effects Bible*, is also fond of Rode microphones. Microphones are like wines: there are many varieties and they appeal to personal choice, but some are standards. The most popular microphone for the most overall usage is the Sennheiser 416.

## [**ALTERNATIVE SPACES: WALK A MILE IN MY SHOES**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_5)

Some sound professionals like to try other ways to record Foley. Mark Mangini is an Academy Award–winning SSE for the movie *Mad Max: Fury Road* (2015) and is responsible for the sound design of many notable soundtracks. He tried a different approach when he supervised the sound on *The Green Mile* (1999). Mangini had grown frustrated with sitting on a dub stage during predubs while the rerecording mixers would finesse the Foley to make it sound realistic. As he states it, “It doesn’t sound like what it’s meant to represent. It is meant to represent some acoustical reality that we can all relate to. We record Foley in a way that is surreal.”

So Mangini decided to try something different. He wanted to mimic reality, so he went on his own location-scouting endeavor. He wanted to “cast” locations for their “acoustical signature,” and he found an abandoned military base that had a concrete corridor and an abandoned prison. He recorded “auditions” of the spaces and had a remote Foley rig assembled with a battery-operated Pro Tools console. He worked his budget to allow for the unusual approach to recording Foley, and he left time and money in the budget for mistakes and unexpected events. At this point, he was convinced he would record the Foley in this new way.

Finding a Foley artist who wanted to try this new concept, however, was not so easy. The logistics of how to record, where to keep props, how to control the sound, technology, and workflow, and other unforeseen issues caused most of the Foley artists Mangini approached to be less than excited. Foley mixers also had concerns. A mixer is used to following a kinesthetic routine with their mixing board and monitor that allow optimal functioning. A change in the recording configuration is a disconcerting issue for any mixer.

Robert Winder, who at the time managed the stages and operations at Warner Hollywood Studios (formerly Samuel Goldwyn, now under new ownership and called The Lot Studios), proposed a compromise. The film had been shot on the lot, and the shooting stages were still available. They had been built with specifications that were authentic to the real-life spaces of prisons and offices. Thus, instead of having fake surfaces and walls that cause soundstages to sound similar to each other, these sets had a more authentic look and sound than most. Frank Darabont, the director, had insisted on this detail in the sets.

So Mangini did a test. He directed Foley artist John Roesch to do sample Foley on both the sets and the Foley stage, and he let the director pick his favorite. The director chose the set samples. Thus, the first issue was resolved. The Foley would be shot on the sets of the film.

The next challenge was how to record the Foley. According to Mangini, Mary Jo Lang, a meticulous Foley mixer, was reluctant to abandon her methodology of mixing, lest the product suffer. She had the configuration of her mixing board and monitor in positions that allowed her to mix with optimal efficiency and maximum technical expertise. It happened that Warner Hollywood had recently upgraded its infrastructure with fiber optics throughout the lot, including the soundstages and all recording stages. This meant it was technically possible to have sound travel across the lot from the soundstages to the Foley stage. It was also possible to have a talk-back system, a monitor, and headphones put on a rolling rig, so Roesch and Hilda Hodges, his Foley partner, could perform the Foley on the sets while Lang watched and guided them from her well-oiled mixing booth.

Roesch and Hodges had more adjustments to make. Mangini wanted the footsteps walked in a nontraditional way. Typically, a microphone is set in one place, and the artist walks the character while staying in one location. Mangini wanted to try to have the characters walk away or toward the microphone to replicate the action on screen. This is not an easy task for a Foley artist who has learned how to walk characters while staying in place. One is not quite sure how far away to walk, as a Foley artist is always aware of where the mic is. Mangini wanted Roesch and Hodges to abandon the established practice. Mangini decided to place the microphone on a boom, as is done in production, and to move the mic stands around. As he would say to Roesch, “John, walk down the mile—really—walk *down* the mile.”

However trying the process might have been for all involved, two things are certain. First, the soundtrack itself is a first-rate sound job, and second, human ingenuity allowed for experimentation.

## [**WALLS INSIDE WALLS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_6)

Walter Spencer bought a house in Echo Park, which is near downtown Los Angeles, and began working as a music engineer and sound editor. He was working on low-budget films and was finding he was doing every task on the sound: editing, Foley, and mixing. He asked himself a question: “Can I turn this house into a place that can do everything?” He decided to try.

He built a Foley stage in his basement, which is 14 feet × 20 feet. He put walls inside of the walls to ensure there were no right angles. He built the pits right into the ground and used decomposed granite and brown dirt for his pits. He has a sandpit, concrete, a full array of props, and a curtain that hides his props. In just a few minutes he can clean the stage, and after pulling a few curtains to isolate sound, he can convert the Foley stage into an ADR recording studio.

His console is a Pro Tools system, which he uses for mixing and editing, and he found his Foley artist, Mike Horton, through a Craig’s List advertisement. He has a 5.1 mixing room as well. The answer to his question, of course, is yes.

## [**A FOLEY HOUSE IS ALSO A HOME**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_7)

Andy Malcolm is a Foley artist in Uxbridge, Canada. Prior to becoming a Foley artist, Malcolm was an assistant sound editor in Toronto and trained with two British sound editors, Al Streeter and Peter Burgis, who had come to Canada from London to kick-start the feature film, postproduction, sound editing business.

Malcolm had studied mime, which turned out to be a helpful prerequisite for work as a Foley artist. Mime taught him how to break down the body into its basic elements and build them up in different configurations to mimic the body actions of the characters on screen. This helped Malcolm to better understand how to give weight and character to the footsteps of the actors.

Malcolm made the move out to Uxbridge to create his own Foley and sound effects studio—on 25 acres—which he has named The Foley Farm. He has converted his actual home—the home he and his wife live in—into a working Foley stage, as well as having added two additional studios to the property. For Malcolm, his work does not include only the traditional Foley that many artists create or even the more design-oriented Foley. Malcolm, instead, sees Foley as encompassing any sound effect he can create or make for a film—including sounds more classically edited in.

Because Malcolm is a well-known Foley artist in Canada, he has designed his own workflow, including a different set of protocols for his team. He has a Foley mixer, Don White, a separate Pro Tools operator, Jenna Dalla Riva, and his Foley artist partner Goro Koyamo.

![Figure 6.4  Various wooden surfaces. Notice the long wooden surface for walking perspective Foley. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Andy Malcolm, The Foley Farm](Images/fig6_4.jpg)

*Figure 6.4*  *Various wooden surfaces. Notice the long wooden surface for walking perspective Foley.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Andy Malcolm, The Foley Farm*

Malcolm returned the favor of having been trained to be the top Foley artist in Canada by training one of Ireland’s Foley artists, Caoimhe Doyle. Doyle had watched Malcolm work in Ireland. She visited Canada to apprentice and train with Malcolm at his facility. Doyle then went back to Ireland to design and build a dedicated Foley stage at Ardmore Sound, where she now works with Foley mixer Jean McGrath.

![Figure 6.5  Malcolm has his entire house wired. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Andy Malcolm, The Foley Farm](Images/fig6_5.jpg)

*Figure 6.5*  *Malcolm has his entire house wired.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Andy Malcolm, The Foley Farm*

## [**SEVERAL IDENTICAL STAGES**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_8)

Julien Naudin, originally from Paris, has several Foley stages—in several countries—constructed exactly the same for him to perform his Foley. However, that is not the only unique aspect to his method. Naudin has several different spaces set up as the realistic rooms they represent. He has kitchens with working appliances and running water, living rooms, and even a car with working parts inside. He separates the rooms with curtains. His aesthetic sense, which is different from what the American audience has become accustomed to, is more naturalistic and understated. So, the rooms are set up realistically to have the acoustic “sound-print” that is more typical in a dialogue film.

## [**THE REAL MAGILLA**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_9)

Heikki Kossi, a Finnish Foley artist, lives in a small town outside Helsinki, where he has built his Foley studio. He has several small stages, each set up as a real room. They are set up more traditionally than Naudin’s, as he does not use curtains to separate them but has an actual different room for each type of room. He sets his microphone on a mattress because his Foley pits are dug directly into the ground. Because he lives in a very small town, he feels fortunate to have found someone capable of mixing Foley quite competently.

![Figure 6.6  Foley pits with various types of dirt and gravel. Notice the various microphones and wall treatments and the isolation of microphone stands from the floor. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Misa Jakovljevic, Markoni Studio, Serbia](Images/fig6_6.jpg)

*Figure 6.6*  *Foley pits with various types of dirt and gravel. Notice the various microphones and wall treatments and the isolation of microphone stands from the floor.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Misa Jakovljevic, Markoni Studio, Serbia*

## [**THE NATURAL TOUCH**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_10)

Misa Jakovljevic prefers to Foley outside sometimes. He does not Foley his animal footsteps inside, and he will often find an isolated area in his Serbian location that allows him to perform his footsteps in the wilderness. He likes the real-world sound that happens out of doors and the natural air on the microphone. However, his studio provides all of the necessary components for interior Foley.

Italian Foley artist Marco Ciorba also prefers to do what he calls “original sound” whenever possible. He likes to go to authentic places to record Foley. Italy has a wonderful array of historic cities and various terrains, and Ciorba likes to take advantage of that aspect of being there.

It should not be forgotten that Walter Murch is infamous for taking his sound associates along to record Foley footsteps at various locales in the San Francisco Bay area. Murch prefers realistic and understated Foley that fits in with production and does not draw attention to itself, nor sound artificial.

## [**NEXT FOLEY STOP**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec6_11)

While it is important to note at this point that the accepted standard for Foley is still to perform it on a Foley stage and rely on the rerecording mixers to add elements that will enhance the believability of the environment, there will always be those who want to test limits. By breaking away from the comfort zone, an artist might discover a better way to approach the work. There is a plethora of possibilities of how you might want to record Foley. Just remember the basic tenets: an artist needs clean recordings that can work with the production sound and the edited effects, and you need to be able to edit the tracks. Rules were made to be learned, so know which ones can be broken and still get the job done.

![Figure 6.7 Various guns and small props. Notice the prop telephone and throw rugs. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Misa Jakovljevic, Markoni Studio, Serbia](Images/fig6_7.jpg)

*Figure 6.7* *Various guns and small props. Notice the prop telephone and throw rugs.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Misa Jakovljevic, Markoni Studio, Serbia*

# [**chapter | seven**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch07)

## [**Mix and Match**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch07)

### [The Foley and Rerecording Mixers](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch07)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-10](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-10)

![](Images/fig0013.jpg)

Ask a Foley artist who their best friend at work is, and almost invariably it will be the Foley mixer. An artist can be gifted, professional, and efficient yet create disappointing Foley for a project. The reason is simple: the mixer and the artist work in collaboration, and both the artist(s) and the mixer work more cooperatively when they have similar philosophies about the work as well as compatible personalities on the stage. The relationship between Foley artist and Foley mixer is so symbiotic that these partnerships can last for many years and produce exceptional Foley with a signature sound that is recognized and respected throughout the professional sound world, as well as be responded to viscerally by the film-going public. It is not unusual for a supervising sound editor to rely on a favorite team of Foley artist(s) and Foley mixer to provide consistent quality and style year after year.

Foley artists can have frustrating experiences with mixers, and navigating a project can become difficult. On a Foley stage, the artist(s) and mixer are spending day after day in a dark room, making noises for a film or episodic series for a supervisor who may have a short postproduction sound schedule or one that includes many film editing changes. Trust between artist and mixer ensures that all challenges are easier and more expeditious, with as little conflict as possible. Thus, it is important at this point to address the role of the Foley mixer in detail and explain how the mixer can best support and work in concert with the Foley artists assigned to the show. Additionally, it is essential for the Foley artist to understand how to be the best partner for the mixer.

## [**THE MIXER IS ANOTHER SET OF EARS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_1)

Ordinarily, the Foley artists are the primary concern when a supervising sound editor (SSE) is putting the Foley team together. Whether the decision about the artists is based on a past relationship, a specific genre of film, or a contractual deal with a company, it is usually the case that the SSE is concerned with who the Foley artists are. Normally, the “lead” Foley artist is the first point of contact for the SSE, and the responsibility for the second artist may be left to either the primary Foley artist or the editor. Additionally, sometimes the SSE has a preferred Foley stage for the Foley sessions. Sometimes the artists come attached to a particular stage. Rarely, however, does an SSE decide to have the Foley performed based solely on who the Foley mixer is; however, rerecording mixers have preferences that might be influential. That being said, there is nothing like a great Foley mixer who knows how to make the most of the room, the acoustics, the microphones, the technology, and, at times, even the artists. The best Foley mixers have the added quality of knowing how to evoke the best art from the talent on the stage. A wise Foley artist values the input of the Foley mixer and lets the mixer know it.

Almost every Foley artist has a favorite mixer. Some have several they really enjoy working with, while others will work only with one. All artists have different preferences for what they like in their mixer. But they all prefer to work with mixers with whom they have a rapport. The same can be said for the mixers. Foley mixers work hard to make the Foley artists’ work sound as good as possible. Even a beginning mixer has a lot to offer the Foley artist. To begin with, the mixer is another set of ears.

## [**MORE THAN SIMPLY RECORDING**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_2)

The Foley mixer is responsible for actually recording the sounds the Foley artist creates. What is usually confusing to those not in the field is why the person recording the sounds is not called a recordist or why they are not called an engineer, as the counterpart in the music industry is called. The job title has to do with the union to which the mixer belongs.

In production, the person who records the sound in the studio or on location is called the production mixer. This is because this person is actually mixing the production dialogue, not just recording it. Thus, the convention has been extended to include the person who records the Foley and the ADR and rerecords in the final dub. Anyone who records sound in film is called a mixer, except for the field recordist, whose specialty is to go out and record customized sound effects for a particular project. There used to be actual recordists on the Foley stage. When Foley was recorded on magnetic film or 24-track tape, a recordist would listen for pops, cracks, or any magnetic tape dropouts from flaws in the recording medium. Additionally, the recordist would keep cue sheets and set up the studio. But today, with all equipment being digital, there is no need for a recordist any longer. There are no cue sheets to shuffle and mark, there are no magnetic film reels to change over, and there is no need for another set of ears to listen for sound problems. Digital recording has ensured that the unwanted sounds will show up in a wave glitch that is visible to the mixer. Regardless, the mixer does the actual recording.

## [**FOLEY MIXING IS AN ART**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_3)

The Foley mixer has usually worked their way up the ladder by being a recordist and, in the days of magnetic film, a loader. The loader actually loaded the magnetic film onto the recorder. The recordist would sit in a separate room wearing headphones, would listen for any problems in the track, and would support the mixer in any way necessary. The Foley mixer would be allowed to sit in that chair only after learning those two previous jobs.

Foley mixing is an art, but a mixer needs some technical knowledge. Today, because of the technology involved with mixing, the mixer needs to know a great deal about Pro Tools and some of the plug-ins that can be used with the Pro Tools software (or a parallel editing and mixing system). But the main qualities of a good mixer include having a good ear for aesthetics, an excellent eye for sync, a skilled hand at recording and punching in for special cues, and an understanding of the job of the artist. As has been para phrased by many professional Foley artists, the Foley artist’s job is on one side of the window, and the mixer’s job is on the other side. They need to understand what each other does, but not interfere.

Before digital recording and mixing on the Foley stage became the standard, mixers recorded most Foley without adding perspective in the recording. There was an expectation that the Foley mixer was recording the sounds flat and that all perspective or reflection in the sound would be added during the final dub. This industry standard led to the practice of focusing on clean mixing, with very little extra character added to any particular sound. The goal was to sound realistic, with a few exceptions. The task of the rerecording mixer included predubbing the Foley to better complement the production track, sound effects, ADR, and music. Any enhancements or nuances added to the Foley were left to the skills and aesthetic taste of the rerecording mixers

Later, as technology changed, recording to hard drives became the norm, and more bells and whistles were available to the Foley mixer. As a result, it was only a matter of time before some mixers began to expand their role on the Foley stage. Add to this the normal effect of the marketplace preferring the least expensive and most productive use of labor, along with the modifications of professional sound unions to allow more crossover in editing and mixing tasks, and the result has been an expanded role for the Foley mixer. Many Foley mixers now utilize tools that were previously the territory of the rerecording mixer. Addition ally, sound editors can predub on their digital workstations, and mixers can perform minor editing tasks with Foley before forwarding the recorded tracks to the editor and/or rerecording mixers.

Foley mixers are as different in their approach to their work as Foley artists are. No two mixers or artists perceive their role exactly as someone else does, nor do they practice their craft in the same way. Their specific personalities, backgrounds, and aesthetic preferences vary greatly. The common thread between all of them is the workflow and professional process that is expected of them.

## [**THE CLASSICAL APPROACH**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_4)

Robert Deschaine started mixing at Gomillion Sound in Hollywood in the early 1970s. Ted Gomillion needed a studio manager, so Deschaine was hired. At this point, he had no experience with microphones, cables, recording, or mixing films at all. He decided to learn all the jobs that were performed at the studio. “Back in those days there were a few studios—Producers Sound, Cine Sound, Gomillion Sound—where people could get training.
