We didn’t get much money, but we worked long hours and we learned our craft,” Deschaine remembers.

Before long, Deschaine was mixing Foley, ADR, and final dubs. He knew every job in the postproduction sound recording and mixing world. Because he would be the rerecording mixer on the films and television shows where he mixed Foley and ADR, he had a unique perspective on how to best mix the Foley. Deschaine remembers someone at Gomillion telling him, “Foley is supposed to be there, and you’re not supposed to pick it out.” With that in mind, he became very aware of how to mix Foley so that it fit in with the scene. He found that all Foley artists had different styles and approaches: “Sometimes there were people who were heavy on their heel-toe or toe-heel, and I could always tell that someone wasn’t doing it right when I got it on the dub stage.”

Deschaine learned to mix at a time when Foley was entering a period of developing specialization in the field. While a lot of the editors were still performing their own Foley, a new breed of Foley walkers was being introduced into the mix. Also, at this time, Foley was recorded onto three tracks of 35-mm magnetic film called a full coat. Deschaine would get the picture and cue sheets for the Foley at 8:00 a.m., and it had to be completed by 6:00 p.m. so it could be dubbed for the next airdate:

> I saw a lot of people \[Foley artists\] who were very talented and could do it quickly and get it in sync, and we didn’t have to cut it. We made sure that when it left the stage everything was in sync because they \[the editors\] didn’t have time to cut it.

### [**Good Storytelling With Sound Should Be Foremost in the Mixer’s Mind**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_4_1)

Because Deschaine had been involved with production and was not yet a rerecording mixer, he was aware of making the Foley sound as much like production sound as possible: “I had to learn perspective and moving the microphone where you think it would be if they were on location.” He was careful about “not getting it too close to the mic or too hot and hopefully you won’t distort.”

Deschaine also mixed ADR, and because he had been an actor himself, his past profession influenced his approach to both Foley and ADR. He has a real admiration for Foley artists who are well prepared and do their homework for each project.

> I would watch these people and they would put their minds into whoever the actors or actresses were, and they would emulate these people. When you would finally put it all together, you would think the actors or actresses were really walking that scene.

Deschaine also recalls, “I would walk in my mind. I would visualize that I’m into their feet. I’d have to walk with them so I could punch in when their foot is in the air, so I didn’t erase anything.” Of course, technology has come such a long way since the days of magnetic tape or 24-track, so mixers have different methodologies for punching and editing on the stage. But because Deschaine trained during a period of more rudimentary equipment, his skill was honed to be specific and efficient.

A mixer with a background as vast and as long-lived as Deschaine can bring to the Foley stage a sensibility of filmmaking that many who have honed their craft on digital systems may need more time to develop. The tools will always change. But good storytelling with sound should be foremost in the mixer’s mind. Sound serves the narrative. Foley is rarely the star of the film, and it is critical to remember the lessons that a veteran mixer like Deschaine can pass on to the younger Foley mixer who is enthralled with technology. Learning on less sophisticated tools empowered Deschaine to refine his skill to a point where he was not reliant on digital when he made the transition. He could make a beautiful and seamless punch in between footsteps and never make the Foley artist repeat the cue. That is what his well-trained skill allowed him to do for his artists.

### [**Pushing the Envelope**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_4_2)

Deschaine is one of the most experienced mixers around and also one of the most empowering. A devoted team player who believes in collaboration, Deschaine has trained several Foley and ADR mixers with respectable careers who began as recordists with him. One of those mixers is David Jobe, who has been mixing for about 25 years. He began his sound career at a small company that worked in electronic newsgathering. He then worked his way into a job at Warner Hollywood in the late 1980s as a “dummy loader.” He loaded the recorders with magnetic film for those who mixed on dubbing stages. As his career progressed, his swiftness at doing his tasks, his intelligence, and his amiable personality led him to a position as an ADR recordist for Deschaine at Skywalker South. It was not long before Deschaine recognized Jobe’s intelligence and discipline and allowed him to mix a few cues on the ADR stage while Deschaine watched and coached. This used to be the traditional method for training new mixers in Hollywood. The studio was a training ground for new mixers. If a recordist seemed to have talent and a good work ethic, they would be encouraged to mix.

Jobe caught on quickly, and before long, he would come into work and be told he had to mix ADR because Deschaine was sick. Jobe still does not know if that was the true story, as Deschaine is known for being generous with his time and his mixer’s chair. Either way, Jobe was moved up to mixer. Thus, he was baptized by fire. Shortly after, he was assigned his own stage at Skywalker South and began his career mixing Foley. He mixed there for several years before going to Warner Brothers in Burbank, where he has been mixing Foley for 14 years.

### [**“I Choose to Mix Closer to the Cliff”**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_4_3)

Jobe is a mixer who pushes himself to excel. Even before Pro Tools became the standard mixing format and provided more options, he was using two or three microphones on a stage. This is a practice he still enjoys. One mic is used to record the prop or footsteps, one might be used for reflection (by aiming it at the ceiling or putting in an adjacent room), and, at times, one more might be placed “off-axis.” He is adventurous in his use of microphones but can exercise caution when he knows the rerecording mixer prefers less perspective from the Foley stage. However, as Jobe puts it, “I choose to mix closer to the cliff. It’s more satisfying.” He is quick to note, however, that when he mixes Foley for television, he is less likely to be experimental. Episodic streamed programming and television shows are mixed to be seen in homes or on computers rather than in a theatre, so they are typically mixed with the various devices in mind.

Some veteran SSEs prefer to have Foley recorded flat, as has been the tradition. But there are those who depend on the Foley stage to add in equalization and perspective, so there is less time spent on the dub bing stage. Scott Hecker finds that rerecording mixers are becoming more open to some perspective and reflection being painted on the Foley stage:

> With the schedules shrinking now, the last thing they want to do is to take the time to put room into it. Smart Foley artists will put in a moderate amount of room application; not as much as it can be, but at least to fit it in the space.

George Lara, Foley mixer for Marko Costanzo at C-5 in New Jersey, likes to use four microphones to paint perspective, utilizing two Sennheiser 416 mics and at least one Schoeps. “We have a big room,” Lara relates, and as Costanzo adds, he and Lara focus on “matching what we hear in production.” While Costanzo and Lara prefer to mix Foley in a live room, part of the Foley stage is designed to sound more like an exterior, which is less live. The team will often mix 16 footsteps tracks and 24 props tracks for a major feature film, and when they worked on a Quibi, they mixed one with the sound quality of a feature. “They have to be juicy to keep you watching,” muses Lara.

![Figure 7.2  Foley mixer George Lara. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of George Lara](Images/fig7_2.jpg)

*Figure 7.2*  *Foley mixer George Lara.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of George Lara*

## [**THE TRADITIONALIST**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_5)

Karin Roulo, a Foley mixer since the late 1980s, is more traditional in her approach. She has a unique background for a Foley mixer (she was one of the first female mixers in the industry) because she started her career as an engineer. She is methodical and precise and is very aware of the technical aspects of mixing. She initially trained at Warner Hollywood (formerly Goldwyn Studios and now called The Lot) under John Bonner, the chief engineer. She trained for a year, and Bill Varney, the lead rerecording mixer, let her sit in on final dubbing mixes. She learned how postproduction sound was used in the final mix of films. She then moved into production as a cable puller.

The cable puller on a film shoot holds the cable for the camera operator. Cables can get in the way, get disconnected, be walked on, or even appear in the scene. The cable puller ensures that none of these events occur. Roulo was utilized in unusual settings because as a small woman (a rarity in engineering situations), she was able to fit into strange places, like under a table.

### [**She Is Tuned in to the Quality of the Recording**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_5_1)

Roulo later found herself at Ryder Sound training to repair Nagras. The Nagra was the standard machine used to record field effects for sound editors, as well as for production mixing. It was here that she became entranced with Foley. John Roesch and Joan Rowe worked with Foley mixer Richard Rogers, and Roulo was not only fascinated with the process but was impressed with the way Roesch and Rowe worked as a team. Chuck Campbell was the supervisor on the stage. “It was a good introduction,” says Roulo of her first experience with Foley. “He \[Campbell\] knew what he wanted, and he was able to tell them. He was able to express how the director wanted the characters in the film perceived.”

Soon after, Roulo was brought in to be the Foley recordist at Ryder Sound. The recordist who usually worked with Richard Rogers was sick, so Roulo worked as a replacement. She stayed on for the entire film.

It was not long before she found herself mixing Foley at Larson Sound in Burbank. She had known the owner, Rick Larson, through her engineering career, and he hired her on the spot. She worked with Hilda Hodges and Alyson Moore on several television shows. As Roulo became more experienced as a mixer, Bill Varney was put in charge of the soundstages at Universal; he brought Roulo over to work on the Foley stage. Before long, she was mixing on several stages all over town. Roulo tuned in to the quality of the recording:

> I know what levels the equipment can handle, and I want to give as full a sound as possible, so I think I tend to layer my sounds more, especially props. I’d rather spread things out and give the dubbing \[rerecording\] mixers more flexibility because they may not see it the same way I do.

## [**CONVENTIONS AND THE UNCONVENTIONAL**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_6)

Some mixers like a Foley stage with very little reflection. They call that a “dead room.” When a room has too much life, they find that it is hard to get rid of the “bounce” or “ping.” While the artists can place room baffles on the stage to protect themselves from reflection, sometimes it is impossible to get the room dead enough for sensitive and quiet cues. Then there is the issue of constantly moving baffles around the room and placing them in an ideal position. Roulo prefers mixing in a dead room. She prefers the more classic approach of mixing the Foley flat but adds, “It depends on the dubbing mixer. I’ve had a few who would have preferred that I had mixed it more, and that is usually when they were on a tight budget.” Roulo has helped construct electronic parts for to the problems of a live room: “They may need to change the edit on the dub stage. If the room is too live, they may have trouble matching the sound.”

Some mixers, especially those who started in music where the instruments are enhanced by more life in a room, keep that sensibility when recording Foley. It is not uncommon for a mixer with this perspective to be unsatisfied with any Foley stage, because they are typically designed to have soft walls and ceilings to deaden the sound. Jobe prefers a live room, or as he likes to express it, “When I say lively, I mean those stages that have more breath to them. When you have a dead stage, it colors the sound. The walls pull out certain frequencies.”

Jim Ashwell, originally from Topeka, Kansas, began his career in music recording. After taking music engineering workshops at the Dick Grove School of Music in Los Angeles, he began practicing his skills at various music studios in area. One studio, called One Step Up, provided Ashwell the opportunity to work as the second engineer, where he worked with such artists as The Eagles. “I hung out and refused to go home,” Ashwell muses. He also worked at The Record Plant, where he did what he calls “overflow” work. By 1982, he was recording sessions on his own and began to transition into being the Foley recordist for some Foley sessions. In 1986, Ashwell became a Foley mixer and worked with many of the more experienced Foley artists in Los Angeles at the time. One of these Foley artists was Dan O’Connell, with whom he had a long professional relationship. O’Connell partnered with John Cucci and started his own Foley stage, coincidentally named One Step Up. The irony is not lost on Ashwell. In 2012, Ashwell, O’Connell, and Cucci went to Fox Studios, where the two Foley artists still work and Ashwell has retired.

Ashwell’s focus as a mixer was less about technology and more about art. He would predub his Foley tracks with the aesthetic sensibilities he had developed as a music engineer. His concern was how to build the scene rather than focus on a specific detail. He often

![Figure 7.3  Veteran Foley mixer Jim Ashwell. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Jim Ashwell](Images/fig7_3.jpg)

*Figure 7.3*  *Veteran Foley mixer Jim Ashwell.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Jim Ashwell*

used a harmonizer on the Foley stage, which allowed him to be more creative. Ashwell recalls, “more than talent, I was willing to take chances and try anything.” He adds, “I only learned enough of the technical stuff to do what I needed to do.” Ashwell credits O’Connell for allowing him the freedom to be creative: “He’s \[O’Connell\] an artist—he approaches it with an artist’s eyes and ears.” By working with O’Connell, who Ashwell describes as “unorthodox” and “less concerned with continuity than building Foley scene by scene,” Ashwell was empowered to experience more of the “give and take” between artist and mixer. As Ashwell muses about how O’Connell would describe him: “you’ve got great big giant ears.”

### [**Less Is More**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_6_1)

As schedules get tighter and more is asked of those on the Foley stage, the mixer spends more time tweaking the Foley with some edits. Digital recording makes this relatively easy for the mixer, and even union mixers are asked to do more of this than ever before. Boundaries between editors and mixers are blurring quickly. Editors are premixing and mixing and doing quick fix edits. New Foley mixers will have no ethical issue with this. But it is good to remember that this is a relatively recent development and comes primarily from two factors: technological changes and economic imperatives. As producers find more ways to cut budgets, they rely on the technology to dictate the terms of employment rather than allowing the actual craft to create the appropriate employment boundary. Without making any judgments regarding this transition, which is a consequence of digital convergence, it does put more pressure on the mixer to also know how to edit Foley without taking the life out of it. Be cautious and undercut rather than being exuberant with the editing tools. Less is more.

Ask a Foley mixer about microphone preferences and, while the opinions vary, there is a consensus that most mixers have a few favorites and do not spend a lot of time auditioning microphones. A music engineer, conversely, will pick the exact microphone that brings out the best in each instrument and singer individually. There are some standard microphones that will usually appear in the Foley mixer’s repertoire. The Sennheiser 416 is a bright microphone and has good midrange. That is the microphone of choice for Walter Spencer of Hi Fi Foley. Richard James Hinton, a Foley artist in Bristol, England, relates that his mixer prefers the Sennheiser MK 60. That is also Foley artist Julien Naudin’s favorite microphone for many of his cues. Neumann is a familiar microphone brand, as is Schoeps. The preference is personal and very dependent on the sound quality of the stage. Jobe likes the Neumann because it has a large diaphragm. He uses the Sennheiser as a room microphone because it “packs a punch,” and he will often use a Schoeps microphone as well. “You want a quality, warm microphone,” he says.

![Figure 7.4  Misa Jakovljevic’s mixer and recordist. In Serbia, they still use a mixture of analog and digital formats and need both professionals at the board. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Misa Jakovljevic](Images/fig7_4.jpg)

*Figure 7.4*  *Misa Jakovljevic’s mixer and recordist. In Serbia, they still use a mixture of analog and digital formats and need both professionals at the board.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Misa Jakovljevic*

How a mixer works with the stage and its peculiar qualities is an important aspect of mixing. Mixers need to have the patience to work with whatever issues come up with the sound floor on the stage and not be deterred by them. Whatever concerns occur with the actual recording are not the problem of the artists. So, a good mixer always makes the distinction between when the recording is less than acceptable because of the equipment, room, or pilot error and when the artist’s performance needs improvement. A great mixer is always sure to communicate the reasons for problems with a recording, because the natural tendency of a Foley artist is to change what she or he has done if the cue has to be redone. If the mixer wants to ensure that the artist repeats the same performance, they need to communicate that.

### [**It’s a Marriage**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_6_2)

Another quality of a great Foley mixer is respect, if not adoration, for the artists. The mixer should have a great understanding of how artists approach their craft so that any misunderstandings can be avoided. It is important to understand that the mixer’s job is to accommodate the sounds. The Foley artist creates the sounds. Therefore, simple syllogism dictates that the mixer’s job is to accommodate the artist. All Foley artists are different and approach the work differently. A good mixer is flexible and tries to learn the secret language of each artist so that the day goes smoothly. It also helps to make for a great session if the mixer truly wants the artists to feel free to experiment.

The same is true of the artists toward the mixers. It is helpful if the artist is concerned with the challenges of the mixer. Foley mixers are ultimately responsible for the quality of the recording, which involves working with the room’s sonic personality and troubleshooting any problems that arise. Whereas the relationship between one Foley artist and another while working on a film is like a marriage, so too is the relationship between the artists and the mixer. It is complicated, nuanced, and, at its best, pure magic.

Some Foley artists might find themselves feeling impatient with mixers. If this is the case, it is best to remember that the mixer is busy with so many technical aspects of the job that she or he cannot always pay attention to every issue that comes up on the stage itself. So, the artist that is patient with the mixer and tries to accommodate the mixer’s technical needs will get the best collaboration as a result.

![Figure 7.5 Foley mixer Diego Staub mixes in the same room with Foley artist Miguel Barbosa, in Spain. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Miguel Barbosa](Images/fig7_5.jpg)

*Figure 7.5* *Foley mixer Diego Staub mixes in the same room with Foley artist Miguel Barbosa, in Spain.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Miguel Barbosa*

Mixers have their own techniques for creating the ideal working environment with artists. Over the years, they learn how to deal with the various personalities, biorhythms, strengths, and weaknesses of each of the artists. Roulo has a specific approach when working with less experienced Foley artists for the first time:

> The first few cues I would tend to play back more than with an experienced team, because I want them to get an idea of what it’s sounding like. I might call them into the booth and have them take a look at it.

When asked what the hardest part is of working with new artists, she says, “If I know they’re having a hard time getting sync, especially on footsteps, and they won’t let their partner try it, that is the hardest thing; when they get territorial and they’re not willing to see another viewpoint.”

Roulo likes learning from her artists:

> Jerry \[Trent\] taught me to visualize the whole scene, to focus on the lead actors and look from a dancer’s perspective. Greg \[Barbanell\] has a light-hearted approach to Foley. He’s very professional and he is good on comedies. For such a big person, he’s very light on his feet.

She attributes this author with teaching her about layering sounds. As much as I appreciate the acknowledgment, I must confess that mixer Richard Rogers taught me to layer sound elements on various tracks to allow mixing and blending on the dub stage.

### [**It’s All Subjective**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_6_3)

David Jobe explains his method for working with various artists: “My hope is that the Foley artists and I are on the same page aesthetically.” If issues arise, however, he adds, “I will have my battles already chosen. I try to, in a kind and diplomatic way, ease them into what I think might be better. It’s all subjective.”

Time is the enemy for both the artists and the mixer. However, while the artists may be feeling stress to finish the required amount of work for the session, it is the mixer who feels the heat directly when the client is in the mixing booth. Invariably, once an audience is in the booth, the mixer is asked to direct the artist, or “play that back for me,” or “tell them to do it again,” or some other such unspecific advice. The mixer has no choice but to please the client, and the artist does not always know what is being said in the booth. Trust between mixer and artist is essential. The mixer can protect the artist from the deleterious effects of micromanaging by a client. Precious time will be wasted by this alltoo-predictable exercise, but the fault does not lie with the Foley mixer. A sophisticated Foley artist is aware of this and lets the mixer know it.

Until recently, it was the convention to have the SSE or the Foley editor come to the Foley stage for playback. This is the process of playing a reel for the editor to determine if the Foley is going to be satisfactory. This is a good use of time for all involved because the Foley team can work undisturbed, but the client has the opportunity to offer input and comment on the possible “fixes” she or he wants. When time is tight or the budget is slim, this is not always the practice. If possible, however, this is the best approach to ensure that the editor gets what is needed from Foley and the Foley team gets the privacy and best environment to create the optimum results.

Regardless of the time pressure and the monetary limitations, the Foley mixer can always have a huge impact on the quality of the Foley. The relationship between the mixer and the artists can be professional, creative, and absolutely magical.

Bob Deschaine has some advice for aspiring Foley mixers: “Go on a dubbing stage and see what the Foley sounds like after it’s mixed.” When all is said and done, observation of what rerecording mixers do is the best way to learn how to improve one’s contribution to the soundtrack either as a Foley artist or as a Foley mixer.

![Figure 7.6 Foley mixer Isael Huard mixing for Chris Szott. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott](Images/fig7_6.jpg)

*Figure 7.6* *Foley mixer Isael Huard mixing for Chris Szott.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott*

## [**FIX IT IN POST: THE DUB**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_7)

The rerecording mix should be an important aspect of Foley. After all, once the Foley has been cued, performed, and edited, it would seem that the focus then shifts to the more elaborate and dramatic sound effects for dubbing purposes. However, what any filmmaker or film sound fan must remember is that all roads lead to the rerecording process, or what is known in the trade as the dub stage. Because the mixing process is the final destination of all sound, as the line in Arthur Miller’s play *Death of a Salesman* states, “Attention must be paid.” Consequently, a clear comprehension of the traditional Hollywood dubbing process and how it relates to the Foley artist, mixer, and editor is essential. Keep in mind that, with the transition to digital editing and mixing, the dubbing process has also been streamlined. An overview of how the traditional dub would progress helps one to understand the moving parts involved in this final process of postproduction sound. There are variations in this process in Hollywood as well as in other film regions. Those interested in film sound will do well to understand how digital workflows have altered postproduction so drastically that the conventions are continually changing, but the process still retains the classic approach to completing and refining the soundtrack in filmmaking.

![Figure 7.7](Images/fig7_7.jpg)

*Figure 7.7*

### [**The Predub**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_7_1)

Predubbing postproduction sound has traditionally been the territory of the rerecording mixers. We find ourselves in a time when the new technology has led many to believe that anyone with Pro Tools can design, edit, and mix sound as well as any professional. That belief may sell equipment, but in practice it is the aesthetics, craftsmanship, and experience that determine the quality of the soundtrack, not primarily the technology.

In the Hollywood postproduction system, there has been, until the 2000s, a convention of three mixers on a dub stage. One was primarily responsible for dialogue (typically the head mixer), one was in charge of music, and one was in charge of Foley and sound effects. By examining this classical approach, one can discern how it still affects the workflow even as technology has blurred the lines of job responsibilities.

Foley is prepared with the purpose of serving the narrative by attending to the vision of the director, the performances of the actors, and the needs of the dubbing mixers on the stage. Regardless of who has recorded the production sound, who has supervised the sound editing, who has designed any special sound effects, who has shot the ADR with the actors, who has composed and edited the music, and who has had responsibility for the Foley, it is the rerecording mixers who have the final task of combining all audio tracks from predubs, to final dub, to the printmaster. They are the firewall for all of the editors, artists, mixers, actors, and composers. The task of the rerecording mixers is one that requires expertise, aesthetic taste, and the diplomacy to juggle the various requests by competing interests.

With that in mind, it should not be any surprise that the “dubbing” mixers are the ones who hear the complaints and see the temperament of the stage sitters. It makes sense that those involved with the editorial team need to understand their pressures on most of their workdays.

The first days on the dub stage traditionally were primarily for predubs. This is, of course, in addition to any temp dubs that had occurred. Temp dubs introduce conceptual sound ideas for the purpose of further galvanizing the picture and editorial collaboration. Additionally, temp dubs allow the production company to present a respectable exhibition to a screening audience for the purpose of gaining insight into the most likely audience demographic for the project, as well as the critiques the selected test audience might offer for further editorial picture changes. The predub has, in the past, been a critical and time-consuming aspect of the dub. Mixers meticulously analyze and scrutinize each sound and finesse the timbre and quality of each piece of the track. This process requires patience and focus.

A well-executed predub assists the final dub, which is the last chance to “get it right.” As David Stone says, “the predubs are like rehearsing a symphony orchestra; details are everything. The final dub is the concert!” “

Traditionally, when adequate time was allotted for the predub, the mixers would take one category of sound and mix the many tracks down into groupings of six tracks. Dialogue would traditionally be mixed down into six mono tracks. ADR would have six tracks of principals and perhaps another six of group ADR. Hard effects might be three to eight sets of six tracks, including any special design effects. Background effects (BGs) would have one set of six tracks. Foley would have six tracks of footsteps and six tracks of props. The cloth track—which is optional for a domestic dub, but invaluable for a foreign “music and effects” dub—would be split off onto its own track and be played at the final dub, but not at the predub.

Most predubs would ideally start with the dialogue. Everything starts with what is in the production track, so the dialogue predubs would often be given a week or more to truly finesse the tracks and make them sparkle. Next might be ADR, then sound effects, then Foley. The music—which is typically being composed right up until the last minute—would not be included until the final dub. Today, as schedules are more squeezed and time is tighter, it has even become acceptable for one stage to be predubbing dialogue while another might be predubbing Foley. In contemporary dubs, the three-person team is not used anymore. Typically, two mixers, and sometimes one solo mixer (the typical practice in New York film culture), will dub the entire show. Many small independent films also utilize a one-mixer model to dub an entire show. Economics is always a consideration, and digital technology has empowered both editors and mixers to be more fluid in executing tasks.

When mixers would predub Foley, they would find that time was used more effectively if the preparation by the editor had been in anticipation of mixing needs. The tracks needed to be tagged properly and sheets needed to be printed out. Some dubbing mixers would watch the meters, some would look at the sheets and mark them up with notes on where they assigned the tracks in the predub, and some would prefer to look at Pro Tools. The conventions for laying out the tracks, however, were similar to dubbing prior to digital because the system was meant to utilize time efficiently, with a common literacy understood by all sound professionals. Rerecording mixers would be looking for principal footsteps in the leftmost channels, background footsteps in the next channels, and finally props, with similar types of props on similar tracks. The cloth track(s) would be at the far right of the cue sheets.

Richard Portman spent most of his years as a dialogue mixer. He would listen painstakingly for hiss and hum on the tracks: “It all starts with the production dialogue. If it is recorded well then everything else is much easier.” Many dubbing mixers would still probably agree with Portman. While his background as an engineer made him more sensitive to the technical recording quality of the production track, most mixers prefer the production dialogue mixing to be the template used for all remaining sound effects.

Some mixers liked to predub Foley against the dialogue tracks. Sometimes the Foley might be mixed while the mixer listened to the sound effects. This was advantageous because both effects and Foley might have coverage on a scene, so they needed to be played against each other to see which covered the material best. Often, the decision to marry the Foley effect with the edited choice was made in predubs. The purpose of predubs was, and still is, to cull down many units to a smaller, more manageable amount in order to finesse the final mix.

In today’s myriad of technological possibilities, the predub is not a fixed convention. Depending on the film, the SSE, the mixer, and the schedule, the predub might be, in reality, an editor’s session from Pro Tools. The dialogue, however, is still the territory of the dubbing mixer. While sound effects and Foley might predub before ever being presented on a dub stage, the dialogue—which is the most sensitive aspect of any film—is still the cherished territory of the rerecording mixer. While work-flows have changed for other sound professionals, the dialogue mixer must still massage the dialogue until it sparkles. They might want to predub the dialogue alone or, as New York mixer Tom Fleischman prefers, have some ambient sound effects to predub against so he can assess how the dialogue will fit into the scene.

Michael Semanick mixes sound effects, and when he predubs Foley, he likes to take a lot of time to predub. Semanick mixed Foley for quite a while before becoming a rerecording mixer. He likes to play the other elements against the Foley in predubs: “When I’m predubbing, I’m trying to use whatever I’ve got to balance against.” Time, of course, is always the issue. Although Semanick does not always get as much as he would like, there are films he remembers where he had the time he requested: “Just on *Sweeney Todd* \[2007\] alone, I took about two weeks to predub the Foley, I think, because it was so important.” Semanick often works with directors such as Peter Jackson and David Fincher, who are careful to put more time in their budgets for postproduction sound.

Ren Klyce, who was the sound designer on numerous films including *The Girl with the Dragon Tattoo* (2011) and most recently *Mank* (2020), is artful in his sound design and likes the Foley to sound like it occurred in production, so from the Foley stage to the dub, he prefers to allow time for experimentation and finessing. Klyce is not concerned with technical perfection as much as he is with a sense of artful randomness in the sound design. Both Fincher and Klyce will negotiate to get the money necessary for postproduction sound so they have the time to get the quality they both feel the professionals, like Semanick, need to do the best sound possible for the film. However, when Klyce and Fincher performed their duties on the episodic show *House of Cards*, schedules were shorter, so the control they more often exercised on a feature was not available. Several different teams of Foley artists performed the Foley, different teams of sound editors edited and predubbed the sound effects, and the final mixes were on different stages with different mixers. Yet, Klyce still managed to have the material predubbed by the mixers, as he prefers. Recently, Klyce has added rerecording mixer to his toolkit, which allows him more control over the final mix.

Veteran television dialogue mixer Larry Benjamin relates that schedules have changed so drastically in the last few years that, while he is predubbing the dialogue on a show, his effects mixer is somewhere else predubbing the effects. Then, they will “marry up” and put in the music (Benjamin) and Foley (the effects mixer) to prepare for the final dub. Additionally, they might tweak some sync while in predub mode. Schedules are one-half the time or less than just a few years ago.

If a film has been through several temp dubs, as most of them are today, then the predub process has happened several times already. While temp dubs were more traditionally a “stand alone” endeavor, and fresh material would be designed and edited for the actual dub, today it is more typical that the temp dubs are precursors for the final dubs and sometimes take the place of predubs. A clever editor will keep the material from each previous temp dub and simply add in the new material. Cost savings, technological advances, and shorter postproduction sound schedules continue to put pressure on final mixes.

### [**The Blending of Mixing and Editing**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_7_2)

More and more often, Foley editors are required to predub in Pro Tools to save time and money on the dub. Whether the Foley mixer adds perspective on the stage, or the editor does premixing “in the box,” the dubbing mixer has to make the final product work with what they are presented. Perspective is subjective. This is a particular problem because the space from one side of the screen to the other in a dub stage is far larger than on a monitor, and the panning decisions done in Pro Tools by an editor may not translate well to the large screen. Once again, the decisions demanded by the marketplace do not always lead to the best results. However, the technology has recently responded to the need for better interfacing from editing room to dub stage. The difference between what the editor perceives when mixing and what occurs on the dub stage has been minimized, so the sessions from the editor translate better on the dub stage.

A convention that is disappearing from the dub stage is that of having an editor on the stage to tweak a cue while in the predub process. The director may want to hear an alternate sound, the sync may be suspect, or the mixers may have a suggestion to improve the combination of cues. Whatever the reason, it has always been the practice to have an editor stage-sit when their reel is up in case fixes need to be made.

More and more, because of time, budget, and technological changes, it is not always the case that an editor will be on the stage all the time. Previously, an editor was available to make editorial changes to sound while the mixers continued with the predub. Nowadays, the mixer might be asked by the director to make a quick edit. This can be problematic. Not only were most dubbing mixers more comfortable and experienced with mixing rather than editing, but the flow of the dub can be disturbed noticeably when the mixer has to change tasks and get into edit mode. There is a flow to the dub that allows maximum creativity and efficiency from the mixers. As more rerecording mixers have adapted to the ebb and flow of mixing and minor editing changes, the final dub process has been streamlined.

Now that the technology used for mixing and editing is the same, there is an assumption that anyone who has Pro Tools can do both crafts. That assumption can lead to the conclusion that one person can do the work of two, so why hire two? As we become more of a society that favors fewer workers doing more tasks with the use of technology, there is the natural assumption that a mixer can edit and an editor can mix. Here, too, there has been vast improvement in the technological response. Fleischman remarks, “Now, it’s not a big deal to go back and change sound effects or put in the Foley.”

That does not, however, lead to the conclusion that the person is talented, skilled or experienced at both crafts. Mixing and editing are very different skills and require very different gifts. While there are those who can and do perform both professions well, it is more likely that a person will have an interest in and the talent for one over the other. The view that the two crafts are close enough in skill sets to be interchangeable shows a lack of understanding of the crafts and what is required to be an expert. Another perspective to consider, however, is that the more flexible any editor or mixer can be will benefit the project, the workflow, and the economic bottom line. In these challenging times of schedules, finances, and labor consolidation, flexibility has become a premium skill.

### [**The Final Dub**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_7_3)

When the predubs are complete, the final dub begins. While the predubs are more intimate, often with only the mixers and the SSE on the dub stage, the final dub is often a cacophony of opinions. This is the last chance to get all the sounds right and all views aired. The rerecording mixers will often have the director, producer(s), picture editor, and sometimes a studio committee on the dub stage contributing feedback while counting dub stage hours and dollars. The rerecording mixers are often the only sound experts ever seen by these decision-makers in the film industry.

With the accelerated schedules and intense emphasis on technology, it is becoming ever more routine for the final dub to be a continuation of a predub or even a temp dub. The lines are blurring. This makes it harder for the mixers to keep their focus on the specific task. The final dub should ideally be the place to integrate, massage, and finesse all “food groups.” It is the finely developed and applied glaze on the well-crafted pot.

Foley is most often not the primary focus at the final dub; rather, the final dub is frequently focused on where music should be played and how the sound effects and Foley should be used in relation to the music. However, there are still opportunities to make changes in the Foley. It is not unusual for the director to decide that they want to hear something different in a scene once the music is added and the entire film is viewed.

## [**WHAT DO MIXERS SAY ABOUT FOLEY?**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec7_8)

If rerecording mixers are the final arbitrators about what best practices and approaches for Foley might be, then a visit to a dub stage to watch how mixers utilize Foley tracks would be an invaluable experience for any Foley artist or Foley mixer. Only a few are able to avail themselves of such an opportunity. With this in mind, there are some valuable insights that rerecording mixers can impart to Foley artists, be they developing their skills or veteran artists who need to freshen their perspective.

Mark Berger, who has won four Academy Awards for rerecording and mixing sound on *Apocalypse Now* (1979), *The Right Stuff* (1983), *Amadeus* (1984), and *The English Patient* (1996), encourages Foley artists to “ground the actor to the earth.” He adds that Foley “creates a sense of place.” As the dialogue rerecording mixer, he likes to dub Foley using the same settings that he uses for the dialogue in order to create the same sense of space that the character speaking is in. Berger pays a lot of attention to perspective in this manner.

Rick Sanchez, who mixes for movie trailers, wants to remind Foley artists and Foley mixers to be sure to put sync pops on the tracks. While this seems like an elementary piece of advice, he states, “They are not always there on the smaller films.” Sanchez has to work very quickly rerecording the sound for movie trailers. Working without sync pops means that he has to be a sound detective and investigate where hard sync should be by finding an impact that he can sync up to the picture. This can be a time-consuming exercise that costs him time and money in an already economically overstressed aspect of the industry.

Miguel Barbosa, who is a Foley artist, editor, and mixer in Spain, has discovered that microphone placement is very important. “A Belgian mixer told me that miking very close is not good because you don’t get air \[enough distance\].”

Finally, Tom Fleischman, who mixes in New York, imparts a simple and optimistic thought that should remind all Foley artists how valuable their work really is: “When you add Foley, it really livens up a track!”

![Figure 7.8 Tom Fleischman sitting at the console in New York. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Tom Fleischman](Images/fig7_8.jpg)

*Figure 7.8* *Tom Fleischman sitting at the console in New York.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Tom Fleischman*

# [**PART | THREE**](#content.xhtml#bck_part3)

## [**The Practical Concerns**](#content.xhtml#bck_part3)

### [The Art and Craft of Foley](#content.xhtml#bck_part3)

# [**chapter | eight**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch08)

## [**Walking in Their Shoes**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch08)

### [Performing the Footsteps](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch08)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-12](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-12)

![](Images/fig0014.jpg)

It would come as no surprise to any film fan who is aware of the role of postproduction sound that, at some point, someone is replacing the footsteps of an actor in a scene. That someone is the Foley artist. It may seem logical that the audience would need to hear the character in the scene walk. However, as we examine the actual dramatic needs in a scene, this may or may not be the case.

Let’s first look at the actual purposes of replacing or enhancing footsteps. When the Foley artist or editor is viewing the scene, many of the footsteps in the scene may be present and sound clear. There may be no reason to do anything at all to the footsteps. Perhaps the character is walking and there is no dialogue, and the scene has production sound that is clear and of high quality. If this is the case, then the Foley artist does not necessarily need to replace the footsteps. Even if the film is going to be released in a foreign market and language is going to be replaced, there is most likely no need to replace the footsteps unless there are ambient sounds from production that the dialogue editor will not be able to clean out or mask. If the production track is fairly clean and there is no dialogue, the footsteps and props from production can remain.

However, there may be dialogue while the characters are walking, footsteps from various edited characters walking on a surface that is distracting or inappropriate, scenes filmed silently (mit out sound), extraneous noise that interrupts the desired dialogue, or the need to use a foreign language over the footstep action. Now the Foley artist has to think about the proper approach to creating the footstep sounds for the character. The artist has to consider the following: is the character male, female, young, old, fast-paced, sluggish, a heavy walker, light and airy, rushed, plodding, anxious, happy, or encumbered in any way? What quality is the actor bringing to the body language and walk of the character? Is there a quirky or distinctive nature to the character’s walk? What does the way of walking tell the audience about the character? The Foley artist should not just put on a logical pair of shoes and start walking. Some artists will audition many pairs of shoes to see which pair seems the most likely match for what the character is wearing (they may not always actually see the character’s feet, and the production track may not offer much guidance) and on what surface(s) the character walks. The artist may consider the “feel” of the character when they walk. The time period and location become critical concerns for shoe choices. Contemporary surfaces are composed of different materials than those of historic times, and the artist will consider this. Thus, just the selection of shoes, if there is ample time budgeted for the film, can be a major decision for each character in each scene. Some artists keep a log of what shoe is identified with a particular character. If time is short and the artist must make decisions quickly, a brief listen to the production track is helpful, as are any spotting notes given to the artist by the supervising sound editor or Foley editor.

## [**FOLEY THEORY**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_1)

If you were to think about how Foley should be approached, what do you suppose you would theorize? Perhaps you should think of Foley as the sonic performance of the character. You are supporting the actor’s performance. Perhaps you would agree with Mark Berger, rerecording mixer, who says the Foley “grounds the actor to the earth” or that Foley “creates a sense of place.” Maybe you would like Walter Murch’s perspective on Foley: that it should not be technically perfect, should fool the listener into thinking it is really the production sound, and should sound fairly random.

Perhaps you would prefer the Hollywood hyperreality style of Foley, or maybe you would prefer the New York naturalistic style. Maybe you would like what supervising sound editor Pat Jackson calls “a huge test kitchen” approach to Foley that she had Margie O’Malley, Jennifer Myers, and Marnie Moore use for *The English Patient* (1996). Perhaps you agree with German Foley artist Niklas Matthaei, who says it is essential to “get the emotion in the steps.” You might confirm the perspective of British Foley artist Peter Burgis, who adds, “you’ve got to understand the body language” of the character. Whatever you might decide, there are many approaches artists take regarding what Foley footsteps should sound like and how they should be performed. Some believe the microphone should be very close. Others prefer the microphone to be a bit farther away. Some like to use two microphones: one for the feet or prop and one for room reflection. Some prefer to record the Foley flat with no reverb. Some use headphones while creating the sounds, while others, like Austin Foley artist Susan Fitz-Simon, find headphones an impediment to the physical experience of performing Foley: “I get too ‘interior’ with headphones on.” You can develop your own theories. You do not have to subscribe to any particular method. Foley is not something you perform according to a rubric.

This being said, there are geographical differences that affect the approach to Foley. The Foley in Hollywood tends to have a quality that is “bigger than life” compared to the New York Foley style. Marko Constanzo, who is a veteran Foley artist in New York, places his microphone at a greater distance from his props or his footstep cues than do the Hollywood Foley artists. The New York style of footsteps tends to be more naturalistic, and the preferred aesthetic is that, in most instances, they sound like production.

When the Saul Zaentz Film Center in Berkeley, California, was an active postproduction sound facility, the Foley was integrated with the edited sound effects and followed the Walter Murch or Alan Splet preference of understatement to an almost imperceptible “felt, but not heard” aesthetic. Across the bay, at Skywalker Sound, the Foley has a more “present” quality and collaborates with the aesthetics of the animated, action, and fantasy films that garner the deserved reputations of sound designers Randy Thom, Gary Rydstrom, Ben Burtt, and, more recently, Ren Klyce.

French Foley artist Julien Naudin relates that, when he works on films made for Denmark, Sweden, or Iceland, he uses very soft-soled shoes because people in those countries walk very softly and wear softer shoes. The aesthetic in that part of Europe is different. When he works on other European films, he uses leather-soled shoes because that is the aesthetic for Western Europe.

## [**METHOD FOLEY**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_2)

When a Foley artist selects shoes for a performance, she or he considers the choice of surfaces. This affects the sound of the shoes. The microphone used—as well as the distance from the microphone—affects the performance. The sonic character of the room affects the performance. The choice of a shoe for the character is not simple, but, as one gains experience, the elimination of improper shoes becomes easier.

If the character is walking on a hard surface, it is important to consider a shoe that is not too “bright”—that is, not too sharp and high-pitched in sound. Chances are that if the shoe has too much “high end” it will not blend into the dialogue track easily. If the character is walking on carpet, it might be better to avoid walking the steps at all. Carpet is hard to record properly. It has a “swish” sound to every impact, and to get enough level in the recording, you have to walk with more definition and weight. If the character is walking lightly or is in a soft shoe or socks or is barefoot, it might not be worth doing.

If the character changes surfaces during a walk, it is critical to have a plan. Are you going to try to make the changes in one take? If so, be prepared. This is not easy. You will be watching the screen, not your feet or the surfaces. If you do not know where your surfaces are and cannot walk from one to the other on the correct footstep in the picture with the correct emphasis, it is better to punch in for the steps that change surfaces. Or, perhaps, you can change tracks and walk each part separately. Your best friend, at this point, is your mixer.

If the character is heavy, that does not mean she or he walks heavily. This is a common misconception: most people would assume that a heavy person is a heavy walker. Yet Charles Durning, a rather rotund actor, had a fast-paced, clipped walking style and was light on his feet. Kathleen Turner, while having an appealing and attractive appearance, has a fast, athletic walk in many of her roles. She walks with more determination and weight than some.

How a character walks in different shoes varies. A woman in heels walks very differently from a woman in flats. Someone in soft shoes has a more casual walk than someone in hard street shoes or dressy hard-soled shoes. Bare feet are hard to portray. I want to use a personal illustration of how a seemingly difficult footstep task can actually be performed in a sensible way for the Foley artist and be useful for the dubbing mixer. In *Die Hard* (1988), the Bruce Willis character is barefoot through most of the film. I walked his character barefooted, but with more weight and emphasis than if I had shoes on. My feet would make joint-cracking sounds now and then. Lee Tinkham, my Foley mixer, had to punch in and let me replace the steps that contained the distracting and jarring “crack” sound. When the character was walking on broken glass, I did one track of straight barefooted steps, and then I added a track of glass impacts by putting glass on tile and “walked with my hands” wearing gloves. I was careful to be varied and selective in the impacts. Some Foley artists used to have a saying in Foley that illustrated the arbitrary nature of some cues: “Next they’ll want us walking on broken glass.” Life imitates art.

When you do a cue that requires bare feet, you will find it takes time to figure out the odd issues your feet have. Maybe you have high arches. Perhaps you are more flat-footed and have a heavier footfall. You can take your time to figure out if you need to walk heel-toe or even toe-heel to make it sound natural. Even the placement of the microphone can help. If the microphone is too close, your feet might sound sticky. If the microphone is too far away, you might sound too light. Be mindful of the room as well. What sounds great in one room will sound different in another. The same is true for the difference in mixers. No two elements in any Foley recording are the same.

![Figure 8.2  It’s for the schoolyard scene!](Images/fig8_2.jpg)

*Figure 8.2*  *It’s for the schoolyard scene!*

My partner on *Die Hard*, Robin Harlan, walked the footsteps for Hans Gruber, the Alan Rickman character. He was suave, international, and poised. She auditioned several pairs of shoes and then selected a gorgeous-sounding pair of shoes that had a rich and authoritative sound. Her character was the boss of the whole operation, and he needed to sound “in charge.” He did. Robin’s choice was artful and appropriate. You will find that shoes have to be tried on several surfaces and in several rooms before you really get a sense of what they can do for you. Also, you might find that in some rooms they become your worst enemy and they betray you. Shoes seem to have personalities all their own. You might also find that the very shoes that sounded great on linoleum sound terrible on concrete. Also, pick your carpet carefully. Shoes can lose their character easily on carpet.

Shoes do not necessarily look like what they sound like. This is an important aspect of shoe selection. Sometimes a director might actually show up on a Foley stage. The director might decide to “cast” the sound of the shoe according to its look. The assumption that one can discern how a shoe will record by how it looks is erroneous and uninspired. Listen to shoes and do not look at them. I remember that I used a pair of glamorous-sounding shoes for stiletto heels in *The Woman in Red* (1984). They were ugly blue shoes that were shredded yet sounded great. My favorite shoe for representing classy, elegant men was actually a woman’s patent leather oxford. I had a pair of brown shoes with flaking paint that was great for tall, heavy men in a hard shoe. If one cannot judge a book by its cover, the same is so very true of shoes. Foley artists find themselves forming a real affinity for some shoes and an aversion to others. There will be certain pairs an artist relies on with regularity and they become like family members. Others are like those cousins one has to visit now and then but really does not want to play with very often.

Some shoes pick up a squeak. A favorite pair of Foley shoes may develop unwanted noises. It might be appropriate to have a squeak for the character. If that is the case, keep that pair as is. A squeak can be eliminated, however, by taping the side of the shoe that squeaks with gaffer’s tape. Many a Foley artist has walked across a studio lot in taped shoes to get a cup of coffee. Another trick is to use Neatsfoot Oil. It softens leather and can eliminate unwanted sounds. Some Foley artists use talcum powder to lessen the creaks.

Foley artists have shoes that are specialized. They have shoes with cleats, both metal and plastic, for sports movies. They have boots that work for military films, boots for westerns, and boots with various heels.

They have shoes for police officers, fire fighters, doctors, and nurses. They have tap shoes, ballet shoes (both soft and toe), and ballroom dancing shoes. They collect shoes on a regular basis and try them out as often as possible. Good Foley shoes are an artist’s best friends, and they treasure them. When a favorite pair of shoes no longer does the job, either by wearing out or by getting that horrible squeak, Foley artists actually mourn.

Sometimes, Foley artists have to walk more than one character at once. This may seem nonsensical at first, but there is a reason for the practice and a method to the madness. When confronted with a scene with lots of background actors (BGs), an artist does not want to walk each character separately. They are not important to the scene and there is no point in wasting tracks. So, artists make sounds that cover more than one person. It is often a good idea to approach this in one of two ways. One way is that one member of the Foley team picks a generic pair of shoes that works for most of the people for one track, and the other (assuming they work in twos) does the track in another generic-sounding pair. The second approach is to have one kind of shoe on one foot and another kind on the other foot. This is tricky but can be effective. It just takes practice. Some Foley artists can make a series of sounds with their feet that are generic but work for crowds. It looks ridiculous, but in the playback it sounds reasonable. Whatever works.

Some Foley artists can utilize the various parts of the foot. This may sound a little strange, but when considering a person who has a gift for walking characters or has trained as a dancer, it makes sense. The foot has more than just a sole. There is the ball of the foot, the heel of the foot, and the two sides. The foot can be manipulated in many ways. A foot can be moved in lots of peculiar ways to obtain different sounds in a shoe or to make footsteps seem less predictable and contrived. One can walk on the insides of the foot and the outsides of the foot, and one can emphasize the ball, the heel, the flat footfall, or the heel-toe footfall. Just play with the sound. Any person who truly wants to investigate all the ways to walk a character will, at some time or other, experiment with how the foot can actually move. Be curious and inventive. What complicates all footsteps cues is one fundamental Foley technique: Foley artists cannot walk forward and travel. Typically, they must walk in place so they are positioned near the microphone. Thus, they have to learn how to make the footsteps sound natural without traveling.

Recently, I discovered that Julien Naudin, at times, has attached a small microphone to the top of his shoe to allow a sense of amplification to the footsteps that sounds hyperreal and larger than life. He has also recorded footsteps utilizing this technique while using a second boom microphone to simultaneously record in the more conventional fashion. Sometimes, he records Foley outside in the wild. He attaches a small microphone to his shoes and walks in a forest while the mixer records his footsteps. Foley artists like Naudin do not let conventions limit their possibilities. Naudin works in Europe and is trusted by the directors to experiment and be innovative.

Another Foley artist who has utilized microphones on his shoes is Richard James Hinton. For the film *Marathon Boy* (2010), he performed a stylized close-up of the boy running. Hinton clipped two lapel microphones to his laces and taped the cords up his legs.

Joan Rowe, a Foley artist since 1978, began her career in Foley at Gomillion Sound accidentally while auditioning as a voice actor. Ted Gomillion, the owner of the studio and a rerecording mixer, liked her sync and asked if she wanted to learn Foley. He accompanied her to a strange room, and they watched some woman, behind a glass window, throwing a car door around and stomping in dirt. Joan thought to herself, “Ooh, I am an artist,” and thought this strange act was beneath her. Then she remembered she had two small children to support and was cleaning houses for a living—film folks often do different jobs to support themselves between gigs—and perhaps she should rethink what she now refers to as her “arrogance.”

What Rowe had to learn about performing footsteps should help in understanding that it takes time to get good at Foley. She had a great deal of trouble learning how to get her footsteps to match those of the people on the screen. She was just beginning her career in Foley and had discovered in her first session how hard it was to perform footsteps properly. Her first day on the Foley stage, she faked her way through the day and vowed to learn how to walk before her next session, which was weeks later. Rowe had found performing prop cues to be easier. This, however, is the typical scenario for a beginning Foley artist. It is easier to relate to making sense of props and how to manipulate them for a reasonable sound than to walk in sync with someone else. Rowe practiced walking like other people on the streets when she was out. She turned on her television and practiced walking to all the characters on the show. She did this for weeks on end. When she was called back in to work, she had some sense of how to walk her characters. However, as Rowe relates it,

> I could not get my feet to walk “heel-toe.” I was just flapping around. It was hard to control my feet. John Post could manipulate his feet many different ways. He could contort his feet. I could not. So, I decided to practice and practice until I got better. Now, my footsteps are really good. But it was not easy for me.

Most Foley artists have expressed a similar frustration. It is not as alien to learn how to perform props, because at one time or another most of the ordinary prop cues one does on the stage mimic what we have done in our own lives. Creating footsteps, however, is different. To perform footsteps in sync, with an awareness of each impact of each step, and make those steps fit the action on screen are less familiar to most of us—that is, except for those who have been dancers. Susan Fitz-Simon reveals that she “practiced a couple of hours a day” to perfect her footsteps Foley. Be patient. Any art takes time to master. Learn from Fitz-Simon and Rowe. It takes tenacity, patience, and practice.

A final thought about footsteps from Robin Harlan and Sarah Monat, who work as a team at Sony Pictures: different supervising sound editors and directors have different tastes about how footsteps should be performed. An example they relate is Quentin Tarantino, who likes his Foley sharp and aggressive and likes the footsteps that way as well. Supervisor Randy Thom likes to hear the surface under the shoe (this is very much a Walter Murch aesthetic), so they are careful not to play the shoe as having too much personality with Thom. Wiley Stateman likes to have the Foley artists carry props and walk just like the characters, so if the character is carrying a golf bag while she is walking, the Foley art ist does too. However, Harlan leaves us with this: “Some people really like it vanilla.”

## [**MUST YOU DANCE?**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_3)

Jerry Trent had been a dancer in many musicals. Raised in Illinois, he moved to New York and became a Broadway and touring dancer. He then went to Los Angeles to appear in the film *The One and Only, Genuine, Original Family Band* (1968). The dancers were asked to Foley their dance footsteps in postproduction. This was Jerry’s introduction to postproduction sound. He continued to make a career as an on-screen dancer and off-screen Foley dancer until he made the transition to being a Foley artist and added the more traditional sounds to his skill set. Jerry has been an organized and disciplined artist, with Foley bags that have categories and compartments for all his tools. Many Foley artists are less organized than Jerry. Some are quite the opposite. But Jerry has taken that famous dancer’s discipline to the ultimate in his approach to his work.

Jerry relates a story about one of his experiences doing dance Foley. He was working on *The Turning Point* (1977) and was asked to do some of the ballet sounds for Mikhail Baryshnikov. The choreographer was concerned that Jerry could not dance like the gifted Russian. He was asked, “Can you do as many turns as Baryshnikov? Can you stay in the air as long as Baryshnikov?” Jerry, in his typical dry delivery, replied, “I don’t have to do the same steps, they just have to sound like the steps. I can do most of this sitting down.” The choreographer was dubious, but Jerry proved his point—what matters is what it *sounds* like. “Sometimes we put the shoes on our hands,” says Jerry. “You don’t have to know the steps. It has to sound like the dancing, not look like the dancing.”

In 1997, I was presented with a unique opportunity to perform dance sounds for animals in the animated film, *Cats Don’t Dance*. Gene Kelly had been the consultant for the choreography that the animators created utilizing cel animation. It was great fun, as I had been a professional dancer and was accomplished in tap dancing. Some of the dancing was done sitting down, some standing upright, and all of it in short takes of just parts of each dance. Dancing cues require vigilant attention to the subtle moves of each foot, but they also include the arms and hands. A single dance for a single character requires paying close attention to the most minute movement the character performs. Thus, it is not easy, fast, or obvious. The Foley artist charged with doing choreographed dance Foley needs to take the time to make each scuff, tap, slide, turn, jump, or toe shoe movement sound as authentic, yet theatrical as is warranted by the film. Each hand movement, flick of a wrist, touch of the body, or touch of an object must be included. In *White Nights* (2005) and *Tap* (1989), Gregory Hines did his own “improvitap” dance Foley on a constructed wooden stage for the purpose of recreating the best sounding tap sounds. Both Jerry Trent and I have replaced dance footsteps for Baryshnikov, as well as the easier tap steps for Hines. My work was for *White Nights*, while Trent did the Foley for *Tap*.

![](Images/fig0015.jpg)

What is essential to remember is that just because the characters are seen to be dancing does not mean Foley artists have to do the same action. They only have to sound like they are doing it. It is sonic acting.

## [**WHERE ARE THE FEET?**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_4)

One of the issues that comes up for a Foley artist performing dance footsteps or ordinary walks is where to look if the feet are not on camera. Robert Deschaine, a mixer on many films and television movies, had great advice whenever he saw a Foley artist struggle with a character walking or running in a long sequence with a lot of edits. He would tell the artist to “watch the shoulders.” That was all he said, all he would need to say. The artist could fall into rhythm, even with the difficult edits. This is the key to tricky walks. The shoulders are the clue to how the character is moving.

Often, a character’s feet are not on-screen, and the edits do not keep the character in the same rhythm. A skilled Foley artist has learned where to look to get the character’s footsteps in sync. It could be the shoulders, the head, the hands, or any other part of the body that indicates the actor’s intention. Picture editing is not focused on the sync of footsteps. Rather, its task is to support the story and the flow of the dramatic or comedic action. So, the sound experts have to concern themselves with smoothing over the cuts that do not match. This can be tricky. There are times when a character is stepping on the right foot at the end of one cut (the A side), only to be in mid-step on the next cut (the B side). One has to try two things. Either option might work. Try double stepping: step the same step twice and see if that works. Or one can let it hang over: do not make the step on the first cut and complete it on the next cut. One never knows which way works better in the rhythm of the walk until one tries it.

Sometimes actors do strange things that cannot be accurately interpreted. John Forsythe had the appearance of taking longer to get out of a car than most actors. This was hard to figure out. Jerry Trent, while working on a television show starring Forsythe, finally saw in one angle of a shot why this was so. The classy Mr. Forsythe was planting his feet on the ground and then taking the time to pull his pants legs down before he got out of the car. Once that unpredictable behavior was discovered, it became easier to anticipate the actor’s exit from the car. The moral of this story? Pay attention to the actor. They are all different with unique habits.

## [**FOOTSTEP SURFACES**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_5)

To begin with, when Foley artists walk characters on a gritty street or sidewalk, some use dirt sprinkled on the surface. Many, however, use coffee grounds, right out of the can. A staple in many artists’ bags might be a container with Folgers or some other grocery store brand of ground coffee. They sprinkle the grounds on the surface and then work it into the surface for the appropriate “grit factor.” If the street is really gritty and they want to give the impression of a dirty walkway, they might use more of the grounds or their selected type of dirt. If the walkway is cleaner, or the scene takes place in a nice part of town and they want to support the beauty of the tasteful environment established in production, they use a smaller amount of the coffee or dirt and grind it into a fine powder. Some shoes have soles that the grit clings to, while others do not. The shoe element must, therefore, also be taken into account. High heels can often be problematic for “grit cling” due to the smaller surface area on the heel.

It takes some practice to walk on a gritty surface. The tendency for the beginner will be to walk normally while watching the picture. However, the real design of Foley comes in altering the footsteps with intention so that every step does not have the exact same amount of grit. On any street or walkway, grit is uneven. Thus, the Foley walking needs to have a certain unpredictability to it as well.

When Foley artists have to walk characters in snow, there are many opinions on what makes the best kind of snow. It used to be common practice to use vermiculite, which is poisonous to the lungs. However, it is absurd to hurt one’s health for a movie. Some use rock salt. Some use shaved ice. Some use a combination that includes cornstarch. A popular technique is to use a stew of cornmeal and cornstarch, plus some dirt if the snow is dryer. If it is wet snow, the artist might use a wet cloth for one track of footsteps, and then record another track with cornstarch on top of dirt, which is on top of a cement surface. The question that must be asked is, “What kind of snow is it?” Wet? Fresh? Refrozen?

At this point, I need to illustrate with a personal story. For those who are not familiar with living in a snowy climate, they might not know how many kinds of snow sounds there are. When I worked on *Chain Reaction* (1996), I had very little experience in real snow. The movie takes place in Chicago, Wisconsin, and Washington, DC, during the winter. There were all kinds of snow on all kinds of surfaces. My Foley mixer, Karin Roulo, was from Chicago, so I relied on her guidance to inform my snow design. Karin would tell me whether it looked like fresh snow, frozen snow, icy snow, or black ice. She would give me verbal descriptions of the sound of the snow in each situation. We would carefully audition each snow scene to ensure that the squeak was appropriate to the kind of snow and that the footsteps were still defined and in character for the actor. It was a very meticulous process, but I did not want a sloppy approach to the snow sounds, because the weather and environment were key characters in the story. There is a scene where the two leads are in an iceboat on Lake Geneva, where we supplemented the edited effects. The lesson is, do not make the assumption that one sound for snow footsteps is correct for all situations. The more precise the casting of the snow, the more subtle and believable the footsteps are. The goal is always to sound like the on-screen environment.

## [**EVERYTHING OLD IS NEW AGAIN**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_6)

Before digital editing, Foley artists would work on stages that recorded using magnetic film. In the late 1970s and early 1980s, there were only three or four tracks to work with. Often, Foley artists would combine multiple characters’ footsteps or multiple prop cues on one track. In television, the Foley artists would hold a piece of cloth in their hands and move the cloth while they walked the footsteps for the characters so there was a sense of movement for the foreign version of the television show.

When 24-track tape became the norm for recording, and later when digital recording became popular, it was the convention to separate cloth out onto its own track so the rerecording mixers could play the cloth at the final dub at a low level for a sense of character movement when ADR was replacing the dialogue or when a foreign version was being mixed.

Interestingly enough, with budgets being tightened and schedules being compressed, the practice of combining footstep cues with cloth movement for television, morning cartoon shows, and low-budget movies has come back as a regular practice. This is true even for the necessary foreign versions, referred to in the trade as M&E versions (music and effects), for the less expensive shows. It is a faster and less expensive method for getting the Foley done, but balancing the sound of footsteps and cloth in these times of such sensitive technology is tricky, at best. Additionally, cueing prior to the Foley recording session is becoming a less reliable convention as well.

Conversely, another trend has continued: that of quality Foley for the long-story format. According to Harlan and Monat, who have been doing Foley for Showtime and other premium cable outlets, the long-story format has allowed the sound effects professionals to put time and money into high-quality sound for the shows. This bodes well for Foley artists, as this trend for narrative is gaining traction. Harlan relates that the schedules and workflows for these projects are similar to those in years past when time and quality were linked. The stories take time to develop, and the producers know that their viewers will be avid watchers, so they want to keep the quality high. Because the long-story format is popular, it is good news indeed to know that there is another side to the postproduction sound trends in Hollywood. Harlan and Monat were glad to discover that they would have five weeks to perform the Foley on the latest *Spider-Man* film. This length of schedule has not been typical as of late. So, while some schedules are tightening, it seems that in the last few years there has been an evolution toward quality returning to postproduction sound.

While prime time television and quality episodic shows still maintain high standards for postproduction sound practices, it is nonetheless true that the less expensive shows are returning to practices long abandoned as less desirable for quality purposes, because they are considered more desirable for the bottom line. It is important to remember that quality shows will still maintain quality postproduction values. But it might be useful to have the skill to work both the high-end methods and the less expensive methods.

![Figure 8.4  Robin Harlan and Sarah Monat at Sony’s Foley stage. ¶ Source: Photo by Brittany Nelson](Images/fig8_4.jpg)

*Figure 8.4*  *Robin Harlan and Sarah Monat at Sony’s Foley stage.* ¶ *Source: Photo by Brittany Nelson*

## [**SHOE SHOPPING**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_7)

When a Foley artist first starts gathering shoes, there are typical places to venture for purchasing and collecting. Of course, the first place to look is the closet. There are always shoes that sound great. It is important to think of various types of characters and what types of shoes they might wear. Once again, how the shoes sound is more important than how they look. Another place Foley artists frequent is thrift shops. These shops carry cheap shoes that can be amazing sonic finds. Artists also buy shoes in an actual store, either retail or discount. The fun begins when they have to audition the shoes. The sales assistant will always assume the artist is looking for a pair of shoes to go with an outfit or for a specific occasion. However, once a Foley artist asks to be allowed to go to the hard tile to listen to the shoes rather than stay on the carpet, an explanation has to follow. Many times, onlookers have assessed a Foley artist as totally nuts because he is walking on tile, bent over, with his ears as close to the floor as possible while he walks so he can hear the shoes. However, the Foley artist needs to listen to the quality of the shoe and try to discern if there are any squeaks or creaks.

## [**GENDER NEUTRAL**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_8)

In contemporary American society, the boundaries between genders are becoming more fluid. This has been the practice in Foley footsteps for several decades.
