However, in the earlier days of Foley, it was often assumed that men do the footsteps for men and women do the footsteps for the female characters. In today’s world of nonconformative gender identity, we do not make these assumptions, as any Foley artist will perform the footsteps for any character. Gender should be irrelevant to the Foley artist. However, several issues can come up when walking another gender. Walking the footsteps for a male character might entail a longer gait or a heavier footfall. The obvious assumption in the past was that a woman might not be heavy enough to walk a big guy, but that would be a misconception. Not only does size not dictate footfall weight, neither does gender. The challenge, rather, is the timing of the weight shifts. Female characters often take steps closer together, unless they are quite tall. So, the Foley artist accounts for that in the rhythm, weight, and footstep length for any character.

Some men have a problem with high heels. Most men spend their lives in flat shoes or boots. Rarely are they in situations to wear the varying types of shoes that female characters might wear. Thus, they are less used to the different heel heights and the adjustments women make to accommodate different types of shoes. Add to that the problem of foot size, and one can see why some men find it hard to locate shoes they can use for “well-heeled” female characters. There are solutions. One is to shop at a store that caters to female impersonator performers or non-binary people. This is easy to do in a city like Los Angeles, but if one is doing film work in another region or in a smaller market that produces independent films, commercials, or games, then these stores are harder to come by. However, many male Foley artists have shoes that sound like heels because of the lighter and brighter sound. They have learned how to manipulate their foot to sound like the shoe is a high heel. Many male Foley artists have conquered this issue easily. Again, gender is truly not an issue. Joanna Fang, a transgender Foley artist at Alchemy Sound in Peekskill, New York, has adapted her shoe selections by tuning her ear to the sound of the shoe and the walking style of the character.

Greg Barbanell, a veteran Foley artist, has been a big guy in the past. While at his heaviest, he could still walk any character with the delicacy and precision of the most agile dancer. His height and weight were irrelevant. This is the case for any professional in the field. Foley artists are all experienced at the sonic portrayal of all kinds of characters, and our physical build is not what is at issue. Far more important for any person who wants to walk Foley well are sync, sensitivity, precision, character intention, and artistic taste. A Foley artist can walk any gender and weight and is not limited by height, build, or gender. Nor does the artist have to be in perfect shape physically. Foley artists can be young, old, fit, flabby, and even physically challenged. Foley artists learn from each other and adapt to their own circumstances.

## [**CRITTERS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_9)

Animal footsteps are a strange hybrid. They are considered footstep cues, yet they are performed like prop cues. Foley artists have lots of tricks for doing animal feet. One is using soft slippers or ballet shoes on their feet. Depending on the surface used, the ear can be fooled. Some Foley artists use gardener’s gloves with tacks on the ends for dogs. Some women have acrylic nails and use their bare hands. However, when doing animal feet in a dirt pit, it might be best to use gloves—one never knows what is in that pit. The important thing is to be impressionistic, rather than focus on the literal movement of the animal. Once again, the picture edits might help or hinder, so it is best to go with the flow and be rhythmic but not overly concerned with precision. This being said, with experience and developed skill, a Foley artist can learn to be more precise, with the caveat that the viewer needs only to be convinced of the authenticity of the animal movement and will not be analyzing every step.

![](Images/fig0016.jpg)![Figure 8.6  Ken Dufva performs animal footsteps. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of David Lee Fein and Ken Dufva](Images/fig8_6.jpg)

*Figure 8.6*  *Ken Dufva performs animal footsteps.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of David Lee Fein and Ken Dufva*

When Foley artists perform the footsteps for cows, goats, or animals with hooves, they can use old film cores from the days of magnetic film or some such device. Most people have heard about using coconuts for horse hooves, and many still do that. Some Foley artists use them with the meat still in them, baked, and then hot-glued to the shell. Some artists have special devices made from plungers or other props. The trick to animal footsteps, however, is the rhythm and the feel of the movements. Animals have many different types of walks or runs, and it is important to bridge the cut without jolting the rhythm. The point here is that Foley artists have various techniques to approach the task of creating animal footsteps and movements. Much like the meth ods animators use for observing animals in real life to adapt their renditions in film, Foley artists can do the same kind of observation and learn the various ways animals move.

Both Richard James Hinton and Meltem Baytok have worked on natural history documentaries for the BBC. As a result, they both have an abundance of experience with animal footsteps. While they do not work together—typically the task is for one Foley artist—they work on similar projects. Baytok is originally from Turkey and learned Foley in the United Kingdom before she could speak English fluently. She relied on reading actors’ faces, emotions, and reactions to understand how to do Foley. “It’s like being deaf and reading their bodies like sign language,” says Baytok. From such an interesting perspective, Baytok enjoys performing the Foley for animals. Some of the creatures she performs footsteps and movements for are foxes, giraffes, wolves, lions, elephants, fish, kangaroos, bears, monkeys, and birds. For one documentary about animals that reproduce, she remembers that she used pineapples for crocodiles having sex in water. Baytok has been known to use cupped tennis balls as well as the old reliable coconuts for various animal footsteps, when she does not use her hands.

Contrary to Baytok, Hinton edits his Foley as well as performs it. His Foley mixers previously worked on a PC-based digital system called Pyramix—from Switzerland—but have made the transition to Pro Tools. “We had to be compatible with the rest of the world and it helps massively when working with other post houses.”

Hinton pays close attention to the microphone technique when he records. In a smaller room, he positions the microphone more closely, but when he works on a larger Foley stage, he likes more distance on the microphone. Hinton stresses that the natural history documentaries normally have bigger budgets, so he can take the time to perform the animals carefully.

When Misa Jakovljevic Foleys animals, he prefers to find appropriate surfaces outside, if at all possible. He does not use the Foley stage for animal footsteps. He likes to find locations that match what he sees on the screen. Sometimes he will perform the footsteps wild and edit them in.

![Figure 8.7  Forest and grass footsteps outside. Chris Szott watches the scene on an iPad while Isael Huard records the audio. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott and Little Hook Sound](Images/fig8_7.jpg)

*Figure 8.7*  *Forest and grass footsteps outside. Chris Szott watches the scene on an iPad while Isael Huard records the audio.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott and Little Hook Sound*

## [**MIT OUT PEOPLE**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec8_10)

The performance of footsteps for characters is a key purpose of a Foley artist. There are computer programs and machines invented to synthesize footsteps and edit them in so as to reduce the need for the live performance of a person supporting the actor’s vision sonically. There will always be someone trying to invent a machine that can replace human labor, and the world of postproduction sound is no different. Although some may hope to cut costs by substituting programmed footsteps for the custom-designed performance of a trained, artistic professional, the result will always pale by comparison. A smart Foley artist is one who knows as many techniques as possible and who is willing to change with new methods and technologies. Change is inevitable. Be a professional that can change gracefully.

# [**chapter | nine**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch09)

## [**Manipulation Techniques**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch09)

### [Performing the Props](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch09)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-13](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-13)

![](Images/fig0017.jpg)

One of the first questions that a Foley artist will be asked whether in a formal interview or a casual encounter is, “What did you use for (name of prop or event) in (name of movie)?” Many Foley artists, unless they have specific and routine props for most cues, will respond with, “I don’t know,” “I don’t remember,” or “whatever worked at the time.” The truth is the majority of Foley artists do not utilize a particular method for keeping records of props used for unusual sounds, unless it is for the purpose for using the same prop for additional cues later or for a sequel. The exception to this is if it was a preplanned set of sounds. If a prop needs to be specially designed for a particular sound in a project, the artist might document the props used and for what cues. In general, however, Foley is specific to a project and is performed in real time, with little reason to keep a written record of the prop used. The time on a Foley stage is expensive, and most artists prefer to work as quickly as possible to make best use of that cost.

## [**STORAGE AND SPACE**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec9_1)

Foley artists need a wide assortment of props to stock the Foley stage. They also need a prop room in which to store them. If they can manage some kind of storage near the stage for regularly used items, the props used less often can be stored farther away. Most major Foley stages have both kinds of storage. Smaller stages and multi-use studios modify the storage for props. Foley artists develop a sense for seeing everything in the world as a potential Foley prop and want a place to store these items. Any kind of organization system works, as long as they can find what they need when they need it.

It is hard to remember a time when there was not a car door or hood used as a Foley prop in a film. While sound effects editors will edit in door and trunk opens and closes, all of the accompanying “life” needs to be performed on the Foley stage. Some stages have a variety of sizes and styles of doors and hoods. Most artists should have at least one and more preferably two choices of car hood and door types for variations in sound quality and timbre. Car doors and hoods are used for many different sounds in addition to those related to car life, and as the artist becomes more comfortable with how things record, there are many squeaks, creaks, and reverberant sounds that can be made on a good-sounding door or hood. The handles and windows on the door will also be useful. Remember: car parts can be used for many other sounds that depart from their original purpose.

Foley artists have three main categories of prop sounds for any film, episodic show, or game. The first category is comprises the typical and predictable sounds that come up repeatedly, the second includes the sounds that accompany edited sound effects, and the third category consists of the unusual sounds that are designed particularly for a scene. It is best to address each category separately. This chapter is primarily concerned with the first and second categories. [Chapter 13](#Ch13.xhtml) is specifically dedicated to the third category.

![Figure 9.2  Various props stored at Little Hook Sound. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott](Images/fig9_2.jpg)

*Figure 9.2*  *Various props stored at Little Hook Sound.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott*

To begin with, the Foley artist needs to pay close attention to the production track when deciding what to use for performing the prop sounds. With few exceptions, the Foley will need to blend into the track and sound realistic. Because production mixers also have variations in the way they place microphones and mix the sound, which alter the clarity and timbre of the props used on the set, the Foley artist has to pay close attention to the style of the production mix as well as the style of the visual film itself. Different mics bring out different qualities of various props on a Foley stage. Foley stages have their own sonic personalities and also add a quality to the prop sounds. Additionally, some Foley mixers prefer to add perspective or some equalization to the Foley prop when it is recorded, while others mix the Foley flat or without any additional electronic treatment.

![Figure 9.3  Additional props organized in drawers at Little Hook Sound. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott](Images/fig9_3.jpg)

*Figure 9.3*  *Additional props organized in drawers at Little Hook Sound.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Chris Szott*

Some Foley artists are freelance artists who at one time or another worked with various other Foley artists. There is enough sharing of techniques that most artists use a lot of the same types of elements for the ordinary and predictable prop sounds. Many artists know each other and freely ask what is used. Some Foley artists prefer to keep their methods and techniques more protected. As is true with any art form, each individual artist has qualities that make her or him unique in the execution and approach to a prop cue other than what props are used. With this in mind, enough professional Foley artists are open to sharing tips in the spirit of openness and education. Rather than being concerned with keeping personal practices secret, Meltem Baytok, a Foley artist in Bristol, says it best: “Nobody can beat your experience.”

When water is required for a scene, whether for an ocean, lake, pond, puddle, or bathtub, once again, there are different approaches. The issue becomes getting the depth of the water believable, while avoiding the water slapping against the sides of the water vessel. If the tub is portable, the obstacle can be the plastic sound or metal “ring off” of the tub. If the tub is below floor level, one has to get into very awkward positions to get hands and arms into the tub. If the stage tub is above the ground, water can hit the sides of the tub and cause an echo. There is always a problem with water splashing onto a surface that makes a sound that is not desirable in the track. Puddles are not difficult to design for either footsteps or as an additional enhancement for any prop cue. A small amount of water spilled onto the floor in a puddle, using a wet rag, chamois, or paper towel to vary the sound, can be a realistic approach. Rain drops falling on a surface can be performed by using water falling from a plant-watering device or a similarly constructed prop. Also, a Foley artist can use fingers tapping on a wet surface or a small puddle.

## [**EVERYDAY PROPS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec9_2)

There are some pedestrian props that most Foley artists use on a daily basis. Practically every movie or television show has some kind of paper handled by an actor in a scene. It might be newspaper, copy paper, note-paper, Post-its, wrapping paper, or some other assortment of paper. Paper is not as easy to record as one might think. Each microphone and stage add a quality to paper that makes it hard to judge what to use. Once an artist is familiar with a stage and the microphone, it becomes fairly simple to manipulate paper for various kinds of cues. In the beginning, however, the point to remember is that what to use for the cue is reliant on the stage, the microphone, the kind of paper used in the scene, and the way the character is handling the paper. Busier paper cues can sound messy and lack articulation. One thing is certain: one kind of paper cannot be used for all kinds of paper. It is a good idea to have many different samples available. Foley artists learn to train their ears to hear subtle differences with paper cues.

When a new Foley artist first starts doing paper cues, the drill is often a trial-and-error approach where the artist will overdo the paper and it will have no specific definition or intention. After hearing the cue with too much noise and too little character, the artist will redo it. Finally, after some frustration, the artist realizes how little needs to be done for paper to “read.” Paper cues may look simple; however, a good paper cue takes discipline. Less is more with paper. The cue needs to be specific, with precise page turns and all paper movement clean and understated, unless it is a debris or background “paper life” cue.

Another common prop is what Foley artists might call “cop gear”: that is, the keys, handcuffs, gun, and club that police officers carry on their belts. Foley artists often use a piece of leather, perhaps a small purse or such, varying keys and handcuffs, some item for a gun, and some kind of small piece of wood if the club is obviously moving. Cop gear is a standard and predictable prop in many films. Foley artists will most likely do one track for a principal player and several tracks for background police. They hold the items in a comfortable position and manipulate the props throughout the scene. The tendency is to do too much of the keys and handcuffs because that is the most interesting and identifiable part of the prop cue. However, metal records “hot” as a rule, so underplaying the metal components and focusing on the leather adds a nicer texture to the prop. It should be said that every Foley artist uses a different ratio of metal to leather. Foley is an art, not a science. There are guidelines and conventions, but not hard and fast rules.

Typically, doors are edited into a scene from a sound effects library or region list, although there are Foley artists who actually take the time to open and shut doors on a stage. There can be a problem with this practice. There are so many types of doors in so many types of rooms. It is a far more effective use of time for the sound editor to audition doors in a library and assign them to the appropriate doors in the scenes and assume the Foley artist will deal with the door handles. Additionally, in television, the same rooms are used in every episode, so the same doors are edited in.

Foley artists typically add “chair life” to scenes. Perhaps a character sits in a wooden chair. The artist makes it creak. He or she will use a chair, board, industrial pallet, or anything else with the right creak. Leather chairs need leather squeaks. The artist might use a chair, a couch, or a purse. Rick Sanchez, a former Foley artist turned editor and mixer, gave me a chiropractor’s block, which I used on a regular basis for many leather sounds. Rocking chairs get creaks added in. Rolling chairs need to roll, and turning chairs need to turn. If it adds drama and interest, Foley artists will find a prop to do it.

Hospital and emergency vehicle stretchers or gurneys are sometimes a “prop character” in a scene. The sound of the gurney is important to relay a sense of urgency or drama to the scene. Rather than thinking of the gurney as just a metal rattling or squeaking sound with wheels, an artist might consider the drama of the scene, the importance of the character, and the surrounding activity occurring at the same time. Even a prop as predictable as a gurney can be designed to contain humor, drama, horror, or even anger, depending on the emotion and the music underscore of the scene.

The typing of a computer keyboard is often edited in, if sync is not a problem. When the keyboard is on camera for a principal, however, a Foley artist most likely does the keystrokes. This takes time and patience, as they cannot predict exactly what the actor is going to do. A mixer who can punch in with precision is very helpful. Keyboards for desktops computers, laptops, tablets, and phones all have a different sound. It is a good idea to have several from which to choose. A discerning ear can easily tell the difference. On *Die Hard* (1988), the character of Theo (Clarence Gilyard, Jr.) is a computer expert who continually types on computer keys. These keystrokes were performed on a cheap computer keyboard on the Foley stage. Although the keyboard was inexpensive, it sounded great. As a side note, the film was re-edited after most of the editors were off the show, and there was a new picture insert that did not match the typing. The lesson here? It is better to keep at least one editor to conform sound edits right up through the final dub.

## [**PROPS THAT CHALLENGE**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec9_3)

Horror films have standard props. Most cineastes know by now that Foley artists often use celery for bones crunching. While edited effects also have sounds to use for this kind of cue, Foley artists often add the celery in because it is easy, sounds great, and is easy to control for timing and dramatic effect. They often use liquid detergent on their hands for the goopy sounds, and they might add in a wet chamois or even sounds they can make with their mouths. Some Foley artists use various meat items from the butcher section for ripping flesh or crunches or such. Some prefer not to use meat. The point is that the sound of the prop can be inventive and offbeat, but it is how the artist uses these standard props to suit the scene. Several tracks of various components can be performed and played together.

Wing flaps for birds are often a Foley effect. Some use old ballet shoes that they hit lightly against their hands. Some use feather dusters. Some use both, with one artist doing one and one doing the other, or they use multiple tracks. Some use mouth sounds.

Glass breaks are sometimes done on a stage and sometimes edited in. A typical approach is to edit in the sound and enhance it on the stage. In *Die Hard*, there is a scene where gunshots hit the glass windows in the high-rise building of Nakatomi Plaza and the windows shatter. The glass breaks were edited in. The glass shards, however, were made on the Foley stage. Robin Harlan and I worked together to create the Foley for the shards. Robin held large panes of glass while I hit the panes with a baseball bat. The microphone was aimed at the shards as they hit the floor. Lee Tinkham, our Foley mixer, had to be mindful to set the volume to a level where the shards recorded without distortion. We wore eye goggles and gloves and were very careful to get out of the way of the impacts. This kind of cue is intrinsically dangerous and requires lots of protection and experience to be secure in the practice.

Debris is a typical Foley effect. Explosions, violent encounters, rock slides and many other events have debris that falls. Foley artists use dirt, rocks, twigs, coins, dice, or anything they can find that works best for the cue. Usually, several tracks are required. Some tracks will have heavier elements, and others will have lighter or brighter sounding elements. It is always important to consider what the elements should land upon. The wrong prop falling on the wrong surface betrays the sound. Once again, it is not what it looks like, but what it sounds like that matters.

Foliage is a prop that is routine in Foley. However, the items used can vary. The tendency now is to try to get fresh foliage. Foley artists will cut branches off of nearby on-site trees and plants, buy flowers and plants, and bring foliage from their own yards. Sometimes, in a pinch, they “supplement” with quarter-inch tape. This practice comes from the early days of Foley when microphones were not as sensitive and analog recording was not so discerning. If they use quarter-inch tape—assuming they can locate it—they use it sparingly. Artists might choose to use the old recording tape for comedic purposes.

Foley artists enhance the sounds of military equipment that characters wear. The backpacks have assorted sundry accessories with emphasized movement of specific elements. Artists have a variety of backpacks, bags, and canvas items on the stage or in their own personal prop collections, but there are times when they must rent the real items from prop companies. It takes time to get the right textures in the sounds. There is an abundance of choices in packs, guns, and other equipment, and all props have particular sound qualities.

The loading of shells and cocking of guns are usually edited in for expediency and specificity. Sound effect libraries have literally thousands of gun sounds. It can be an inefficient use of stage time to perform them, but there have been many films where the artists have been requested to do them either due to a shortage of editing time or a lack of library effects, or because the artist wanted to give the editors and mixers some choices. Again, there are no hard and fast rules, just personal preferences. What really does need to be done is all the handling of guns by the characters.

Guns rattle in films, although in real life, “rattly” guns would be very poorly maintained and dangerous. In film, however, the drama that is added by having a bit of “gun life” can be theatrically tasteful. The trick is to do just a little. Foley artists try to avoid too much rattle because, when played back, it sounds comedic. Some artists use real guns (they plug them for safety), and some have various props for particular gun sounds. Dead bolts, door handles, starting pistols, office staplers, and wrenches have all been gun life at one time or another.

When a film has a gambling scene, there are cards, dice, chips, coins, paper money, felt tables and, in short, lots of activity. These types of scenes can be grueling. There is a lot of detail, and the on-camera action cannot be faked because it is usually the story point of the scene, so the audience is probably going to notice it. Foley artists have to split out the principal on-camera gambling action and be very precise with each card movement, each chip spill, and each dice roll. They then do general background tracks for the atmosphere that they see on camera. Sound editors cut in more background sounds to add another layer.

![](Images/fig0018.jpg)

Roller skates, skateboards, baseballs, bats, golf clubs, tennis racquets, and any sports equipment cues are sweetened in Foley. The artists use the correct props, usually, but if they are not available, the artists consider the sonic elements in the prop and find reasonable replacements. Face slaps, hand claps, caresses, back pats, and even sexual encounters are sweetened in Foley. Brushing hair, brushing teeth, brushing a horse, or any kind of brushing prop is faked with whatever “sells” for a brush and the item being brushed. They do not have horses on the stage, but they may have wigs or furry coats. The more an artist is confronted with a prop, the more the artist has collected a variety of ideas on how to perform it.

At some point in many films and television shows, Foley artists will be asked to do body falls. Normally, a film with a decent budget and time will cut in a hard impact of some sort and rely on the artists to enhance or sweeten the stage. Most Foley artists have some variation of a technique that puts the artist on the floor with cloth, throwing, in a controlled manner, their torso and arms on the ground using the cloth for mass, with the focus being on the character of the particular fall. Some Foley artists actually throw their bodies down on a floor in some fashion. This might not be the safest method, nor does it allow the most control over the sound, feel, and weight of the body landing. Some artists use some kind of contraption they can throw on the ground to mimic a body landing. Again, the actual need from the artist is the particular movement of the character’s fall in the scene. There is an important lesson here: the artist is manipulating a prop or movement to *sound* like the action. Being literal is not necessary, nor desirable.

Jerry Trent recalls that in *Funny Lady* (1975) the close-up of Barbra Streisand for the song “Great Day” showed her clapping out of sync with the song. Trent was supposed to Foley the claps. He followed picture, and he was asked to “fix it” and put it in sync with the music. “How?” he asked, “It’s a close-up. I can’t change the picture.” This is an illustration of a common problem Foley artists have with props. They are asked to fix something that will result in drawing attention to the lack of continuity, when their job is to do the opposite. The solution is always to perform the prop in the best way that covers the visual error. The artist might need to skip one impact, add an impact, or change the quality of the sound entirely. It bears repeating: no rules, just conventions. Artists do what works.

## [**LISTEN, DON’T LOOK!**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec9_4)

At one time or another, a Foley artist might have to deal with an authority figure’s input about how to perform a prop cue. One solution that many artists have used is to do the props behind a baffle on the stage so the person cannot see what is being used. The reason for this is simple. Typically, the person thinks that the appearance of the prop has a relationship to the sound. The Foley artist has thought carefully about how to create the sound and has a plan. So, by occluding the vision of the onlooker, the artist can proceed to perform the prop and ask how it sounded. The problem can then become the onlooker asking, “What did you use?” as though it really matters. Some artists will use the exact same thing that was objected to earlier by pretending to try another idea, but just hiding the prop. Austin Foley artist Susan Fitz-Simon speaks for most artists when she comments, “you can’t tell someone how to use the prop.” Performance is personal, specific, and an art. Regard less of how another might assume the cue should be performed, no two sound professionals see or hear a cue in the same way.

![Figure 9.5 Rick Owens demonstrating putting two props together to alter sound. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Rick Owens](Images/fig9_5.jpg)

*Figure 9.5* *Rick Owens demonstrating putting two props together to alter sound.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Rick Owens*

Alyson Dee Moore tells a story where she had been using a can opener for a gun cue—a reasonable choice. The mixer at the time, who is now an excellent Foley artist, insisted she use something different. He did not believe the prop would work. After several attempts to make the sound work using props he insisted she use, Moore went behind a baffle and performed the cue. The mixer said with enthusiasm, “That was great! What did you use?” Moore, quite amused, answered, “What do you think? My can opener.” The baffle shields all from the critical eye and lets the critical ear be the judge. It is not always the tool, but how you use it. Moore says to all budding Foley artists, “Whatever you think is going to work, try it. You just never know what is going to work.” Remember: Foley is an art. Artists have to be bold and trust their it safe, they may never find the most artful and creative sound.

Robert Mott described what he calls the “watching directors.” In the days of making live sound effects for television news, some of the directors would watch him work and be concerned about what he was using. “They used to listen with their eyes,” Mott remarked with an impish smile. The lesson is simple: watch the film and listen to the prop. Do not watch the prop. It affects one’s perception.

## [**CLOTH**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec9_5)

![Figure 9.6 Walter Spencer performs a cloth cue while Mike Horton looks on. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Walter Spencer, Hi Fi Foley](Images/fig9_6.jpg)

*Figure 9.6* *Walter Spencer performs a cloth cue while Mike Horton looks on.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Walter Spencer, Hi Fi Foley*

Foley artists enhance the clothes that the characters are wearing. Typically, there is one pass, at the end of the recorded tracks, that is a dedicated cloth pass. An artist has a variety of fabrics with which to work. The primary cloth pass coordinates with the dialogue of the characters and is mixed through the center speaker at the final dub. However, if there is a specialty cloth, such as a leather jacket or wedding dress, that cloth will be treated as a separate prop and will be assigned to a different track in the session. This being said, some artists prefer to separate out various tracks for cloth for important characters, as Pakistani artist Kashif Ejaz does. The danger with this practice is that it can create too much “whooshing” noise on the Foley tracks that will need to be eliminated in the final mix. It is always a good practice to be judicious and mindful about cloth noise, both performed and from one’s own clothing.

Robert Rutledge is credited by some in the Hollywood community with the innovation of the single cloth pass. Up until the early 1980s, it was customary to have cloth that a Foley artist would carry in your hands and move while walking a character. This is what Jack Foley did. Bob had affected other artists with this “new” way of utilizing cloth. My own introduction to Rutledge included a correction in my use of cloth. As I worked with him on a film, he watched me holding a piece of cloth while performing a footstep cue, and then he showed me how to use one piece of cloth on a separate track to follow the general movement of the characters as they walked or made bold gestures. He explained to me that it would fit in with the dialogue. Rutledge, an experienced sound editor as well as gifted Foley artist, had a better perspective than I had on how cloth mixed in with dialogue.

## [**PROP CHARACTER**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec9_6)

Actors handle props differently. Depending on the training of the actor and the character portrayed, along with the conventions of the time period in the film and the style of the genre, there can be quite a few variations in how to do the props the actors handle. In a comedy, the props sometimes are supposed to get a laugh. In *Young Frankenstein* (1974), the candles in the famous “put the candle back” scene sound unrealistic and heightened for broad comedy. In *Glory* (1989), when the young soldier is asked to load and shoot his gun over and over, it is clean, loud, and dramatic. In *Malice* (1993), when the Anne Bancroft character is pouring and drinking her single malt scotch, it is not just a drink. It is her best friend, and she handles it accordingly. The attentive Foley artist will take into account all of the character and dramatic purpose of the prop and not get into a rote performance.

Most Foley artists have an affinity for performing props before they are comfortable with footsteps. Both David Fein and Ken Dufva found props to be easier in the beginning. “I think Ken has an instinctual creativity about what to use. It probably started to feel natural pretty quickly,” says Fein, “Our approaches are pretty similar.” Jody Thomas prefers to do props for animation. “It keeps me on my toes,” she says. “I love figuring it out!” Props are personal for artists, and their particular methods for performing them may vary profoundly yet be equally effective. As Ronni Brown, Foley artist at Skywalker Sound remarks, “how would I take it apart? How would I put it together?”

# [**chapter | ten**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch10)

## [**Hang It as a Unit**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch10)

### [The Knack for Sync](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch10)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-14](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-14)

![](Images/fig0019.jpg)

The quality that causes the most anxiety for the beginning Foley artist is almost invariably how to walk a footstep cue or do a prop cue and be in sync with the action on screen. The ability to match the footfall of the character or the action of the prop on screen is enough to cause any beginning Foley artist some trauma. Even the most experienced and talented Foley artists whose names appear on screen for all of the major films have had this anxiety.

The first time I entered a Foley stage was an unforgettable and strange experience. The year was 1980. The world had just discovered who had shot JR Ewing on the television show *Dallas*. I walked into this dark and cluttered room where Duane Hensel, who was a veteran Foley artist and sound editor and who had worked on all the Universal television shows of the 1970s, was on the stage waiting for his new trainee. In those days, all Foley artists were called “walkers” because the majority of the craft focused on replacing the footsteps of characters, with props cues taking a lesser priority. He proceeded to guide me through some of my first Foley cues. One of my cues that day was to drop a remote for an explosive on the ground for the latest episode of the Ewing clan. I do not know if my work was good, awful, or what, but I can say that I felt I was in over my head. I was nervous, timid, and enthralled. I was actually working on a television show for money doing this strange new craft. By the end of the day, I was exhausted from the excitement and stress. I was pretty sure I would never hear from these people again. But I did hear from them again, and, thus, my temporary gig turned into a permanent career.

## [**PATIENCE AND PRACTICE ARE YOUR FRIENDS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_1)

Why do I relate this story? Because the pressure any beginning Foley artist will feel trying to do Foley might be as terrifying as it was for me. I got better at it and so will anyone who persists. Patience and practice are our friends. Good Foley sync takes both, as do most skills in life.

When I first worked on television shows, Foley artists were working with simple and primitive technology compared to what is used now. Normally, there were three tracks on which to record. This meant that all the footsteps, the props, and the cloth were often combined. Foley artists worked with partners, and the two would usually walk two characters or more at one time on one track. The projectors used did not have high speed, so all of the Foley artists in this era, from the 1970s into the mid-1980s, had to work in real time and then rewind to the beginning of the reel, which was a 10-minute reel, also in real time. Thus, work procedures and even coffee breaks were dictated by the rewind times.

In my early days as a Foley artist, my Foley partner (sometimes Duane Hensel, sometimes John Post, and, most often as time went on, Jim Bailey) and I would pick characters and walk them simultaneously. We would usually do two tracks of footsteps and one track of whatever props we could manage that were not going to be edited into the sound effects tracks. These cues took up all three tracks. Out of necessity, the cloth movement was added in with the footsteps tracks. We would hold a piece of cloth, a pair of softened jeans or some similar fabric, and move in sync to our footsteps. It was a simple technique and, by today’s standards, rather crude. As time went on, we recorded to the three tracks (called full coat) and one additional track (single stripe) for a grand total of four tracks of sound. We could separate out a character now and then or record more props. It was during this addition of the fourth magnetic stripe of audio that the practice of a single cloth pass began.

## [**WHEN SYNC WAS KING**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_2)

In television, the time was, and still is, shorter from turnover to dub than for the typical film schedule. The shows air weekly and there is not much time to cue or edit. There were many instances in the 1980s when Foley artists would get a show the night before it aired and have a few hours to record the Foley. There would be virtually no time to edit the sync. The sound editors would always hope that the Foley sync would be tight enough to require minimal editing. Also, as two characters’ footsteps might be recorded on one track, editing into the track meant affecting the Foley performance of both characters. If one artist had better sync than another, the editing would adversely affect both. Thus, it was better to let the viewer’s eye default to a natural impressionism that would save the editor the headache of fixing the sync. Even today, when characters can be separated easily because of all the tracks available in digital recording and editing, one can still spot Foley that has not been edited because of the time restraints in television production.

When Foley artists have trained under these conditions, their ability to walk in excellent sync becomes a premium skill. The ability to perform with such precision is more important than when there is ample time to edit the Foley and finesse it. Back in those days, the ultimate compliment the supervising editor could give would be to say, “The sync is so good, we can hang it as a unit.” This meant that no editing would be necessary, and the staff sound editors could focus their time on editing the “hard” effects that were required in the show. Because there was also limited time to pull library effects to be edited, and most editors did that on their own, a Foley artist’s ability to walk in good sync was greatly valued.

![Figure 10.2  Miguel Barbosa syncing up his grass footsteps in Santiago de Compostela, Galicia, Spain. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Miguel Barbosa](Images/fig10_2.jpg)

*Figure 10.2*  *Miguel Barbosa syncing up his grass footsteps in Santiago de Compostela, Galicia, Spain.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Miguel Barbosa*

In feature films, the emphasis is different. It becomes more important to have style and finesse in the Foley, because it is assumed that a Foley editor will tighten or improve the sync if it is not sufficient. With the luxury of the time afforded to feature films, it is more desirable for the Foley to have creativity and taste. Sync can be edited in more easily than Foley can be performed and recorded. There are Foley artists who have spent a great deal of time working on features and have not had to have their Foley “hung.”

## [**LANDING GOOD SYNC WILL PAY DIVIDENDS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_3)

The sync of those who began their Foley careers in television is more reliably accurate. However, once these artists have been in Foley for several years, they have the luxury of thinking of the feel of the cue more than the sync. When a Foley artist has experience in both television and film, a lovely symbiosis ensues. The artist is able to “land the cue” quite easily with effortless sync and yet put the emphasis on the art of the cue. This is not to say that those trained only in film lack good sync. There are Foley artists who have never walked a frame of film for television, yet who have wonderful sync. Nor is it true that every television-trained artist has amazing sync. But those who have trained in the trenches of television are used to getting good sync, usually in the first or second take, because there was insufficient time to redo a cue and then wait for that unendurable rewind time. As Foley schedules have been shortened and demands increased, the more an artist is able to do, the more that is expected. This is the nature of how any art or craft works. In these challenging economic times, landing good sync pays dividends that are not necessarily concrete, but always valuable. The best way to get any sync issues worked out is with practice. Budding Foley artists can practice while watching television shows, or even while they are streaming films and episodic series. In these days of technological convergence, there is rarely a time when one can get away from a projected image on an elec tronic device. Some Foley artists confess to practicing by mimicking what others do in public.

## [**THE “FEEL” IS PERFORMED, NOT EDITED**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_4)

With the advent of digital recording and editing, the importance of sync has changed considerably. It is possible for the Foley mixer to tighten the sync of an artist’s performance immediately within the recording session. David Jobe, a veteran Foley mixer, will sometimes improve a minor sync issue by moving a footstep or prop slightly in the recorded track editorially. Although editing is usually not the responsibility of the mixer, sometimes it only takes a quick nudge on Pro Tools to shape the cue and let the editor focus on the more specific and difficult editing. If a mixer has the ability, the tools, and the desire, this can be a quick fix.

However, not all mixers prefer to edit on a Foley stage. When Karin Roulo would mix Foley, she preferred to leave the editing to the editor. Her perspective was that she had her area of expertise and responsibility and preferred not to blur the lines. I hasten to add, however, that while Roulo’s purist attitude is shared by other mixers, it is becoming the minority opinion, as more projects require mixers and editors to cross over and work a bit in each other’s sandboxes. Economics will often trump traditional boundaries, especially in hard economic times.

![Figure 10.3  Aleyna Seymen focuses on her footstep sync. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Aleyna Seymen](Images/fig10_3.jpg)

*Figure 10.3*  *Aleyna Seymen focuses on her footstep sync.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Aleyna Seymen*

In the world of independent filmmaking, games, and film schools, the salient point is that the Foley artist does not have to have impeccable sync. However, the better the sync, the more the artist can focus on the aesthetics and style of the performance.

## [**PERFORMANCE IS BASED ON THE CHARACTER**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_5)

When a Foley artist is walking a footstep cue, the performance is based on the character established by the actor and the flow of action created by the picture editor. The Foley artist is changing surfaces, bridging awkward cuts, camouflaging changes in walking stride, and attending to the perspective of the character within the scene. At the same time, the artist is hitting the best possible sync so the least amount of editing will be required. One might wonder why the sync cannot just be cut in so the artist can let that part of the task go. The answer lies in remembering why Foley was developed as a craft. Footsteps do not have an impact that can be measured. Each actor has a different way of walking. The artist reproduces this performance sonically. As Foley artist Chris Szott adds, “there has to be emotion behind it.”

This brings up an interesting issue—that of the digitization of Foley. Will there be a time when Foley is simply edited in? Will Foley artists become unnecessary? Because Foley is a performance and is particular to each film, each character, and each story world, the personal quality of each cue would dictate that Foley artists would still be indispensable. Yet it makes sense that some filmmakers will forgo the Foley artist and try to cut in the effects and footsteps to save money, even if quality is sacrificed. One practical skill that an experienced Foley artist learns is how to fool the eye when a picture edit—for many possible and excellent reasons—leaves a character stepping twice on the same foot. The skill required for a Foley artist to perform a character’s walk so the gait is matched yet appears smooth from one side of the edit to the other side requires decision-making that is specific to the scene and edit. This decision is a human action that exudes artfulness and craftsmanship. Algorithms cannot replace the human component so essential to the craft.

Foley artists use specific techniques to improve sync when performing a cue. When walking a character, watch the cue once. Notice the overall nature of the walk. Is it even and deliberate? Plodding? Mercurial? Every actor has certain noticeable traits when walking, regardless of the film. Now watch the cue again. How does the actor’s body move? Do the shoulders move a lot? Does the head bob up and down? What about the arms swinging? Actors give many cues that help the Foley artist move with the actor.

Often it helps to watch each specific footstep, but sometimes doing this can slow the Foley artist down and the sync can be slightly behind footstep impact. There is usually a rhythm to each walk, except when the transitions occur. The shift of weight, the change in direction, and a sudden distraction can all cause the rhythm to shift.

It is fairly typical for a neophyte Foley artist to walk with a flat footfall. Plop, plop, plop is what it will sound like. Then there is the tendency to over articulate. Too much heel-toe will sound like ta-da, ta-da, ta-da. Neither sounds natural. A character will have some heel-toe footfalls, some plops, some scuffs, and some minor weight shifts. If an artist can master these subtleties, it will help with sync.

Sync is often easier when performing prop cues. Many prop sounds have a specific impact. A book being set down, for instance, is easier to do in sync. There is a precise moment when the book connects with the surface. That is the moment to focus on. An oddity that occurs in Foley is the pickup of a prop. Often the Foley artist will cheat the sound of picking up a glass, book, or some such item. Although in real life we do not always hear the item being picked up, in Foley the artist will enhance that sound to add drama to the prop. The trick is to perform the pickup without too much scrape or swipe, lest it sound artificial and draw too much attention.

## [**THE AUDIENCE IS WATCHING THE FILM IN REAL TIME**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_6)

Then there is the place when the prop has an abundance of activity—gun movement or saddle and bridle for a horse, among other possibilities. There is normally a place in the event where the eye and ear meet in harmony and it looks and feels correct. This is the part of the prop to focus on. I call this the “sweet spot,” the term used in sports to describe that perfect place where the ball meets the racquet or bat and just sails away effortlessly.

If, however, the prop is amorphous, like papers moving or leaves fluttering, then a Foley artist will let a natural sense of composition guide the performance. The audience is watching the film in real time. Whatever the audience will catch, the artist should catch. With more practice with these types of cues, Foley artists will automatically see more specific events in every cue.

## [**PERCEPTION IS REALITY**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec10_7)

While sync is critical, the performance of the character is crucial. The feel of the character will actually help with sync. If the Foley performance is truly connected to the actor’s actions, the sync will be good most of the time. However, it really takes a lot of experience performing Foley before sync becomes second nature to the artist. Once sync is more comfortable, the emphasis shifts to performance, feel, and style.

To achieve accurate sync in animation or games is more of a challenge. Whereas a live-action film has a predictable rhythm to it, animation and games do not. The timing is artificial and created for effect. It is hard to anticipate the clothes jumping out of a dresser in *Beauty and the Beast* (1991) or the various permutations of the characters’ actions in games. It takes time and experience to master the peculiar timing issues that arise in computer-generated and interactive formats. Additionally, when one is performing a similar action in a game with only a slight change, it is essential to make the difference the important part of the performance. That is what the player’s choice will evoke. Every choice the player can make requires a slightly different performance to emphasize the game element.

Often, when someone is new to performing Foley, it is hard for that artist to tell when the sync is close enough. Approximate sync can seem just fine. Usually, if a cue is one frame late, only the very trained eye can perceive it. Many can perceive it if a cue is two frames late, and if a Hang It as a Unit cue is three frames late it is obvious to most viewers. Early sync is not so forgiving. Foley that is one frame early “feels” funny. Foley that is two frames early is noticeable to most. Thus, when one is performing a cue, one frame late is not a tragedy. There is some forgiveness in the sync, unless one is “topping production”—that is, layering the Foley on top of the sound already on the production track.

Most experienced Foley artists have had to work on many projects to get good sync. It is a goal worth striving for. It takes time, patience, and a critical eye. In the meantime, the emphasis should be on the feel, character, and rhythm of the performance. Even the best in the craft bless the editors and mixers who aid in their efforts.

# [**chapter | eleven**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch11)

## [**Adaptive Sound**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch11)

### [Sound in Animation, Games, Radio Plays, and Movie Trailers](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch11)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-15](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-15)

![](Images/fig0020.jpg)

## [**ANIMATION STATION**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1)

Foley has become an important part of animation. Originally, animated films had very little sound edited in or used music cues to indicate the sound. In early Warner Brothers’ cartoons, orchestrated music cues were used to telegraph a character’s entrance or mischief. Witness Bugs Bunny as he creates havoc with Elmer Fudd. Pizzicato violins may indicate the rabbit sneakily walking up to Fudd.

Today, animated films and television shows are filled with sound. Edited effects and Foley often dominate the soundtrack. In animated musicals, there are few moments when the audience is not hearing music, effects, or Foley. The soundtracks are lush and creative. Yet there are still some films that stand out as excellent examples of how Foley can be genre-specific.

In *The Incredibles* (2004), we watch as several superheroes attempt to live ordinary lives. The use of sound effects and Foley supports this premise. In one scene, our hero is in his office cubicle explaining the policies of his insurance company while pretending to deny a woman’s claim. We hear the light background sounds of the office: papers, pencils, chairs, and hands on tables. The props and footsteps are very realistic to match the animation. Sound and picture support a live-action view of the world. No magical or playful sounds are used. The world, sonically, is ordinary.

In *The Lion King* (1994), as we watch the hyenas and Scar banter back and forth, we notice that all of the footsteps and body movements are evident. However, as the song “Be Prepared” begins, the focus of the sound, including Foley, changes. We are now attending to the song, as is the Foley. Instead of hearing every footstep, we hear only the prominent ones. We hear the marching hyenas and a few of Scar’s more obvious footsteps. We hear the bones of dead animals, played heavily and brightly over the music. We hear only the loud body hits and falls. The song is the star, not the Foley. When the song finishes, the sound effects and Foley become, once again, more prominent. The sound effects, Foley, and final mixing choices are altered to accommodate the music.

*The Triplets of Belleville* (2003) is a wonderfully creative film with an offbeat story, unusual characters, inventive animation, and a soundtrack that supports writer-director Sylvain Chomet’s vision. The Foley is sparse, as is the animation style. There is almost no dialogue in the entire film, so the story relies totally on the animation and the sound.

A young boy lives with his grandmother and becomes a bicyclist. The grandmother has a clubfoot. In one sequence of scenes, we watch the young boy open a box and take out his new puppy. The box is cardboard and has paper in it. The prop work is underplayed and artful, yet it is not overly precise. The dog footsteps are prominent as we discover that the dog’s main goal is to bark at the train. He travels up and down the stairs several times a day to give the riders their daily salutation. The grandmother, while making the boy’s bed, discovers a scrapbook showing his passion for bicycles. She buys him a tricycle, which causes him to clap his hands with glee.

As the sequence progresses, we see the passing of time as the dog, now aged, goes up the stairs to bark at the metro, but more slowly now. We watch the grandmother following her grandson, who is now grown up, as he trains on his bike. She spurs him on with her whistle. After the ride, he flops onto the dining table while his grandmother uses ordinary household items—a vacuum cleaner, eggbeater, and lawnmower—to relax and sooth his muscles.

The film has a charm unlike other animated films. The style is indicative, rather than detailed and lush, and the characters have only the most important and exaggerated characteristics etched on their faces and bodies. We are voyeurs, watching and listening to them proceed through their lives. The Foley is never obtrusive or lifelike. It is like the animation—illustrative and whimsical.

It is essential to mention *WALL-E* (2008). Not only is the film special for its wonderful vocals and sound design by Ben Burtt, but the special inventive Foley for the futuristic dystopian yet lighthearted animated feature, created by Foley artists Dennie Thorpe and Janna Vance, is masterful in its execution.

Today’s animated films are completed in postproduction sound much the way live-action films are. The sound effects and Foley are added in after most of the animation is completed or at least sketched in. This, however, was not always the case. In the early days of sound effects in animated films, the sounds—and there were very few—were recorded like the voices: before the animation was drawn. The animators had to draw the characters and movement synchronized to the voices and the sound effects. A crewmember, called the track reader, was responsible for analyzing, frame by frame, where the sounds would be located. The animators relied on the track reader and drew in the specific mouth movements to match the voices and the action to match the sound effects. Before sound effects were part of the soundscape in animation, music was the aural accompaniment in cartoons. The use of various stingers, drum hits, and other expressive orchestration emphasized the action in the narrative. The term “Mickey Mousing” came to mean the music literally mimicked the action on screen. In film scoring, composers like Max Steiner would use this device to parrot the action on screen in early sound films.

### [**Historical Tracings of Animation Foley**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1_1)

Once sound effects were recorded for use in animated films, many of the witty and inventive sounds we have come to identify in animation hail from Jimmy Macdonald, who worked at Disney Studios. Macdonald, the voice of Mickey Mouse after Walt Disney and a sound expert at Disney, altered our expectations of sound effects in animation. Macdonald had been a drummer, and he was also an engineer. Because of his unusual background, he was often called on to create his own sound “gadgets,” which later became revered props in the Disney prop room. They were invented by a man whose whimsy and imagination, and whose skill as a musician, engineer, and voice actor, contributed to his playful and rhythmic perspective on sound. Macdonald—originally from Scotland—constructed over 200 contraptions, all nicely categorized alphabetically in a card catalogue.

![Figure 11.2 Illustration of one of Jimmy Macdonald’s gadgets. This is the “marching men,” intended to recreate many cartoon characters walking together by manipulating the device.](Images/fig11_2.jpg)

*Figure 11.2* *Illustration of one of Jimmy Macdonald’s gadgets. This is the “marching men,” intended to recreate many cartoon characters walking together by manipulating the device.*

Although he was not specifically a Foley artist, Macdonald did do some of what Foley artists do. He used specially made props for specific sounds for animation. The conventions he set with this special gift of his changed the way Foley artists approached animation Foley forever. Instead of the reliance on music for sound effects, as had been the tradition in theatrical cartoons for many years, the audience was becoming accustomed to special “bings and boops” and many other indescribable sounds that present-day artists create as well. The irony is that, after Macdonald retired and there was yet another regime change at Disney, many of his props were thrown in the dumpster. The old prop room was being cleaned out for rebuilding, and those who were doing the dumping did not know what they had. Fortunately, Macdonald found out about this from his colleague Joe Herrington and actually retrieved many of these props from the dumpster. Presently, Herrington is the official curator for Jimmy’s inventions.

Treg Brown was to Warner Brothers what Jimmy Macdonald was to Disney. The animated sound effects, so familiar to audiences who are fans of Warner Brothers cartoons, are the legacy of Treg Brown. Brown was a musician who had played with, among other bandleaders, Red Nichols. During the Depression, he worked at Paramount as a music editor on features for Cecil B. DeMille. Eventually, Brown went to Warner Brothers and worked with Carl Stalling on the Warner Brothers cartoons. While Stalling composed music with sound effects sensibilities, Brown, as a musician, added sound effects with his musical sensibility. The Warner Brothers cartoon sound effects style—famous for its musi cality and humor—was due to the collaboration of Brown and Stalling. Both men had the unique ability to anticipate when the other would contribute the aural gag.

What is really essential to understand about Macdonald and Brown is that the sound effects we have come to expect to hear in animated films hail back to inventive imaginations from the early days of animation. The conventions, styles, and even the timing of animation and television cartoons are historically linked to the first animated shorts, which were accompanied by music and a few well-placed sound effects that were added in for specific dramatic and comedic purposes.

Carl Stalling is considered the first cartoon composer to include music puns in his music for cartoons. He is one of the three composers, along with film composer Max Steiner and MGM cartoon composer Scott Bradley, credited with inventing the click track, which allowed musicians to play a musical score to a film not yet completed. Stalling utilized instrumentation to mimic sound effects for his cartoons and orchestrated lush arrangements for his musicians.

In 1993, composer Richard Stone carried the Warner Brothers animation baton with his inventive music in *Animaniacs*, *Taz-Mania*, *The Sylvester and Tweety Mysteries*, and *Tiny Toon Adventures*, as well as *Pinky and the Brain*. Stone’s introduction to film music was as a music editor, who edited film scores for such films as *Witness* (1985) and *Platoon* (1986), after completing his bachelor’s degree studying cello at Indiana University in Bloomington, Indiana. He continued the Warner Brothers house style of humor and complicated orchestrations for his cartoon compositions. Even with the addition of sound effects and Foley in animated films and cartoon shows, music still accompanies the action to highlight various story points.

### [**Who Walks Like That, Really?**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1_2)

The first issue that a Foley artist must engage with is that animation is a story world unto itself. Forget about anything about physics. Animators play with science and art like no one else would imagine. The characters in animation walk like no one walks. The humans in an animated feature will possess enough gravity to make them credible, but the purpose of animation is to take the audience members somewhere they have never been. The suspension of disbelief is taken as far as possible.

Animals in animation are anthropomorphic. This means they possess human qualities while remaining animal-like. Animators study animals carefully to watch how they move and how they respond. But they also work with the narrative to ensure that the animal creatures maintain relatability. They often talk and display empathy and comprehension. A Foley artist has to move like the animated animal animated animal responds in the story world.

The footsteps of animated characters may or may not follow normal patterns. The viewers may see left, right, left, right. But then again, they may not. The reason for this is that animated characters are moving to serve the story, humor, and relationships. Also, the timing of animation is not the timing of live action. Rabbits may hop slower, then faster, to accommodate dialogue. A donkey may walk with a humorous gait to add personality to the character. The Foley artist may be called upon to create a special kind of walk for an animated character. Take nothing for granted when performing Foley footsteps for animation. And remember that animated features and episodic cartoons are very different.

Additionally, the footwear that animated characters wear is likely to be from a different time period. Thus, shoe auditions are imperative. Listen to a variety of shoes carefully. There is no production track to work with. All of the sounds will be artificially constructed. The Foley artist is creating the ground surfaces, the walls, the furniture, the clothing, and the footwear; in short, everything the character wears and touches. It is an exciting adventure. Everything the Foley artist does actually matters.

### [**Proper Properties**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1_3)

The challenging aspect of creating props in an animated feature that is situated in a make-believe world is that one does not know what that world is supposed to sound like. This is fantastic and fraught with possible dangers. If the Foley artists have good communication with those who make the final decisions, then they will get the information they need to make excellent choices regarding floors, costumes, props, etc. However, they will also be prepared to make alternative tracks if they do not know what the sound editors are doing for the film. This is the precarious aspect. There is always the possibility that the editors are assuming that Foley is creating certain special sounds and the Foley artists are expecting the editors to be handling those duties.

Additionally, there are special synthesists who create special design effects that complement animation projects. The Foley artists need to know who is responsible for what sounds. This is always the main question in every film, television, or game project, but in animation, it is more critical than ever. More often than not, there will be specially designed sounds that will need to be imagined. Who gets the honor?

![Figure 11.3 Mike Horton performing props for Tron: Uprising. ¶ Source: Courtesy of Walter Spencer and Hi Fi Foley](Images/fig11_3.jpg)

*Figure 11.3* *Mike Horton performing props for Tron: Uprising.* ¶ *Source: Courtesy of Walter Spencer and Hi Fi Foley*

Oftentimes, action in animation is hypnotically fast. Whoa! What just happened there? Let’s look at that again. You may find yourself wondering what you just saw fly by. Animation is not designed to have every little detail seen. The first priority is to make sure the story points in props are done properly. Take the time to do those props with taste. Make sure you know if this animated project is over the top or understated, elegant or slapstick, infantile or sophisticated. The nuance and details come later.

### [**Technological Transitions**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1_4)

It is interesting that, as film has gone to digital and animation uses more computer-generated imagery (CGI), the actual process of making an animated film has not changed much. The tradition of hand-drawn storyboarding has survived the technological convergence. Additionally, if sound supervisors are involved early enough in the process, some of the sound is recorded early. But, typically, it is done in postproduction. The voices, however, are done before any animation is rendered.

One change, however, is that there are more temporary sounds added in by picture editors. This can be useful if it helps the sound supervi-sor get a better idea of the trajectory the supervising animator wishes to take with the sound design.

One facet that complicates performing Foley for animation is that the picture is not totally finished when the Foley artists work on it. They will be working on a scene that is partially rendered and partially in pencil, perhaps with parts of it missing completely. There is a leap of faith that comes with performing Foley for animation. The flow the artist is trying to create with her or his imagination and creativity has to continue even when the picture abruptly disappears or changes drastically.

The rhythm may alter significantly for story purposes, and that can create challenges. When this happens, the principle to keep in mind is that the animated story world is created out of whole cloth. There is nothing real to begin with, so the artist is not locked into anything other than what the animators, the actors, and the sketchy sounds that are on the track from previous temp dubs offer. The Foley artist may be wacky or realistic, understated or overblown, whatever is appropriate to the narrative and the world created by the animators. Keep in mind that the story for animation is written particularly with the animators in mind. That is what is so unique about animation. The story allows the characters and the audience to go somewhere fresh and new. Sound effects and Foley can create new aural concepts that were not considered part of any world before.

### [**Episodic Cartoons**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1_5)

Animated features are treated with the same budgetary and artistic considerations as live-action feature films, and they have similar post-production schedules. This is not necessarily true when considering episodic animation in comparison to episodic live-action shows, whether they are long-story formats or weekly shows. Cartoons are made on short schedules and tight budgets. A half-hour animated television show will be given one day for the complete Foley treatment. Additionally, most cartoon shows have a budget for only one Foley artist. The work is fast and furious. Rather than create the story world fresh, there is a codified setting that is repeated weekly with known characters and progressive actions or storylines.

Often, television animation is not edited. The Foley mixer will try to create enough life on the stage so the rerecording mixers do not have to do too much predubbing. However, there is not much prepping before the tracks go to the dub stage.

### [**Combining Animation With Live Action**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_1_6)

Director Kevin Lima always wanted to be an animator. With his background in theatre and puppetry, in 1980 he enrolled in Cal Arts, where many animators learn their craft. “That’s where Disney animators were made,” muses Lima. He adds, “I’m an actor who hides behind a pencil.” After working at Disney as a character animator, Lima’s directorial debut was *A Goofy Movie* (1995). As supervising Foley editor, I worked closely with Lima, his picture editor Greg Perler, and SSE David Stone, and I learned a great deal about Lima’s interest in sound for animation. “You were my teachers,” Lima revealed to me, as he discussed working with Stone and his sound crew during his first real entry into sound for animation. Lima believes sound for animation is when “you’re building everything from the ground up,” with an “aural landscape of our own choosing.” That is the salient point about sound effects and Foley for animation: the sound is specific to the narrative imagined by the animators. Beginning with the voices of the actors, which drive the emotional character of the animation, the story world is created from scratch.

When Lima transitioned to the hybrid movie *Enchanted* (2007), he recalls, “I had those tools in my box, and the emotional journey of the characters and the sound worked hand in hand.” Lima was mindful to have his story world move from a real-life world to the animated world, and he even considered how the footsteps of the characters would gradually transition from animation to live action. What Lima learned from working on his various projects with different sound teams was that “some think deeply, some think literally.” Regardless of the approach, Lima is not one to impose his perspective automatically. “It’s such a collaborative artform. You can’t rule with an iron fist.” Lima believes he is only one piece of the puzzle; “we are all storytellers,” says Lima. What I learned from Lima is how much pleasure a sound professional can experience when working with a gifted, open-minded, and funny director. When anyone asks me what some of my favorite films were to work on, without exception I always include *A Goofy Movie*. Lima’s word to the wise is “too much sound becomes distracting.”

## [**GAMING THE SYSTEM**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_2)

![](Images/fig0021.jpg)

Today’s interactive games, whether online, apps, or consoles, are very sophisticated. Games are complicated and utilize the most advanced techniques in animation and sound design. There are detailed storylines with many decisions for the players. Along with these decisions come distinct sound choices. Once again, genre plays a part. If the game is more playful, the sound choices have a light and whimsical quality. The more mysterious games utilize sounds that evoke unsettling emotional responses. What is important to note is that Foley is used within the games in varying degrees. A lot of game sound design relies on library sounds that are programmed in to respond to the player’s decisions. A Foley artist performs the scenes with the same purpose as that applied to the most expensive and dramatic films. The difference is in the length of the sequences. In games, the sequences are quite short, as a rule. Each alternate one is performed with specificity and detail, and a great deal of attention is paid to the style and genre of the game. The more choices afforded the player, the more Foley that is included in the action. What is more intriguing is the cross-pollination between games and films as entertainment on the big and small screens. Sound becomes equally important to both. However, as games become more complex, fewer of the sounds are from libraries and more sounds are specially designed. This affords more opportunities for sound design and Foley. The question often arises as to whether Foley can be replaced by digital means. This point can be argued several ways. As the book continues, I will offer  perspectives on why it is unlikely that the ideal way to utilize Foley will be to digitize it. If one recalls that Foley is a performance, and an organic one at that, then while economics makes it enticing to replace it with a mechanical or digital technique, the difference between what can be edited and what can be performed will always be just that—the difference.

As we move into the next phase of sound in media, whether it is film, advertising, television, animation, the internet, or games, there will be a constant adjustment to the use of Foley. It is impossible to predict how technology or cultural styles will affect how sound in general and Foley specifically will be utilized. As with all good education, we study the classical methodology and learn to work with the variations according to the circumstances. However, it is essential to stay flexible as the convergence culture becomes ever more pervasive. Television, games, animation, and film are now all possible to consume on the iPad, Nook, Kindle Fire, smartphone, and other such personal electronic devices. In no time at all, will we be able to personally choose how we wish the narrative to proceed? How will that change what the art of Foley will be responsible for?

If you are someone who games, either using software or online, at some point, you have probably wondered how the sound is integrated into the games. If you are an expert at gaming, then it is more likely that you have some knowledge about sound drivers and how the interactive choices you select alter the abundance of sound choices embedded in the game. What may not be as clear to you is how Foley is cued, performed, designed, and selected for the games you play. Perhaps you are curious about how Foley in games differs from Foley in films or episodic television.

Foley differs significantly from its inception to its interaction within the game. Foley began as a minor player in the gaming world and now is as necessary in most games as the other elements. Sound in games has advanced considerably, largely due to Charles Deenen. Originally from the Netherlands, at 12 years of age Deenen began playing around in his school’s computer lab and wrote a sound driver for games, which he then sold to companies in Europe. By the time he was a teenager, he had started his own company, The Maniacs of Noise, and was selling composing software and the complementary hardware to game companies. At 20, Deenen went to work for Interplay as the head of their audio department—designing sound for the *Lord of the Rings* games—which turned into an 11-year stint improving game sound quality, audience testing, adopting a more Hollywood model for game sound, and outsourcing the music composing. He then took a short break and worked as a sound designer for films in Hollywood. He followed this with ten years at Electronic Arts (EA) in Vancouver, where he oversaw several teams of sound designers and invented techniques to incorporate more believable soundtracks for games. After ten years with EA, he began free-lancing. Deenen now combines his role as a game sound designer with the role of sound editor on feature films, so his experience with sound effects and Foley has had an unusual trajectory.

### [**More Work Than a Movie**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_2_1)

While many film professionals may scoff at the thought of working on a game, it may be surprising to know that the actual design, scripting, acting, visual rendering, and sound design of a game are endeavors that have more in common with an animated film than a live-action movie. The planning and detailed work that are involved with a game are beyond comprehension for those of us who are “civilians.” For our purposes here, we will focus our attention on just sound in general, and Foley more specifically. However, to give an indication of how much work goes into a game, I want to relate anecdotally a *Star Trek* game story. William Shatner came in to voice his lines for the game and saw many sheets of script. He logically assumed it was the script for the entire game. In reality, what he was looking at was the list of his lines alone. There were 25,000 lines of dialogue for the entire game. It took “weeks and weeks and weeks,” says Deenen, to voice all of the characters in the game.

Early on, a player might have a choice of a few paths to take in a game. Thus, the sound choices were minimal. As memory grew, sound designers were able to make more sound choices, adding to the complexity of the games.
