To illustrate, a simple sword clank might have had three to five choices in the beginning of game sound. Now, sound designers can have soft clanks, medium clanks, large clanks, and over-the-top clanks and have distance and perspective on each clank. Additionally, each clank can be stretched, shortened, deepened, and so on. The purpose of the myriad Foley cues is to enable the game player to enjoy many choices with interactive play.

Foley, in the beginning of its inclusion, might have had a few footsteps or prop sounds that were used in specific places. Now, the beginning of a footstep, the middle of the footstep, the end of the footstep, the longer footfall, or any part of it can be utilized, stretched, and made to accommodate a plethora of choices. The goal is to make every sound become as infinitely variable as is possible with whatever process ing is available. However, there are some Foley sounds that must be performed in real time “as is” and played that way.

### [**Foley Is More Often Required Than Optional**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_2_2)

Interestingly, the discussions about replacing Foley with digital footsteps and such are not part of the lexicon in the high-end game industry. The reality and organic nature of Foley are very desirable in games. It is the nature of the performed action of the Foley as a vast contrast to the designed sound elements in games that appeals to both game designers and players.

Typically, two Foley artists are required for the more deluxe games. The reasons are different than for feature filmmaking. The energy and physical industry required for the kinds of Foley sounds in games can be exhausting. While only one artist works a cue at a time, that artist might pound something metal at high velocity for many takes and simply need a break. So, the other artist takes over for a stretch of time. In feature films and television, the reasons for working in twos have to do with combining sounds for one take or helping each other with cues. However, in games, it is the type of cues that is tiring. Additionally, there is a great deal of repetition with only slight variations that can be a bit numbing to the brain and the body.

Footsteps for games can be tedious. The Foley artist might be repeating the exact same scene with only minor variations, such as scuffs, leans, or even a toe tap. To all but the most perceptive eye and ear, there is almost no difference. But even the most minor difference has to be Foleyed. A surface change, a room change, a different response to an action, or a different placement of a foot all have to be performed fresh as if seen for the first time. It can be tiring for the artist because the vari ations are so slight. Yet, the energy and the characteristics of the game character have to remain consistent.

Footsteps for action games, on the other hand, are challenging for another reason: the artist has to run and run and run, but each time has to change the direction, or the footing, or the impacts and not lose balance or breath. The changes, once again, are minimal, yet substantial.

Props for games vary as well. In some, they are easier, as in a “car game” that requires a “tumbling of objects or bullet impacts,” says Deenen. However, games that include many types of weapons and a lot of variations in the handling of objects require much more time to Foley. A game might take one hour to Foley, or it might take four weeks. In today’s economic struggles, four weeks is a long time to Foley one project.

At one point, Foley artists were performing sound for games without any visual clues as to what the game would look like. They were essentially performing wild Foley, but with a scene having been previously described to them. However, as Quick Time became available, this aspect of performance became easier. Now, artists have some rendering with which to work. Deenen is largely responsible for developing the sound aesthetic in games that is more like a feature film. He was concerned that the visuals were getting better and the sound was not. After working at Interplay for 11 years, he felt the industry was at a standstill and he needed a break.

After working in features for a while, he developed a sense of what he felt was missing from games, and when he returned, he introduced several changes to game sound. This evolution included inventing techniques that allowed the sound to be more believable, which supported the visuals if they were not convincing. He learned from working in films that great sound design can actually help visuals appear better. Now, he believes strongly in getting the best composers to write the score, the best sound designers to design and edit the sound, and the best Foley artists to perform the Foley. First-rate Foley is expensive. It requires a good-sounding stage, two excellent artists, and a gifted mixer who knows how to record Foley for games. That, Deenen discovered, is the real secret: a great mixer who understands the difference between mixing sound for films and mixing sound for games. “The Foley artist will be made or broken by the mixer recording,” says Deenen. So, who are some of these gifted artists and mixers, and what makes them good models for someone who wants to perform or mix Foley for games?

### [**Multiple Depths, Layers, and Distances**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_2_3)

The person recording the Foley has to be much more involved with the actual Foley process than with film or episodic television. To begin with, Deenen discovered that the use of one microphone—typically the method used to shoot films and television—would mean the Foley artist would have to perform a prop the exact same way and with the exact same ferocity and depth as all prior takes, but at different microphone distances and angles. This is a nearly impossible task. It quickly became clear that a mixer who could adapt their recording methods to utilize three or four microphones at a time, at various distances, and using varying settings would allow the artist to perform the action while the mixer recorded it on several tracks with the different distances and depth perceptions. This saved time and energy for the Foley artist. Few mixers, however, are ever required to develop such a skill.

David Jobe, who has many years in the chair mixing ADR and Foley for both features and television, was up for the challenge. When David Lee Fein was the Foley artist for a game, Jobe was mixing for him and Deenen wanted to try some different techniques with processing and microphone placement. Jobe was accommodating and innovative and caught on to what was needed. Both Jobe and Fein turned in fantastic work for Deenen. The qualities they displayed were innovation, a tireless sense of curiosity, and a desire to try something new. Fein, who is discussed in [Chapter 1](#Ch01.xhtml), is a veteran Foley artist who never loses the desire to jump in and have an adventure on the Foley stage. Soft-spoken and thoughtful, he masterfully handled everything Deenen wanted from Foley.

Foley artist Dan O’Connell can create magnificent props with whatever is around. He often surprises the sound supervisor with inventive ideas not considered previously. When Deenen first encountered O’Connell, it was to create the sound of a gigantic, creaky ship on an ocean. The problem? There was no visual referent to which O’Connell could relate. When O’Connell pulled out one small wooden chair, Deenen was concerned. He reminded O’Connell that this was a very big and creaky ship. O’Connell agreed. He said, “Trust me.” O’Connell knew his prop and got every possible creak at every possible volume imaginable. The lesson here is twofold: a gifted and experienced artist knows how to work the prop, and never assume that what something looks like is any indication of what it sounds like. What Deenen says about O’Connell now is that he is one of the best at bringing the kind of energy and vitality to Foley that is required for game sound.

![Figure 11.5 Charles Deenen has found that he works better these days with less hardware. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Charles Deenen](Images/fig11_5.jpg)

*Figure 11.5* *Charles Deenen has found that he works better these days with less hardware.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Charles Deenen*

Another Foley artist that understands the complexity and “punchy, big impacts” that Deenen says are necessary for the over-the-top Foley in games is Gary Hecker. Hecker has become the “go-to guy” for games, says Deenen. Hecker is particularly good at very boisterous and attention-getting Foley that works beautifully in the game world. He works very quickly and has a knack for making the Foley as loud and as big as necessary—which is essential in today’s cacophonous games. Many film Foley artists specialize in more naturalistic or understated Foley. Games and action films have cross-pollinated their sound aesthetics, so understatement is not appropriate for game Foley. As Foley has contributed more to game sound, Foley artists more accustomed to working on feature films and television find they are often asked to take on a game project. The approach is very different, because the artist must work in short cues that do not lead to a story necessarily but do contribute to the multiplicity of variations provided for the gamer. Each cue is miniscule in length compared to traditional Foley work and requires intense concentration, as each choice a player makes must include a variation in the Foley sounds performed.

### [**Simply Put**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_2_4)

Foley plays a part in all three aspects of sound design in game sound: interactive, cinematics, and interactive cinematics. The cinematics aspect of game sound is the most similar to films. The interactive, or “in-game,” aspect is the least, and the interactive cinematics is a hybrid—half like a film and half like an interactive. The tasks for Foley change depending on which of these three are being performed. The in-game aspect is the heaviest and most complex. This is the aspect that has the multiple choices that determine the direction in which the game will go next. Thus, the Foley required for this part of the game will be altered and pro cessed. The cinematic aspect will be performed more as a film, with two artists working together, and the hybrid aspect will have both.

The preferred microphone to use when recording Foley for games is a shotgun microphone. It will pick up the sound without getting other ancillary noises. The type of microphone is not the primary challenge when recording and mixing Foley for games. The challenge is in utilizing multiple microphones at several distances and recording them all at once while processing them to get multiple sounds out of one performance.

Deenen used to have many racks of equipment. Now he uses Pro Tools with a lot of plug-ins. He finds that is all he really needs to do the work, and had found it unnecessary to invest in too much equipment. He believes it is better to get the right equipment and learn to use it properly.

When asked why it would not be easier to put sounds in a library and use them for the Foley, Deenen leaves us with this thought: “It takes more time to get it from a library. It is quicker to get an artist who can do it. And it sounds better.”

## [**RADIO DAYS TO RADIO PLAYS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_3)

![Figure 11.6 Ele Matelan performing a radio drama Foley effect with a grass bag simulating running through grass at WildClaw Theatre. ¶ Source: Photo by Chuck Bender, courtesy of Ele Matelan](Images/fig11_6.jpg)

*Figure 11.6* *Ele Matelan performing a radio drama Foley effect with a grass bag simulating running through grass at WildClaw Theatre.* ¶ *Source: Photo by Chuck Bender, courtesy of Ele Matelan*

Ele Matelan and Joe Griffin work together as a Foley artist and Foley mixer team in Chicago. While there is a growing abundance of film work in Chicago, the two have found a niche designing and performing audio drama Foley for Chicago theatres. Theatrically produced radio plays traditionally are a rich arena for a sound artist, and they have grown in popularity as contemporary audiences become reintroduced to the audio drama. Matelan began as an actor, studying in Texas at Southern Methodist University in Dallas. Her entry into Chicago theatre and her introduction to Foley presented her with a unique opportunity to work at theatres such as WildClaw Theatre and The House Theatre. As a voice actor and Foley artist, Matelan has made herself a valuable asset in the Chicago theatre and film scene.

Griffin met a theatrical sound designer at the Goodman Theatre in Chicago, and he found that his work as a production sound mixer on low-budget features could be expanded into the theatre scene. He had been a recording engineer for advertising, which quickly led to commercials. He and Matelan have worked together for about five years and work on film as well as radio plays.

Matelan’s entry into radio play sound was in 2011 for a production of *The Elephant Man*. She specializes in horror plays and is known as “the one with the cowboy boots,” as she considers herself a true Texan. She sees Foley for audio drama as “an extension of the characters you are illustrating.” She was influenced by Disney sound effects expert Jimmy MacDonald, and she prefers an “economy of movement” when performing Foley for either radio theatre or films.

When the two work on film projects, she performs her Foley in Griffin’s home studio, where he uses Pro Tools. Using cue sheets, Matelan will often add cues to the session to give the client “the luxury of not liking things.” When working on her theatrical radio plays, Matelan utilizes several microphones for a tabletop and the floor and uses a wooden riser. She often has an assistant, and the two combine sounds for more complicated cues. She concerns herself with how many “expendables” she might use for the show and meticulously plans her performance with the discipline of a theatre actor.

During my years as the Telecommunications Department Endowed Chair at Ball State University in Muncie, Indiana, I was privileged to be the Foley sound artist for several Christmastime radio dramas presented at the university for broadcast on Indiana Public Radio. These radio dramas were presented to a live audience at Sursa Hall at Ball State and broadcast simultaneously; they were broadcast again nearer to the holidays. The rehearsal process was similar to that of a theatre production, with the director leading the cast in table readings, theatre improvisations, and the more practical aspects of using voice as the primary mode of expressing character and relationships, rather than visual blocking and movement. Two directors, Jennifer Blackmer and Mathew Reader—both from Ball State’s theatre department—rotated duties year to year. While their directing styles were different, both Blackmer and Reader had a deep appreciation for what the Foley sound person could bring to the story.

![Figure 11.7  Foley table setup for Ele Matelan as Foley artist for a radio drama. ¶ Source: Photo by Joe Griffin, courtesy of Ele Matelan](Images/fig11_7.jpg)

*Figure 11.7*  *Foley table setup for Ele Matelan as Foley artist for a radio drama.* ¶ *Source: Photo by Joe Griffin, courtesy of Ele Matelan*

### [**Impressionism for the Ears**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_3_1)

I had participated in radio dramas before and had established my personal methodology for creating sounds using the cinematic sensibilities I had honed over my decades in film sound, along with my experience as a professional actor. My approach was to consider what the radio listener would “fill in” aurally when combining the dialogue spoken by the actors, the music played by the orchestra (yes, a live orchestra), and the sounds I contributed. Thus, I found I was creating sounds with the oddest combinations of props to make convincing “aural plot points” to support the narrative. Whether it was vocalizing animal sounds, walking on surfaces to replicate grass, snow, concrete, dirt, wood, or carpeting, or creating environmental sounds and prop sound effects, I wanted to engage the imagination of the listener with the poetry of impressionism.

An example of this was creating a car sound for the taxi in *It’s a Wonderful Life*. I decided to give the listener a sense of being inside the taxi, rather than doing the predictable engine motor, gear shifts, brakes, etc. that one would anticipate. Instead, I focused my efforts on creating the *inside* of the taxi with the sense of movement and sound the inhabitants would experience. It was my sense that hearing the dialogue while the characters were riding in the taxi would be more intimate and avoid the issue of sound recordings or the predictable car sounds. My props were a skateboard, the top of a tackle box, and a leather massage square for seat creak. I placed the skateboard atop the tackle box top and manipulated a rickety sense of fast travel with my left hand; with my right hand, I creaked the leather against my torso. I recall that director Reader was curious about this contraption I had designed, but hearing it back over the speakers during the performance and later on the radio broadcast, he found the choices quite convincing.

### [**Character Signifiers**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_3_2)

Another example of an alternative prop choice I utilized was for *A Christmas Carol*. In this radio drama, the ghosts of past, present, and future were all created strictly from their dialogue with Scrooge. It occurred to me to design a signature sound for each one that would alert the listener to the magic and apparition-like presence of each ghost. It seemed to me that each ghost needed a different leitmotif that distinguished it from the others. I used bells and jingles for ghost past, a bell tree for ghost present, and ominous creaks and squeaks for ghost future. For Marley’s ghost, I utilized several sets of chains of varying pitches and weight and hit them continually against the door or my body, depending on the story location, to imply a sense of entrapment in his own relentless sense of doom. Sound evokes emotion and a haptic sense of identity in the listener. Paul Brown, producer of Ball State University’s PBS station, directed the documentary *Comfort and Joy—An IPR Radio Drama Retrospective*, including interviews with many of us who were involved with these projects.

### [**Cast and Crew**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_3_3)

In 1999, I was asked to play the part of the sound person for the musical production *Wicked City Blues*, by Cornell Christianson and Norman Thalheimer, for a performance at the Tiffany Theatre in Hollywood. The musical is a send-up of a film noirish detective story played by radio actors, and my role was as the sound person who was also one of the characters. We did this show again in 2000 at the Norris Theatre in Palos Verdes, and once again for a backer’s audition at the York Theatre in New York City in 2004. Since that time, the show has been performed in other places with other casts and has been reworked.

In our various productions of this show, my role would grow to include some physical comedy and even a tap dance (Norris Theatre production), which put focus on the artistic contribution of the sound (Foley) artist in the radio play. The New York production included Tony nominee Jenn Colella (*Come From Away*) as the leading lady.

What was important for me to understand in this role was that I was both the performer and the actual sound creator for the radio drama part of the story. It was an interesting paradox to be both cast and crew. What any Foley artist may discover when doing sound for a radio drama is that it is necessary to be aware of one’s onstage presence, while knowing when not to distract from the story being told. This nuanced balancing act requires a real understanding of theatre, as one is contributing both visual and audio components, being part of the cast, but not stealing focus from the characters on stage.

In December 2020, The Alliance Theatre, located in Atlanta, Georgia, produced an innovative radio drama of *A Christmas Carol* at Georgia State University’s Center Parc Credit Union Stadium parking lot. The play was presented as a drive-in movie, with attendees being charged per car and featuring the actors in large containers the size of small rooms, complete with lighting and small set pieces. Percussion professor Stuart Gerber, from Georgia State University’s School of Music, performed the Foley effects in his own container that replicated a miniature Foley stage. I was hired as consultant for this project to collaborate with Gerber, sound designer Ben Coleman, and director Leora Morris. We worked together over the course of two months. Gerber’s gifts as a percussionist were considerable, and with Coleman and Morris as co-adapters for the play, it was a unique and exciting experience. Even during the Covid-19 pandemic, theatres have innovated to allow live performances, while expanding their audiences.

The point of these illustrations is to emphasize the need to break out of conventions and utilize sound components to support characters and story in different and experimental ways. Rather than working within a comfort zone of familiar techniques and methods, sometimes a Foley artist will find a situation in which reinventing the purpose and execution of performed sound requires fearless abandonment of all things known, while relying on tried-and-true techniques for combining sound elements to support the narrative. Radio drama presents such an opportunity and can encourage new ideas for both listening *and* hearing. As Austrian sound artist Cornelia Baumgartner says, the critical issue for live radio plays is “how to create sounds when you don’t have the big props.”

![Figure 11.8  Containers for individual cast members and Foley artist Stuart Gerber for a radio play at Alliance Theatre in Atlanta Georgia. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Stuart Gerber](Images/fig11_8.jpg)

*Figure 11.8*  *Containers for individual cast members and Foley artist Stuart Gerber for a radio play at Alliance Theatre in Atlanta Georgia.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Stuart Gerber*

## [**HITCH YOUR TRAILER**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_4)

Postproduction sound editor and mixer Rick Sanchez has been working on movie trailers since 1998. Until 2019, Sanchez worked at Post Haste Sound in Los Angeles. Now, he occasionally freelances. Sanchez’s professional journey began as a music engineer after taking classes in Los Angeles. Around 1975, a friend told him that, “a guy got fired today,” at Record Plant, which was a premier recording studio. The job involved, among other duties, sweeping floors. Sanchez happily took the opportunity and began to work days and observe nights. He recalls setting up sessions for artists such as The Eagles, Stevie Wonder, and John Lennon. “Everyone had their own favorite mics and placements,” Sanchez recalls. “I was told to sit and watch and don’t say anything.” He did, he did, and he did not. These were the days when recording studio staff would do “whatever it takes to make the artist comfortable.”

Sanchez worked his way up to substitute recording engineer and then primary engineer. His years at Record Plant, both in Los Angeles and in San Francisco, trained Sanchez to have a finely tuned ear for music and sound, which came in handy in 1991 when he transitioned into mixing Foley for artists including Fred Brown, Michelle Sharp, Rick Partlow, and Monique Reymond. It was in 1996 that Rick Partlow introduced me to Sanchez as a replacement Foley artist partner for me. Sanchez’s understanding of mixing was invaluable for his development as a Foley artist, and we worked on television shows and films for a few years. Both Part-low and Sanchez were my Foley partners on *Cats Don’t Dance* (1997).

When Sanchez moved his skills over to work as a sound professional on movie trailers for films to be released on video after theatrical release, he found his experience in mixing and performing Foley to be an asset. He was skilled at adding any additional Foley for the trailers himself in the small studio where he mixed and edited sound effects to the “stems” from the rerecording mix. Sanchez also mixed the voice-over narrative for the trailers. Many of the voice-over actors are familiar voices to film viewers, as there are a few select voice actors who do most of the trailers. One such voice actor, Mark Elliot, is often heard on the trailers for Disney videos.

Budgets have gotten smaller, and more demands have been made of those who make trailers for the video releases. Sanchez notes, “as technology got more sophisticated, the producers would say, ‘oh, well, you can fix that’ or ‘can you put a sound effect or whoosh in there to cover it up?’” As another illustration of the expectations of producers for trailers, he relates that often there is a misunderstanding that sound for action films is louder, more aggressive, and more emphasized than sound in dialogue or animation films. When mixing sound for the video for a dialogue film, he muses that the producer queried, “why is ours not as loud as theirs?” (referring to an action film trailer) to which Sanchez reflects to me, “It’s a sweet little commercial; it is not world-wide wrestling.”

What is important to understand about the world of movie trailers is that they are little “mini” films in their own universe with their own sets of protocols. One person might be mixing the trailer with the voice-over, the sound effects stems from the final dub, additional Foley, and enhanced sound effects. The narrative is not the concern; rather, the task is to “sell” the product to the potential viewers, so sound, including Foley and music, is included to advance the purpose of the advertising trailer, not the story in the film itself. With this in mind, there is no real crew to do the work. Rather, one sound expert will typically rework the original sound effects, add some Foley if necessary (in an editing studio or small mixing room), and repurpose the music. For Sanchez, his experiences as a Foley artist, mixer, and editor have allowed him to work on trailers and understand the entire postproduction sound process.

## [**FOLEY COMPLEMENTS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec11_5)

As you can see, Foley for animation, games, radio dramas, and movie trailers serves varying purposes, depending on its role within the world of animated films or cartoons, games as interactives or cinematics, radio dramas to evoke character, or movie trailers to supplement the original sound. Unlike its original purposes to create and expand the aural representation of the characters in film, support the narrative, and collaborate with edited sound effects as a partner in sound design, Foley’s use has expanded to breathe life into animated characters, bring a plethora of choices to a gaming experience, evoke a sense of connection to voice-driven radio drama, and to amplify the commercial appeal of a movie trailer.

By utilizing the very skills, conventions, protocols, and best practices of high-level film Foley, the artist can become versatile at employing the craft for many different situations. The Foley artist may be asked to perform visually, be invisible, make heightened but realistic sounds, create an environment from props that do not in reality exist in that environment at all, and fill in segments of Foley between other Foley performances already codified in the project. Flexibility, a working knowledge of more performance arts than simply film, and a willingness to learn by making mistakes are valuable skills for a Foley artist open to multiple opportunities.

# [**chapter | twelve**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch12)

## [**Chef or Cook?**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch12)

### [Ingredients and Recipes](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch12)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-16](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-16)

![](Images/fig0022.jpg)

If someone decides to pursue a career as a Foley artist, there comes a moment when they realize that the responsibility to create the recipe for the sound falls on the artist and not on someone else. This is when they must ask the question, “Am I a chef or a cook?” This is not a question to be taken lightly for, in reality, all Foley artists are some combination of both. However, if an artist is at the stage where you are asking how to make a sound or how to approach a particular technique in Foley, you are leaning toward being a cook, which is a great place to start. This chapter is meant to help guide a new artist along with more specificity.

[Chapter 9](#Ch09.xhtml), which details the specific use and manipulations of props, gives some overall guidance regarding how Foley artists work with props that are used repeatedly in films. I recommend a review that chapter for assistance with technique, as well as this one, while developing skill with props. [Chapter 8](#Ch08.xhtml) is meant to give insight and guidance with advice from Foley artists regarding footsteps.

If, however, you feel you need some real basic ingredients and recipes that can be broken down, either because you are beginning the process or because you are finding gaps in your development in Foley, this chapter may be of help to you. A word of caution to all readers: no one can tell you how to be a chef. You have to develop your own style, your own flavor, and, eventually, your own recipes for how to create sounds for various films, television shows, games, animation projects, and commercials or advertising spots. At some point, you must let go of any idea that there is actually a rubric or hard and fast rule to follow. There is not. Foley is an art. Art has no rules, only guidelines. So, let us begin with some ingredients, then a few basic recipes. I encourage you to experiment and become the great chef you can become in Foleydom. The true stars of Foley did not copy anyone else. They were bold and tried things others did not dare. What is the worst that can happen, really?
