## **RECIPES**

1.  **Cop gear: baseball glove (or leather purse), keys, handcuffs, dead bolt**
    
    Hold in your hands in a way that feels comfortable. Be sure to arrange the configuration to feature the leather of the baseball glove to get leather squeak. Only feature handcuffs if it seems appropriate to the scene. Otherwise, keep in mind that metal records hot. Handcuffs and keys will record hotter than the leather.
    
2.  **Gun rattle: dead bolt, door handle, starter pistol or handgun that is plugged**
    
    Hold the item lightly so it has a little bit of “gun life” to it. Too much rattle will seem silly and unreal. The point is to add a sense of drama to the scene. If needed to, hold two items together but do not rub them together. This will take a little practice.
    
3.  **Eyeglasses: either dark or reading glasses**
    
    The trick to having glasses sound natural is to control them with one’s hands so they sound like they are against skin. Do not have them dangling unless they are dangling in the scene. If they are on a desk, be sure to have the sound of the desk as they are picked up. Always consider what they are on.
    
4.   **Paper sounds: newspaper, sheets of paper, writing paper**
    
    The trick to paper is to use it lightly. It is surprising how little it takes for paper to record. Be as specific as possible. Do not overdo and be precise. Even when touching the paper, be very specific and clear about the touch. I suggest playing back the paper cue every time until you are certain you know what you are doing with paper. Newspaper in particular is likely to record more crinkles than expected.
    
5.  **Snow: cornstarch, corn meal**
    
    Cornstarch is for the squeak. If the cue is on concrete, use some corn meal for the body of the snow and a little bit of cornstarch for the squeak. Squeaks record differently on every stage and with every microphone, so this is trial and error. If the cue is in dirt, use a little bit of cornstarch in the dirt. If the snow is slushy, use a wet chamois on concrete and some wet mud for the snow. If the snow is frozen over, use some shaved ice if possible. Otherwise, use coffee grounds, wet concrete, and trial and error until it sounds right.
    
6.  **Ice skating: block of ice and either an ice skate or a heavy bladed knife**
    
    This can be tricky, but the motion of the skate has to match the cuts of the scene. Watch the impacts of the skater. Also, a pen sometimes works well on the ice. I have also used concrete with some water on it and a knife or a sharp pen.
    
7.  **Boat dock: wooden pallet**
    
    There is nothing like having a wooden pallet on a Foley stage. There are many uses for it, but this one is essential. The creaks are particular to every wooden pallet. They are used for moving furniture and large appliances. They are a necessity for every well-equipped Foley stage.
    
8.  **Bus steps: metal square with rubber mat under it**
    
    This works well, unless the bus is in busy traffic. Then forget the rubber mat. Decide what is aesthetically pleasing for the scene.
    
9.  **Escalator: heavy metal surface with rubber mat under it**
    
    Same as for bus steps with conditions: more expensive venues have nicer sounding escalators. Use discretion.
    
10.  **Bare feet footsteps**
     
     May not be necessary on carpet; on a hard surface overplay slightly, but use the mixer as a guide. Walk toe-heel instead of heel-toe if it does not record right. Experiment.
     
11.  **Children’s footsteps: lighter and smaller**
     
     The trick to children’s footsteps is that they are faster, smaller, and lighter.
     
12.  **Running footsteps: be impressionistic**
     
     It is not always possible to get every footstep. So, look for the basic feel of the rhythm. The edits will not match; get the basic feel of the run. Chances are, in a scene where someone is running, there will be music. But the biggest mistake is to get caught up in a rhythm and stick with it. Pay attention to changes as the edits change.
     
13.  **Leather car seat: chiropractor block or leather jacket**
     
     Be tasteful with this. Think about the quality of the car.
     
14.  **Horse footsteps: coconuts with meat baked inside and hot glued in**
     
     This takes practice. Figure out the best way to hold them. Do you want to hang on and keep your wrists lose? Do you want to have straps to keep them on? Follow the back feet. Horses lead with their back feet and fall onto their front feet when galloping or running. Watch the edits. Rather than be precise, be rhythmic. Watch the musicality of the horse. This is also true for other animals, by the way.
     
     Microphones should be positioned as follows: one boom microphone over the top of the horses and one small body microphone buried in the dirt (if possible) to give the horses extra weight. Dig the dirt up underneath so the hooves do not get hollow. The dirt should continue to fill up in the coconuts.
     
15.  **Cats: either hands with fingernails or gloves with small tacks in the fingers**
     
     The trick to cats is that they do not walk with their claws out. They use the pads of their feet. So, they walk very lightly. They only use their claws when climbing on cloth or when kneading.
     
16.  **Dogs: bare hands with fingernails, gloves with small tacks in the fingers, or fake nails**
     
     The trick to dogs is that they use their claws more than cats, but not consistently. They slide around. So, use the claw sounds to help dogs get their balance and to establish that they are around. Too much claw sounds fake. Also, the dog collar and dog chain are very useful in “selling” the dog.
     
17.  **Water: plastic pool if no built-in tank**
     
     The trick here is to avoid hitting the plastic with the water. Fill the pool deep enough to get a variety of pitches, but not so deep that when water splashes out of the pool it will hit hard surfaces, splash onto concrete or wood, or hit a microphone stand. Deep glugs sound nice and have plenty of bass. Hit the surface of the water with flat hands to record light splashes. Play with the pitches. Be very careful not to splash the microphone. If there is a hydrophone, that is great. Most artists do not. Use a boom microphone and aim it down, but not directly at the water or it will get splashed. The mixer can try various positions. Also, try two microphones to see how they record. Be adventurous. Every room is different.
     
18.  **Goop: wet chamois, body lotion, mouth noises, hair gel, sour cream, hand soap**
     
     This is a trial-and-error thing. One really needs to see what works. Normally, it will take several tracks to get goop just right.
     
     One can also get a chicken from the grocery store. It is expensive and full of bacteria, so be mindful. Wash hands and the area it is used on. The bacteria from a chicken is deadly when not cooked. Take this warning seriously and use Lysol.
     
19.  **Bones: celery**
     
     Need I say more?
     
20.  **Grit: coffee grounds right from the can**
     
     Get the cheapest can of coffee and keep it dry. Use it sparingly and grind it down with underfoot if the concrete is supposed to be slightly gritty. If the surface is supposed to be in a sketchy part of town, use more.
     
     Be careful. Grit pops on a microphone. That is why Foley artists will use coffee instead of dirt. However, one can use dirt, just be mindful.
     
21.  **Kisses: yes, Foley artists kiss their hands**
     
     It can get sloppy.
     
22.  **Hugs: Foley artists typically hug themselves**
     
     Get the right clothing.
     
23.  **Claps: be mindful of the bounce in the room**
24.  **Cloth passes: soft blue jeans**
     
     If doing one cloth pass for the entire scene to fill in for any ADR, use jeans or generic cloth. If special cloth is needed, then do only that cloth on its own track. Rule: less is more. Do not overdo cloth. I can promise that if the cloth movement is overdone, the rerecording mixer will not play it.
     

![Figure 12.2 Celery kerackkk! ¶ Source: Photo by the author](Images/fig12_2.jpg)

*Figure 12.2* *Celery kerackkk!* ¶ *Source: Photo by the author*

![Figure 12.3  Joan Rowe preparing for a cloth cue. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Steve Lee, Hollywood Lost and Found](Images/fig12_3.jpg)

*Figure 12.3*  *Joan Rowe preparing for a cloth cue.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Steve Lee, Hollywood Lost and Found*

![Figure 12.4 Meltem Baytok taking great care to balance the car door so it will not move. She is working a prop against the car door, which she has stabilized against the pits to avoid unwanted resonance. Notice the placement of the shotgun microphone. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Claudio Ahlers](Images/fig12_4.jpg)

*Figure 12.4* *Meltem Baytok taking great care to balance the car door so it will not move. She is working a prop against the car door, which she has stabilized against the pits to avoid unwanted resonance. Notice the placement of the shotgun microphone.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Claudio Ahlers*

## [**SAFETY TIPS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec12_4)

1.  Use eye goggles when working with wood splinters or glass. Splinters and chards are not fun.
2.  Gloves are essential when working with any kind of debris.
3.  Have hygienic masks on the stage when doing a lot of dirt cues. Lungs need to last a lifetime.
4.  Have a broom and a mop on the stage. Keep in clean.
5.  Keep the props organized.
6.  Do not put yourself in danger. It is a movie.
7.  Water can get rancid and attract scary critters. Clean out tanks, pools, and receptacles.
8.  Do not drink out of a glass, cup, or anything without washing it.
9.  Do not drop anything loud or heavy on a stage before saying, “Watch your ears.” The mixer needs to know to turn the microphone down.
10.  Always know where the light switch is.

## [**HINTS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec12_5)

1.  Commit to the cue.
2.  Remember it can be done again.
3.  Be experimental.
4.  Try layering sounds.
5.  Remember that props sound different depending on what they are touching. Example: metal changes the sound of a prop. So does wood.
6.  Think in terms of components. The exact thing is not needed. What is it made of?
7.  Think outside the box. What story world is the project in? The sounds one creates need to support the narrative in that story world.

# [**chapter | thirteen**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch13)

## [**Magic Wind**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch13)

### [Unusual Foley Requests](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch13)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-17](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-17)

![](Images/fig0023.jpg)

I was filling in for a Foley artist colleague on the video *Pocahontas 2: Journey to a New World* (1998), which was a sequel to the animated film *Pocahontas* (1995). The cueing had been executed by a well-intentioned, but seemingly inexperienced editor. One of the cues, a prop cue, was called “magic wind.” While this is a proper sound design concept, and the wind in the original film had a magical quality, this would have been a special design element executed by sound editors.

When the editor called the stage to see how we were doing, we politely asked what he meant by “magic wind.” His response was some version of, “You know, do the wind that you see.” He probably was unaware that this request had two distinct problems: first, it was a sound design that was going to be handled by edited effects, and second, it had to match the effect that was in the original feature.

Foley artists will get requests that do not necessarily make sense to them. There is a plethora of stories that float around the editing rooms and Foley stages about requests that are either impossible to do or more appropriately done by editors or special sound design. Sometimes sound editors are not certain about what they want, and other times they cannot communicate what they hear in their own imaginations. The purpose of relating this story is to illustrate that some Foley cues will not make sense to the artist, even if they do make sense to editors. Communication is always essential, and to ask for more information about what the artistic intention is can help the Foley artist a great deal.

## [**SOMETHING NEVER HEARD BEFORE**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_1)

Robert Mott relates a story about a television newscast for which he was preparing sound effects:

> They wanted the sound of an atom bomb. \[It is important to note that no one had ever heard one before. The testing of the bomb on American soil was a new phenomenon.\] They went out to field record, and the bomb melted the microphone. They parked so far away, yet it lifted the van off the ground.

So, they asked Mott to make a sound effect for the atom bomb. He had only worked with established taped or disc recordings. He watched the footage and saw the mushroom cloud. He used a recording of the Mogambi waterfalls and slowed it down for the roar. Then he added a layer of a wall being demolished and raised the level. By the time he was finished, he had used four records, some of them doubled up for the resultant effect.

Sound professionals have often been asked to create unusual sounds with the expectation that anything can be done on a Foley stage. Foley artists will often try to solve these puzzles with creativity and imagination, resulting in effects that surpass the editor’s request. Other times, the results are not as satisfying. These special requests take time and money to execute.

## [**THE SLOWED-DOWN EFFECT WAS OMINOUS AND BIZARRE**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_2)

John Post, while working on The Thing (1982), needed to have a sound for the monster underneath the board planks. Supervising sound editor David Yewdall asked him to create the monster moving under the planks while the planks came apart and flew into the air. This is a tricky effect to create on a Foley stage, but Post was always up for the challenge. So, he and Duane Hensel took short pieces of lumber and laid them on the stage, rather like dominoes. The two men stood at each side of the planks, and in sync to picture they grabbed a plank and rattled it against the floor and the next plank. Then, Post put on his editor’s hat, and after the cue was recorded he slowed it down to a snail’s pace, which added more depth. The slowed-down effect was ominous and bizarre.

As a side note, Post also did a dog voice for the dog as it ran through the snow for the same film. Today, an actor in SAG-AFTRA would be hired to do that.

David Jobe was the Foley mixer on some of the *Terminator: The Sarah Connor Chronicles* episodes (2008). On one episode, the Foley artists were asked to create something for the terminator’s metallic, articulated arms. David Fein “grabbed an I-beam and rubbed it. I picked the chorusing voice on the Eventide \[harmonizing hardware\] and we did it,” Jobe recalls.

## [**FOLEY HUMOR EVOLVES OUT OF EVERY PROJECT**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_3)

Joan Rowe relates a story about a request on *ET: The Extra-Terrestrial* (1982):

> Spielberg wanted ET to be “liquidy and friendly,” and sometimes “liquidy” sounds slimy. It’s hard to put words to sound. I walked through some stores and listened to the movement of packaged liver in a flat container. It had a “cheery little sound.”

Rowe would handle this package of liver as one of the sounds for ET’s body. Every few days, she would go to the store near the studio and get another package. She would walk into the store in her Foley attire (leotards and Foley shoes), and one time she heard the cashier whisper to the person he was chatting with, “That’s the woman who listens to the liver!” Rowe also recorded a track of Jell-O clumped inside a wet towel and a track of a bag of popcorn to augment her liver effect. She moved all three props in sync with ET as he moved.

One of the most frequent questions regarding the Foley on *Predator* (1987) is how the predator’s body was created. There is a complicated answer. The predator’s body required several tracks that would be played together. One track was a wet chamois. Another was hand lotion on my hands. A third was a wet leather purse. A fourth track was some mouth noises. We called it “predabody.” There was also “predaface,” “predahands,” and “predafeet.” Clearly, Foley humor evolves out of every project.

![Figure 13.2  “That’s the woman who listens to the liver!”](Images/fig13_2.jpg)

*Figure 13.2*  *“That’s the woman who listens to the liver!”*

## [**COLLEGIALITY GOES A LONG WAY IN ART**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_4)

Greg Barbanell had an odd task for the film *Defending Your Life* (1991). He spent a day trying to find a way to “walk in heaven,” as the supervisor had requested. Barbanell believed that this was not really a sound you should really hear, but the supervisor was attached to the idea. Barbanell cannot remember how it was resolved, but it cost stage time and money lesson here? Some ideas should be ironed out before they hit the Foley stage. Foley artists will not always have control over this situation.

Both *RoboCop* (1987) and *RoboCop 2* (1990) utilized a created contraption for RoboCop’s footsteps. David Fein and Ken Dufva had done the original film, so when I was asked to do the sequel, it was necessary to duplicate the same footstep quality. Fortunately, the mechanical device designed for his footsteps still existed, so I was able to use it. It looked a bit like a mechanical foot and had a heavy springlike action that took both hands to handle properly. It was exhausting to manipulate this ingenious invention, and the microphone had to be placed in a careful way to avoid getting slap back from the metal in the foot. What is essential to understand here is the necessity for continuity in sequels.

The sound is a sonic identifier for the character, and fortunately, the prop still existed and Fein and Dufva were happy to share it. Collegiality goes a long way, especially when collaborating with other Foley artists.

## [**BEST LAID PLANS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_5)

On *The Exorcist III* (1990), there were scenes with nurses walking around a hospital. It is normal and customary for Hollywood Foley artists to do nurses in soft tennis shoes or athletic shoes. Nurses walk a lot and always wear comfortable shoes. Their shoes are not a story point, typi cally, so Foley artists walk them to give a sense that the nurses are there, but not to distract from the dialogue or action.

![Figure 13.3 The RoboCop foot device. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Steve Lee, Hollywood Lost and Found](Images/fig13_3.jpg)

*Figure 13.3* *The RoboCop foot device.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Steve Lee, Hollywood Lost and Found*

Sometime during the Foley schedule, Richard Anderson, the SSE, brought actual nurses’ shoes to the stage. He told me that director William Peter Blatty wanted the nurses to be Foleyed in these shoes. The nurses’ footsteps had already been performed but had not been edited and delivered to the stage. Yet it seemed Blatty wanted to contribute an idea for sound. He did not know that the characters had already been walked and that the tracks would fit nicely into the mix, but he knew he wanted literal nurses’ shoes. The rest of this story is that the nurses were performed again in the nurses’ shoes. Because editors were working with magnetic film at the time, there were now duplicate cues performed and this added to the confusion. This illustration is not to disparage any director, but to point out how often directors do not understand what happens in postproduction sound. The essential element is how the footsteps and props *sound*, not what a shoe or prop actually is.

Another nurse story comes from the film *Malice* (1993). Picture editor David Bretherton, who had won the Academy Award for his work on *Cabaret* (1972), was supervising the predubs, while director Harold Becker was shooting ADR in Canada. When Bretherton saw background nurses walking on screen, he decided he wanted to hear them in stiletto heels. If you have not seen the film, you need to know that it is a serious drama. The nurses were in predictable nurses’ shoes, and I have no idea why he wanted to hear high heels. So, I rewalked all the nurses in shoes that sounded like sexy stiletto heels. But I kept the tracks with nurses’ shoes, just in case. When Becker came back, he wanted the tracks with the softer sounding nurses’ shoes and rejected the stilettos. Do not be surprised if a situation like this occurs. It happens more than one might think. Art is mutable, and Foley artists may be asked to do a cue several different ways or to change the way they have done it without explanation.

Joan Rowe tells a story on herself. She is a very creative Foley artist, but admits she sometimes gets herself in trouble with her zeal. While working on *Speed* (1994) for supervisor Stephen Flick, she wanted to do the gears grinding for the elevator. Flick prefers to have the sound effects editors cut in the creative sounds for machinery. Time permitting, he would take great effort to audition and present effects for the editors to design and layer. But Rowe would not be dissuaded. She found a magnetic film rewinder that had a glitch in its mechanical action. When she wound it backward, it “made a good sound,” and she mounted it on a table on the stage. Then she put an editor’s metal trim bin upside down on the table for echo. She wound the rewinder forward for the elevator going up and backward for the elevator gears grinding. Then she banged wires against the trim bin to sweeten the sound for the gears.

When Rowe presented her wonderful creation to Flick, he was impressed with her ingenuity but not as happy with the time she had used to create the effect he had already pulled out of library effects. She is not sure if her Foley was used, but as she puts it, “I did it because I wanted to. My favorite part of doing Foley is that it is an absolutely creative process.” Sometimes, this kind of risk is going to be validated, sometimes not. This kind of Foley artist is valued for their innovative spirit. With more sound design relying on Foley because of smaller budgets or crews, this is a prized talent. Additionally, independent filmmakers often need Foley artists who can create what may not be available in editorial. The other side of this approach is to keep the budget in mind. Always. This fearlessness in trying new sounds was one key reason I asked Rowe to perform the Foley for *A Goofy Movie* (1995).

![Figure 13.4  Ronni Brown surrounded by props on her Foley stage. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Ronni Brown](Images/fig13_4.jpg)

*Figure 13.4*  *Ronni Brown surrounded by props on her Foley stage.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Ronni Brown*

Misa Jakovljevic works in Serbia. He worked as an assistant to the Foley mixer. (In Serbia, the mixer is called the sound technician, and Jakovljevic assisted him.) The Foley artists worked in the dark and were very “magical” according to Jakovljevic. At one point, they were stymied trying to make a “walking tree.” Jakovljevic, in his exuberance, said, “I have \[an\] idea!” He went into the studio, put together some props for the cue, and performed the cue successfully. At that point, the Foley artists told him to get out! However, he is now a veteran Foley artist in Serbia. His enthusiasm, his humor, and his impetuous desire to do the work finally availed him the opportunity to get into the work he now loves.

Greg Barbanell once worked on a film that had a lot of scenes in casinos. The supervisor wanted all the chips to sound different, so he brought in chips from many different casinos. Barbanell used all of the chips and manipulated them in as many ways as he could. When asked if the different chips actually sounded different, he replied, with his typical dry humor and without missing a beat, “No, they didn’t. But we did what they wanted.”

## [**EVERYTHING IS MYSTERIOUS AND SECRET**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_6)

A similar experience happened when Dan O’Connell was working a prop and the supervisor knew what he was using. Repeatedly, he kept asking O’Connell to change the prop because he did not like the sound. The supervisor did not think it sounded like the prop on the screen. (This was back in the day when Foley artists had time and editors would sometimes visit the stage.) Finally, O’Connell went behind a baffle, used his original prop, and said, “just listen to this and see if you like it.” The supervisor loved it. By obscuring the prop, O’Connell encouraged the supervisor to ignore his own bias about what prop was being used and allow his ears to decide. Now, O’Connell never lets anyone see what he does for props. Everything is mysterious and secret. It might not be necessary to go to such lengths to ensure that one’s artistic integrity is respected, but one might want to try a variation of this method if an onlooker decides the sound is “wrong.”

Robert Deschaine, a veteran Foley, ADR, and rerecording mixer, was mixing Foley on a movie, and the editor wanted fresh grass for the Foley every day. Fresh sod was brought in, and Deschaine carefully watered it every night and put it under globe lights. At the end of three nights, the grass was dead and did not sound right. Someone said, “Why don’t we use quarter-inch tape and put a cloth on it?” They did so, and it was remarkably realistic. Sometimes we *all* overthink this process.

When working on *Antichrist* (2009), Julien Naudin relates that the director, Lars von Trier, wanted the sound of the inside of a man’s body. Naudin said, “I could not do this, of course.” So, he put a microphone inside a raw chicken and another microphone on the floor close to the chicken. He then manipulated the chicken to give von Trier the sound he wanted. Naudin considers von Trier to be easy to work with because “he knows what he wants. We have many brainstorms before the movie.” Most directors are not as involved with Foley as von Trier, but he has a strong sense of what he wants from film sound. Naudin is immensely creative and knows exactly how to give von Trier anything he wants.

## [**YOU DO NOT KNOW WHAT IS GOING TO HAPPEN**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec13_7)

Many years ago, I worked with a young, but smart editor who was new to supervising at the time. He spent all his time on the stage and micro-managed every cue. It was a frustrating experience for everyone, because no one can be in another person’s head and know exactly what it is they are imagining. Additionally, try as he did to express just how he wanted every prop to sound, it seemed that he used every word in the sound lexicon to no avail. After hours trying to decipher his concepts, we started getting silly with our humor to get through the day. Finally, after thinking our expertise had been ignored all day, we began to believe we were building a rapport with the editor. But we were wrong. The clarifying moment occurred when he commented on how he wanted the street to sound. He said quite seriously, “Just junk it up.”

![Figure 13.5 Susan Fitz-Simon takes a break from working on Orange is the New Black. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Susan Fitz-Simon](Images/fig13_5.jpg)

*Figure 13.5* Susan Fitz-Simon takes a break from working on Orange is the New Black. ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Susan Fitz-Simon*

Another experience working with a Foley editor on the Foley stage during a session had to do with replacing some footsteps. We were nearing the end of our work for the day when an editor came to the stage and asked us to replace a few of the steps in the middle of a scene. She did not want the entire walk, just a few of the steps in the middle of the walk itself. There was a picture edit that changed the scene significantly, and the footsteps no longer matched the picture. The problem for us was that since we had finished walking all the characters, the stage had flooded and that particular surface had been destroyed. I suggested we rewalk the entire scene on a different surface. My concern was that the new footsteps would not match the old ones. She rejected that idea. So, I obediently replaced just the few steps she wanted. I still do not know how that turned out. The essential lesson from these incidents is this: while a Foley artist may have one perspective of how to solve an issue, the editor may have another idea.

What is important to take away from all of these examples of unpredictable instances that have occurred is this: you do not know what is going to happen, and you cannot control how other people are going to respond. You do not know what crazy ideas might come at you that you are supposed to implement with no explanation, nor are you always going to be respected for your opinion or expertise. You will have to find your own way to make peace with that part of being an artist. Unless you are going to be a solo filmmaker who does everything yourself, someone, at some time, is going to pull the rug out from under you or cause you to doubt yourself. Being a Foley artist, or any kind of artist, requires you to interpret the ideas of others who might not acknowledge what you can do. It is a life lesson we must all learn to grapple with.

Robert Mott expresses it best when describing his ultimate responsibility as a soundman. He says, “I had to fill in what they imagined.” When asked, “What was your favorite part about doing sound effects?” His reply, which speaks for all who do Foley today, was, “I think the creative challenge.”

![Figure 13.6 It needs to sound more purple.](Images/fig13_6.jpg)

*Figure 13.6* *It needs to sound more purple.*

# [**PART | FOUR**](#content.xhtml#bck_part4)

## [**Past, Present, and Future**](#content.xhtml#bck_part4)

### [Connecting the Foley Dots](#content.xhtml#bck_part4)

# [**chapter | fourteen**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch14)

## [**Professional Panorama**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch14)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-19](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-19)

![](Images/fig0024.jpg)

Film and television professionals interact on a regular basis, whether it is while working on the same production, talking shop over lunch, sharing time at a sound awards banquet, the Emmys, or the Academy Awards, participating in a conference or festival, or attending other professional events or parties. They run into each other at restaurants, coffee shops, and retail stores. They may even find each other at the same grocery market. When they do share time together, it is likely the conversation will veer toward work, the industry, or shared knowledge of common experiences. It is with this context in mind that I offer some insights and observations from sound professionals, including a team of Foley experts who all work at the same facility, two Foley editors who went through the same college sound design program and now work together, and a production sound mixer who champions women in production sound. Additionally, I give brief mention of two documentaries that feature the work of women who work in film sound and the development of a museum dedicated to film sound, with a special emphasis on Foley.

## [**ALCHEMY POST SOUND**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec14_1)

The players for this roundtable all work at Alchemy Post Sound in Peekskill in New York’s Hudson Valley. Alchemy is responsible for an impressive array of films and episodic shows, including *I’m Thinking of Ending Things* (2020), *I Know This Much is True* (2020), *Fosse/ Verdon* (2019), and the Emmy Award–inning *Cartel Land* (2015). This discussion includes Foley artists Leslie Bloome and Joanna Fang, Foley mixers Ryan Collison and Connor Nagy, Foley editors Nick Seaman and Laura Heinzinger, apprentice Annie Taylor, and Alchemy owner Andrea Bloome (along with husband Leslie). On the company website is a mission statement: “APS enhances the audience’s experience of a story through performed sound, combining a deep knowledge of the art of Foley with innovation.” I spoke with the entire team of Alchemy Post Sound on a Zoom call in April 2020, while New York was in the deep crisis of the Covid-19 pandemic. Although everyone was in their own “Zoom box,” there was a sense of camaraderie and collaboration that was clearly evident. I had visited Alchemy Post Sound a few times in the past and am familiar with the facility, the team, and the culture they have created.

Andrea Bloome is considered by the rest of the team to be “the brains behind the business.” She strives to have a positive interaction with all of her clients and believes the key to a successful company is a truly collaborative approach to the work. She remarks, “you’ve got to hire the right people; the right fit … my job is to facilitate their creativity. I remove the roadblocks … we need to be a cohesive unit.” Husband Leslie, a 30-year veteran Foley artist, worked in Los Angeles with various artists such as Paul Holzborn, John Post, Rick Partlow, and Kitty Malone and also spent time working at the Saul Zaentz Film Center in Berkeley in the late 1990s with Foley artists Margie O’Malley and Marnie Moore. “The budget is the real issue,” he says, and he concerns himself with “what we can bring out and how can we help the story sonically.” He emphasizes that “building the team is everything to us. Everyone has been a fantastic addition.” The facility has two Foley stages with control rooms, one convertible Foley-ADR studio, and editing rooms, along with Foley prop storage areas and a main reception area. The stages have areas of “live space, dead space, and really dry space,” laughs Leslie.

![Figure 14.2  Joanna Fang, Leslie Bloome, Ryan Collison, and Connor Nagy at Alchemy Sound in Peekskill, New York. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Andrea Bloome](Images/fig14_2.jpg)

*Figure 14.2*  *Joanna Fang, Leslie Bloome, Ryan Collison, and Connor Nagy at Alchemy Sound in Peekskill, New York.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Andrea Bloome*

Joanna Fang, a graduate of New York University in film and music, has worked at Alchemy as a Foley artist for over six years; she relates that she was not taught a lot about sound while at the university. Most of her experience was working on student and independent films. She performed the tasks of sound editor, Foley and rerecording mixer, and Foley artist. She found Foley to be the most intriguing and has found a home at Alchemy. While she says that, at film school, “they don’t teach you adaptability … it is interesting how quickly things become dogma,” a great thing about working at Alchemy is “we’re constantly evolving our sound … we inherently know what the other person needs.” She adds that there is a “measured openness” to new ideas.

Nick Seaman, lead Foley editor, says, “a lot of people can wear multiple hats,” to which Ryan Collison, Alchemy’s lead Foley mixer, adds, “I had to put everything aside, my pride, my sensitivities,” because it is essential that “everyone is on the same page.” Collison had worked at Sound One in New York City, and he now commutes from his home in Queens to Peekskill. Laura Heinzinger, who works closely with Seaman as a Foley editor, discusses their approach to Foley cueing, “The budget comes into play.” She likes to pick out the key plot points of the film, while concerning herself with “what the mixer is looking for” in the Foley session. Collison agrees that Foley sessions run smoothly when cues are “specific and consistent, names spelled properly,” and he likes information about the surface and even shoe type. Details such as if a surface is wet or dry, for example, are helpful. Fang adds, “every person knows every shoe, every prop.” Apprentice Annie Taylor, a graduate of Savannah College of Art and Design (SCAD), interned with Leslie Bloome on the Foley stage for three months and was hoping to be a Foley artist. When the opportunity to work full time at Alchemy arose, provided she would be willing to be an editor instead, she was more than happy to adjust her expectations. She found cueing and performing Foley to be much different than she had experienced at SCAD, which she found to be more theoretical. Cueing for a real show is stricter than in the sound design program at SCAD, and she also muses that, at SCAD, Foley shoes were “communal.”

Leslie Bloome, Fang, and Heinzinger are active musicians. Bloome plays bass, Fang is a guitarist, and Heinzinger is a pianist. Annie Taylor, the newest addition to the team, plays bassoon. Taylor laughs, “the bas-soon is the cello of the woodwinds.” Both Fang and Heinzinger have also been involved with film scoring.

What is notable about the sound team at Alchemy Post is the noncompetitive and supportive work environment they enjoy. As Fang explains, both Bloomes brought their collective business philosophies from their other experiences to Alchemy to create a special work environment. As Leslie Bloome expresses it, “I wanted to create sounds without boundaries.”

## [**FILM SCHOOL FLOWS**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec14_2)

Both Anna Gramlich (*The Goldbergs*, *Madame Secretary*) and Shaun Burdick (*Empire*, *The Blacklist*) graduated from SCAD in 2010 from the MFA in Sound Design program. They moved to Los Angeles shortly afterward and have been roommates ever since. They both had to find piecemeal work by looking on Craig’s List for any kind of independent projects. By 2014, both had found employment at Sony Studios in Culver City. Burdick and Gramlich credit professors John Sisti and David Stone for their tutelage at SCAD. Both feel that having these two film sound professionals as teachers was very helpful. Gramlich appreciates them for instilling the notion that they would have to be realistic about “pounding the pavement” and recalls being told, “it’s not going to be handed to you.”

The two are both Foley editors and work on different episodic series (often with Foley artists Sarah Monat Jacobs and Robin Harlan), and they have found the workflows to be very different than the more fluid workflows they practiced at SCAD. “We knew what our weaknesses were, “ remarks Gramlich, and she and Burdick helped each other at first so they could refine their skills. While Gramlich prefers editing sound effects and music, Burdick likes editing Foley and dialogue.

![Figure 14.3 Shaun Burdick recording a log down a wooden ramp. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Shaun Burdick](Images/fig14_3.jpg)

*Figure 14.3* *Shaun Burdick recording a log down a wooden ramp.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Shaun Burdick*

What Gramlich and Burdick discovered as they transitioned from students to working professionals is the greater emphasis on collaboration in the workplace. Because both had come from theatre backgrounds, they were glad to discover that working at a film studio includes a sense of teamwork rather than the more independent emphasis they experienced in school. Nonunion work, most often on independent films, had afforded more creativity, but the discipline required to meet deadlines and satisfy producers at Sony has been an essential and welcome adjustment. Burdick and Gramlich agree that the generational differences present some challenges. Gramlich observes that the older professionals do not always like being questioned. “They hold their ground,” she explains.

![Figure 14.4  Carrie Sheldon working the boom microphone in production. ¶ Source: Photo courtesy of Carrie Sheldon](Images/fig14_4.jpg)

*Figure 14.4*  *Carrie Sheldon working the boom microphone in production.* ¶ *Source: Photo courtesy of Carrie Sheldon*

While the duo assert that they experienced too much emphasis on the tools at SCAD, they quickly admit that the technology speeds up the process, which can be both good and bad. Gramlich likes to work on shows where she can be more creative and likes to consider, “what is it I think it sounds like,” and Burdick found working on *Blacklist* to be “weird … throws really cool curve balls.”

## [**PRODUCTION PERSPECTIVE**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec14_3)

Carrie Sheldon (*Liza on Demand*, *You*) is a boom operator in production sound. While attending Lane Community College in Eugene, Oregon, she took courses in broadcasting and visual design and demonstrated a talent for sound from the beginning. In 2006, Sheldon agreed to work in production sound for a student who was doing her film thesis on the indigenous people in Panama. This experience led to her decision to move to Los Angeles to pursue a career in production sound. Similar to Gramlich and Burdick, she took whatever jobs she could find, and by 2005 she was working regularly. Although she was eligible to join the production mixers union (Local 695) in 2008, she waited until 2016 when she was offered a stable situation on a TV show.

Sheldon discovered that the convention in her profession is to bring your own gear and “charge back” the expense to the production. There is continuing pressure to keep the gear up to date as technology changes. As Sheldon advanced in her career, she became increasingly aware of sexism in production sound. She perceived a distinct preference for men to be hired as both mixers and boom operators. For this reason, she and production sound colleague Mary Jo Devenney started the group LA Sound Sisters, which offers support and advice and shares work opportunities with members. Sometimes if a producer wants her to work on a low-budget show, “I will donate my gear and send out a \[female\] that I know is trying to be in the sound department and that needs the experience,” explains Sheldon.

Production sound mixers and boom operators are responsible for recording the dialogue on the set of a show and are the essential link to the sonic template that postproduction sound professionals must utilize. How a show is mixed on the set influences how sound editors, Foley artists, ADR editors and mixers, and rerecording mixers approach their crafts. A good production track sets a great foundation for sound design and enables both editors and mixers to focus more on the art of sound, rather than the technical crafting, to overcome or compensate for a less than stellar production mix.

What Sheldon discovered was that only 7% of the union production sound professionals are women. Sheldon and Devenney decided to work toward diversity and equity. “I’m really trying my best to change the face of 695 \[production sound union\],” proclaims Sheldon. “It’s possible for me to recruit enough women and help get them into the union and get 10% over the course of my lifetime … and that is what I’m trying to do.” Sheldon amplifies,

> As we get more women in positions of directing and more people of color … these people have not as much experience as the men … and they need to take a little longer, and they might make mistakes and whatnot, and you find a lot of crew members will say, ugh, she doesn’t know what she is doing. What they don’t realize is you’re actively working against the diversity and inclusion of these people if you don’t understand that we need to be there to support them being taught. They haven’t had as many hours on the job, so of course, they’re going to make some mistakes.

While Sheldon is concerned with the attitudes of some men on the set when they see a woman handling the production sound, “not every guy will give you a chance, and this business is word of mouth,” she is enthusiastic about her work: “my favorite thing is to put microphones on clients.”

## [**WOMEN IN SOUND**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec14_4)

At Ball State University in Muncie, Indiana (where David Letterman attended), the Virginia Ball Center for Creative Inquiry awards several fellowships every year to faculty who have a specific project that is chosen to be funded. The professor and the students spend a semester focused on the single purpose and project of this class. I was awarded one of these fellowships to work with 13 students that I personally selected to work on a short film about women who work in film sound. The students and I traveled to New York and Los Angeles, and as I guided them through the process of the industrial method of filmmaking, we produced the short film *Amplified: A Conversation with Women in American Film Sound*. Sheldon, along with Devenney and other production sound women, were interviewed. Additionally, we interviewed Foley artists, sound editors, mixers, and facility owners. What we discovered was that what Sheldon has uncovered is rampant all over the professional sound industry in film. What was illuminating were the various responses the women had for coping with and even thriving in a part of the film industry that frequently and sometimes blatantly privileges men over women. The general consensus is that situations are getting much better for women in film sound because the younger men coming into the industry have more positive perspectives regarding women working in filmmaking.

Another professional woman in sound who appears in *Amplified* is Midge Costin, the Kay Rose Endowed Chair in the Art of Sound and Dialogue Editing at the University of Southern California. She has also produced and directed her own documentary about film sound that is getting rave reviews, nominations, and awards. *Making Waves: The Art of Cinematic Sound* (2019) is a wonderful documentary that does not specifically focus on the role of women in film sound, but Costin does include interviews with some notable women in the profession. The film is written by Bobette Buster, with music by Allyson Newman, cinematography by Sandra Chandler, and editing by David J. Turner. In this film, many notable film professionals discuss sound design, editing, and Foley.

## [**THE HOLLYWOOD SOUND MUSEUM**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec14_5)

In 2015, film sound professional Steve Lee cogitated around the idea of a museum that would feature and celebrate the many aspects of film sound and the individuals who have contributed to the craft. “I envisioned an entertaining place to learn more about creating sound for film, TV, radio, and other media,” says Lee. His vision is coming to fruition as The Hollywood Sound Museum. One of the features of this museum is “an interactive Foley exhibit where guests could perform sound effects in sync to a film clip of their choice—and then hear it played back.” Lee has raised money for this nonprofit endeavor and has collected many artifacts to display. While the goal is a “brick and mortar” museum for all to visit and enjoy, during the Covid-19 pandemic Lee has adapted his museum to be enjoyed virtually at [HollywoodSoundMuseum.org](http://hollywoodsoundmuseum.org). He is also creating regular webinars with professionals from every aspect of film sound.

## [**FOLEY FUTURES**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec14_6)

The continuing interest in Foley as a craft as well as a performative art has resulted in blogs, social media pages, scholarly articles, podcasts, webinars, and films about the world of Foley and the professionals who continue to perfect and adapt the craft for the ever-changing applications. Film schools are incorporating more courses about film sound, and an increasing number of these institutions are including Foley in their curriculum, with some schools either adapting rooms to accommodate Foley or designing dedicated Foley stages. Those who are enticed into the profession can find more opportunities to start their own Foley businesses or amplify their media work utilizing Foley. What is increasingly clear is the evolution of Foley and the interest in those who are Foley artists continue to provide opportunities to learn it, perfect it, practice it, teach it, discuss it, and write about it.

Foley began as an arcane addition to edited film sound and has developed into an essential part of film, television, web series, games, animation, trailers, radio plays, and commercials. In 1989, when I appeared on *Late Night with David Letterman* demonstrating Foley, very little had been written about Foley, and few professionals were interviewed or even credited for their work. While there is still a large percentage of film goers who have little knowledge of postproduction sound and the professionals who create the soundscapes Foley is an intriguing and mysterious art that attracts a great deal of interest.

# [**chapter | fifteen**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch15)

## [**Foley Reflections**](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch15)

### [Final Thoughts of Experts](#content.xhtml#bck_Ch15)

DOI: [10.4324/9781003008439-20](https://doi.org/10.4324/9781003008439-20)

![](Images/fig0025.jpg)

I usually ask film and sound professionals if they have some parting words for readers interested in Foley. The following are some of the responses I have received.

## [**Mark Mangini**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_1)

(supervising sound editor, sound designer, rerecording mixer, musician)

Sound is a dramatic player. It’s not as interactive a process as I think it needs to be.

When asked what he thinks prepares someone to be an expert in sound:

> Some of the best training they can get is taking acting and screenwriting classes.

## [**Jody Thomas**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_2)

(Foley artist, story editor, creative executive)

It’s really empowering to get to work with people I think are great, and that we get along and have a good time.

## [**Karin Roulo**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_3)

(Foley and ADR mixer, engineer)

When commenting on what a good Foley artist looks for in a footstep cue:

> You look at how he or she is portraying the character.

## [**Robert Deschaine**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_4)

(Foley and ADR mixer, rerecording mixer, facility manager, actor)

There are different ways to approach a project. Some thought as an editor, and others did it as an artist or actor.

## [**Meltem Baytok**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_5)

(Foley artist)

When asked how she perceives her process when performing Foley:

> They are my toys, and I am playing to make the right emotions.

## [**David E. Stone**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_6)

(supervising sound editor, dialogue editor, effects editor, animator, educator)

Everything that moves and causes friction has a different context.

## [**Michael Semanick**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_7)

(rerecording mixer, Foley and ADR mixer, music engineer)

You’ve got to pay attention to the artist and the picture.

You can make it your own and sometimes you can make it better. A good \[Foley\] artist can say, “OK, that’s good … now I’ll just sweeten it.”

When discussing people’s obsession with new bells and whistles in technology:

> I think they forget to actually listen.

## [**Richard Portman**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_8)

(rerecording mixer, engineer, educator)

I teach the aesthetics and the theory of sound. My classes are lecture classes and then they \[the students\] have to come in and sit down and put it all together.

When they \[the students\] come in they know nothing. That’s the great strength. They don’t come in with something on their minds and then you have to destroy that before you can teach them what to do.

First you’re a writer, then a director. It’s a talking book. That’s the key. If you can’t hear the sound, then that’s bad printing. It’s smudged.

## [**Miguel Barbosa**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_9)

(Foley artist, editor)

Sometimes I have to rush a lot.

When asked how he responds to hearing lots of different opinions about how Foley should be done:

> Having different views and approaches are helpful.

## [**Mark Pappas**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_10)

(Foley editor)

We have the ability to make things better now, and I think sometimes we do.

I think the most important thing is to be on top of everything with the technology because of how quickly it changes.

Try to search out the people who are the best in line with what it is you want to do.

## [**Misa Jakovljevic**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_11)

(Foley artist, editor)

When asked what he thinks about when performing Foley:

> Where is the soul in that sound?

## [**Jerry Trent**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_12)

(Foley artist, dancer)

Most of the real things don’t sound like the real things.

You have to know what goes on in making a movie, what goes on off camera and on.

## [**Tom Fleischman**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_13)

(rerecording mixer)

It’s all about the story.

## [**Alyson Dee Moore**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_14)

(Foley artist)

It’s kind of like how people eat a corn cob: some people eat straight across, in very neat rows. Some eat around and some eat all over. I’m more of an eating in clean rows type.

When commenting on some good advice she got (from your embarrassed author):

> You are the prop.

## [**Aleyna Seymen**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_15)

(Foley artist)

Everything is about feeling.

## [**David Jobe**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_16)

(Foley and ADR mixer)

There is no such thing as a perfect mic in Foley.

When asked what he pays attention to in recording Foley:

> The aesthetic choices, the quality of how they \[the Foley artists\] paint the sound, the perspective and richness of the sound.

## [**Cornelia Baumgartner**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_17)

(sound designer, Foley artist, composer)

In every object lives a sound.

## [**Andy Malcolm**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_18)

(Foley artist)

The ultimate Foley for a drama is recorded on location.

## [**Kevin O’Connell**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_19)

(rerecording mixer)

Mixers are artists. If a light bulb goes out, we call an engineer!

## [**Julien Naudin**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_20)

(Foley artist)

When asked to describe American Foley:

> \[Playfully laughs\] Whoosh, whoosh!

## [**Scott Hecker**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_21)

(supervising sound editor)

Get it in the ballpark.

When commenting on too many cues or choices in Foley:

> The last thing I want is what I call “clogging the arteries.” It’s just too much.

His advice to others wanting a career in sound:

> Watch as many movies and TV shows as you possibly can and listen to what you are about to endeavor. Go to school on other people’s professionalism.

## [**Greg Barbanell**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_22)

(Foley artist, editor)

Feeling good about a movie you’re working on is a plus. Keep it simple.

## [**Ele Matelan**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_23)

(Foley artist)

We’re all magpies.

## [**F. Hudson Miller**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_24)

(supervising sound editor)

The spice that’s all around it.

## [**Heikki Kossi**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_25)

(Foley artist, editor)

There can be rules, but every project is different, and you have to rethink everything.

## [**Rick Owens**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_26)

(Foley artist)

I try to hear the Foley in my head, and sometimes I vocalize it.

## [**Don Sylvester**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_27)

(supervising sound editor)

Give as much as the actors give on the set.

## [**Joan Rowe**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_28)

(Foley artist, actor)

I am not someone who listens to the Foley. I want it to support the drama of the piece.

## [**Karan Singh**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_29)

(Foley artist)

Whenever you’re performing, you have to be the character, and the soul of the actor should come at you.

## [**Richard James Hinton**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_30)

(Foley artist, editor)

It was the most frustrating job for the first three years. One day you wake up and you are more focused on the quality.

## [**Hilda Hodges**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_31)

(Foley artist)

After all these years, I still love it.

## [**Marco Ciorba**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_32)

(Foley artist)

The Foley can add some soul to the story.

## [**Elisha Birnbaum**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_33)

(production sound mixer, Foley artist, editor)

If you manage to do Foley to sound real, that is the best achievement you can have as a Foley artist.

## [**Charles Maynes**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_34)

(field recordist, editor)

Commenting about how studio postproduction supervisors see sound as a widget in the movie:

> Did you think about what the widget did?

## [**Marko Costanzo**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_35)

(Foley artist)

Use your ears.

When asked how he has decided what to use for a prop:

> It’s what works at the time.

## [**Mauro Eusepi**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_36)

(Foley artist)

Foley sound is to movies what air is to humans—no one thinks about it until it is missing.

## [**Malcolm Fife**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_37)

(Foley editor)

When commenting about how to satisfy problems with limitations, yet still be artistic:

> That is the new challenge of filmmaking. We’re all spread out now, and there is this idea that anything is possible.

## [**Douglas Murray**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_38)

(supervising sound editor, Foley editor, dialogue editor, effects editor)

It’s not about having lots of sound; it’s about having the right sound.

## [**Steve Lee**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_39)

(sound wrangler, owner of Hollywood Lost and Found)

I love Foley for its ability to create an indigenous performance.

## [**Goro Koyama**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_40)

(Foley artist)

Good Foley can tell a story as well as other sound elements. It is music without melody and dialogue without words.

## [**Greg Hedgepath**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_41)

(supervising sound editor)

Foley adds life to a scene.

It’s like making a painting.

## [**Chris Szott**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_42)

(Foley artist)

Sometimes when you get into the groove, you need to go back to the story.

## [**Leslie Bloome**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_43)

(Foley artist, advising Joanna Fang when she began Foley at Alchemy Post)

You’ve gotta do a lot of living.

## [**Peter Burgis**](#content.xhtml#bck_sec15_44)

(Foley artist)

If it sounds right and is telling the sound I want to make, I use it.

There are no rules.

# [**Glossary**](#content.xhtml#bck_glossary)

**A side** The part of a scene or angle prior to the edit.

**Across the curriculum** Educational term that refers to designing courses that interconnect various disciplines rather than studying with a specialized and isolated focus.

**Automated dialogue replacement** (ADR) The practice of recording a character’s dialogue in controlled studio, synchronized to picture. Similar process as used in recording Foley.

**B side** Refers to the part of a scene or angle after the edit.

**Background** Sound effects or characters that are not the primary focus in a scene. Incidental characters or props that contribute to the scene and environment.

**Baffle** Structure used to mute or deaden Foley effects performed on a Foley stage. Usually made from foam or a similar absorptive substance. Can be built-in or portable.

**Bright** Refers to timbre of the Foley or sound effect. Light and sharp in quality.

**Cheat** Refers to the Foley technique that masks an imperfect match in edit or performance, i.e., change rhythm of footsteps imperceptibly or disguise mismatched prop performance.

**Cop** gear Shortcut term for the combination of props used for the gun, club, leather belt, and handcuffs worn by police officers.

**Cue** Term used to identify the specific Foley effect to be performed, usually with a precise time code to begin and end performance.

**Cue sheets** Paper sheets with specific cues planned and defined for Foley artists and mixers to use on the Foley stage and later the dubbing stage.

**Cut-in** Term of art that refers to editing a sound effect into a scene.

**Cut sound effects** Term of art that refers to edited sound effects as opposed to performed Foley effects.

**Dance Foley** Foley footsteps that are specifically dance steps, often performed by Foley artists who are trained dancers.

**Dead room** Foley stage or recording room that is designed to add little or no life or bounce to sound; usually contains many soft walls and surfaces to absorb sound.

**Designer** Person who actually conceives and designs special sounds for a specific imagined effect, often utilizing synthesizers and plug-ins.

**Dirt pit** Foley pit filled with specific dirt for performing the footsteps of characters walking in dirt.

**Dub stage** Specially designed room with rerecording capabilities used for the final mixing of all sound elements used in film.

**Dummy loader** Recordist who loaded recording machines with magnetic film. The machine was called a dummy because it only held film for playback and did not record, but it was connected to recording equipment on a Foley or ADR stage.

**Effects kit** Specific sound effects auditioned and assigned for the sound editor to edit in to a specific scene or segment of film; usually presented by the supervising sound editor.

**Fine-cut** Precise editing of Foley or sound effects, usually the final step in editing a scene or segment.

**Flat** Term of art that refers to a recording technique that does not add reverb or perspective.

**Flop the track, flop a track** The technique of taking an edited piece of sound and turning it over so the sound will not engage with the sound head. Used to eliminate sound from a track. In modern computer editing, clearing out the sound is the technique.

**Foley dancer** Foley artist who does dance Foley.

**Foley speak** Slang language used by Foley artists, mixers, etc. using terms of art that are shortcuts to describe practices and techniques.

**Foley stage** Recording area where synchronized sound effects are performed.

**Foley walking** Technique of replacing the footsteps for characters in film on a Foley stage.

**Foleyed** Term of art that refers to performed sound effects by Foley artists in sync to picture, as opposed to edited sound effects.

**Foley’s room** Term given to the recording area used by Jack Foley to perform his synchronized sound effects.

**Full coat** Type of magnetic tape used for recording Foley, which had four tracks available for recording at one time.

**Grit cling** Dirt or other substance that sticks to shoes when used for a gritty surface.

**Grit factor** Aesthetic choice of how much of a substance is used to cause a walking surface to sound gritty, i.e., for a dirty city street.

**Gritty surface** Walking surface that has an added substance to make it sound dirty or exterior.

**Gross cutting** Preliminary editing that gives a general idea of sync and style, to be followed by more refined and specific editing.

**Gun life** The general light rattle of one or more guns to indicate their presence in the scene, for dramatic effect.

**Gush, gunk, gore** Refer to onomatopoeic slang used to describe sounds used for horror or suspense scenes.

**Hang it as a unit** To use Foley as recorded, with no editing. Common practice in television when time is limited. Comes from the prior practice of recording on magnetic tape, when Foley was recorded on full coat.

**Hard effects, hard FX** Edited in sound effects as opposed to performed Foley effects.

**High end** Bright or thin sound in recording or mixing; refers to higher pitched range of sounds.

**Hot** Term of art referring to recording or mixing sounds with a high level of volume. Often used to describe recording Foley effects that need to play in a loud scene.

**In the box** Term of art that refers to sound effects being recorded, edited, and mixed in one computer system or “box.”

**Kits of library effects** Prearranged combinations of sounds from the sound effects library auditioned and selected by the supervisor for editors to use to layer for sound event in film.

**Land the cue,** land a cue To get the Foley performance accurately and with the correct style or character. Usually used to describe exceptional performance on a first or second attempt or “take.”

**Laundry list** Term of art for a casual list of requested footstep and prop cues for the Foley artist that has no time code, details, or specifics.

**Leader** Film with no sound recorded used as a blank in picture or soundtrack, usually found at the beginning of a reel or as space holder for future sound effects.

**Looping** Term of art that refers to ADR in contemporary usage. Derives from the historical practice of recording an actor’s lines without synchronization, which could be played in a “loop” for others to listen and critique.

**Low end** Term of art that refers to sounds that are darker and deeper in timbre, i.e., “low-end rumble” used to effect mystery or fear.

**M&E** Music and effects. Refers to soundtrack that includes a complete mix of effects, music, and production tracks, with dialogue edited out to be used for foreign films where language must be replaced. Usually a more complete series of effects than a domestic release where the original language remains.

**Mag** Magnetic tape. Used for recording sound, music, dialogue, and Foley prior to digital recording.

**Manipulations** Another term for prop Foley.

**Marry, married effects and Foley** Term of art that refers to effects that rely on both edited effects and performed Foley effects for a complete sound event.

**Metal ring off** Term of art that refers to the resonance that hangs on in a recording after impact is recorded.

**Motion capture** Also known as performance capture, it is the practice of recording the movement of actors or objects for 3D animation for live-action and animated films, as well as games.

**Mouth sounds** Foley sounds performed using the mouth; usually supplements other Foley sounds. Often comical.

**Multiple tracks** Use of more than one recording track for various aspects of one sound event.

**Optical sound** Sound that was recorded on optical film, prior to magnetic tape. The sound track was on a stripe of film.

**Panning** The practice of mixing a Foley effect to have sound travel from one side to another, i.e., left to right as a person walks across the screen.

**Paper life** Term of art giving a sense of the presence of paper without specific movements delineated.

**Platform** Educational term that refers to a planned curriculum that builds skills and knowledge from the first course to final courses; each course relies on knowledge from the previous course.

**Postproduction** The process of filmmaking beginning with picture editing and ending with the final mix or dub.

**Predub** The process of mixing sound effects, Foley, ADR, and dialogue in their own groups before the final dub or mix where all sounds are blended together.

**Predubs** The collected sound materials separated by “food groups” into dialogue, principal ADR, group ADR, sound effects, background effects, Foley footsteps, and Foley props.

**Pro Tools** Digital recording, editing, and mixing system that can be used on a Macintosh or PC computer. Industry standard.

**Production track** The sound track recorded during production, usually focused on dialogue in the scene. Sounds recorded in production are included and utilized as a blueprint for sound effects and Foley.

**Pull, pulled** Term of art that refers to auditioning and compiling sound effects from a library for use in a soundtrack.

**Punch in** Technique used in Foley mixing where the mixer picks a precise spot to begin recording in the middle of a prerecorded cue and enters the cue at that point.

**Quarter-inch** tape Recording tape used in reel-to-reel recording.

**Read** Term of art that refers to how something is interpreted by the audience.

**Recorded wild** Custom Foley sound effects that are recorded on a Foley stage without sync for a specific purpose; usually recorded with variations.

**Recordist** Sound professional who assists the mixer. Historically, duties included preparing the magnetic tape and ensuring quality in the recording. Contemporary recordists most often assist with setting up recording sessions for mixers, troubleshooting Pro Tools, and keeping track of Foley cues.

**Ring off** Residual sound that is recorded after the impact of a prop is completed.

**Scuffs** Footstep cues that are not full footsteps, but have a characteristic quality of muted impact.

**Shoe element** Quality of shoe(s) used; character of shoe used.

**Single stripe** Magnetic film that has one track available to record sound.

**Snow design** Specific combination of substances used to create specific snow sounds, depending on the quality of weather depicted.

**Soundman** Historic term of art relating to the profession of making or recording sounds for film, radio, and television.

**Stems** The specific groups of dubbed sound material used in soundtracks, i.e., dialogue stems, sound effects stems, music stems.

**Streamers** Lines that move across the film screen or monitor that alert mixers and Foley artists to the beginning of a cue to be performed.

**Sweet** spot The prime part of a sound that defines the character of the sound; comes from sports terminology.

**Sweeten** To enhance a sound that already exists; to add a quality not already existing.

**Synthesist** A sound professional who creates special sounds and musical effects using synthesized keyboards and instruments. The synthesist is often mistakenly referred to as a sound designer.

**Temp** dubs A temporary mix of sounds to present in a film or film clip, not intended to be the complete soundtrack. Usually used for audience testing.

**Topping production** Adding Foley or sound effects with sound already in production; requires precise sync and is played in concert with the production track.

**Trim bin** Metal bin used in editing that allows editors to collect unnecessary film or hang bits of film to be replaced in the soundtrack.

**Turn over, turnover** The convention of giving the most recent version of film to the next step in process; usually used in reference to picture editing giving the final edited version of film to sound editorial for effects, Foley, ADR, and music.

**Walk the Foley** Slang term of art referring to performing the Foley effects for the film. Comes from the practice of footsteps being the critical part of Foley that needed to be replaced.

**Walk, walking** Perform Foley footsteps.

**Walker** Foley artist. Slang.

**Watching directors** Term coined by Robert L. Mott, referring to directors who watch what is used for a sound rather than listening to the sound itself.

# [**References**](#content.xhtml#bck_r001001)

# [About the Author and the Illustrator](#content.xhtml#bck_sec0004)

![The author at the Denver International Film Festival in 1989. Photo courtesy of Larry Laszlo & Associates, CoMedia.](Images/fig0026.jpg)

*The author at the Denver International Film Festival in 1989. Photo courtesy of Larry Laszlo & Associates, CoMedia.*

##  **ABOUT THE AUTHOR**

Vanessa Theme Ament is a former Foley artist and sound editor who has performed Foley in films and television since 1980. She worked on many notable films including *Predator*, *Die Hard*, *Noises Off*, *A Goofy Movie*, *Dolores Claiborne*, *Malice*, *RoboCop2*, and *Platoon*, which won the Academy Award for Best Sound in 1986. In 1989 she demonstrated Foley on *Late Night with David Letterman*. She completed her PhD in Moving Image Studies at Georgia State University and has recently written chapters on film sound in several edited books. Her book *Divergent Tracks: How Three Film Communities Revolutionized Digital Film Sound* was released in 2021. Vanessa is the former Edwin F. and Virginia B. Ball Endowed Chair in Telecommunications at Ball State University, Muncie, Indiana.

## **ABOUT THE ILLUSTRATOR**

David Stone is a sound editor and a veteran of over 100 feature films, including *Gremlins*, *Top Gun*, *Die Hard*, and *Ocean’s Eleven*. He was the supervising sound editor for *Predator*, *Edward Scissorhands*, *Beauty and the Beast*, and *Batman Returns*, among others. He is the winner of five Golden Reel Awards, and he won the 1993 Academy Award for Sound Effects Editing for his supervising work on *Bram Stoker’s Dracula*. Dave worked as a commercial artist, hoping for a career as an animator, before his addiction to sound editing began. He has illustrated the two previous editions of *The Foley Grail* as well as this third edition, and along with Vanessa Theme Ament, he wrote *Hollywood Sound Design and Moviesound Newsletter: A Case Study of the End of the Analog Age*.
